Spectral Theory of Dirac Operators
Tigran Harutyunyan

Abstract




Annotation

The main issues of the spectral theory of Dirac operators are presented, namely: transformation operators, asymptotics of eigenvalues and eigenfunctions, description of symmetric and self-adjoint operators in Hilbert space, expansion in eigenfunctions, uniqueness theorems in inverse problems, constructive solution of inverse problems, description of isospectral operators, and some other questions.
This book is aimed at specialists in spectral theory and graduate students of mathematics at universities.

Introduction

This book is based on the lectures taught by T.N. Harutyunyan in recent years at the faculty of Mathematics and Mechanics of Yerevan State University.
Our aim is the representation of the mathematical aspects of the spectral theory of the so-called canonical Dirac operator.

Consider the well-known Pauli matrices

\sigma_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&i\\
-i&0\\
\end{array}\right),\quad\sigma_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\\
\end{array}\right),\quad\sigma_{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\\
\end{array}\right),

(0.0.1)

which have the properties

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\sigma_{k}^{*}=\sigma_{k},&\text{(self-adjointness)},&k=1,2,3,\\
\sigma_{k}^{2}=E_{2},&(E_{2}\text{ is an identity matrix)},&k=1,2,3,\\
\sigma_{k}\sigma_{j}=-\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k},&\text{(anti-commutativity)},&k,j=1,2,3,~k\neq j.\end{array}

The canonical Dirac system of ordinary differential equations we call the system

\left\{\sigma_{1}\dfrac{1}{i}\dfrac{d}{dx}+\sigma_{2}p(x)+\sigma_{3}q(x)\right\}y\equiv\left\{B\dfrac{d}{dx}+\Omega(x)\right\}y=\lambda y,\qquad y=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{array}\right),

(0.0.2)

where
p(\cdot)
and
q(\cdot)
are given scalar functions,
\lambda
is a spectral parameter (
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
), and
y
is an unknown function.
For the first time, the name ”canonical form” was given by Gasymov and Levitan in the article
[
GL66
]
, published in 1966.

V.A. Marchenko (see
[
Mar77
, p.30]
) suggested a more general definition.
He called an operator equation
By^{\prime}+\Omega(x)y=\lambda y
the Dirac system, if
B^{2}=-E
and
B\Omega(x)+\Omega(x)B=0
, i.e., in this definition there is no restriction on the dimensions of matrices
B
and
\Omega(x)
, moreover, they can be any operators in the corresponding Hilbert space.
If we take
\dfrac{1}{i}\cdot\sigma_{1}
as matrix
B
(and this appears in the works of
[
Tit61
,
GL66
,
GK67
,
Mar72
,
Mar77
]
), then any
2\times 2
dimensional matrix-function, which is anti-commutative with
B
, has the form
\Omega(x)=\sigma_{2}\cdot p(x)+\sigma_{3}\cdot q(x)
.
For this reason, we call the canonical Dirac system the equation (
0.0.2
).

Note that we can write (
0.0.2
) as a linear normal system of differential equations of the form
y^{\prime}=A(x,\lambda)y
, where the trace of the matrix
A(x,\lambda)=-\lambda B+B\Omega(x)
is equal to zero, which ensures the Wronskian of any two solutions of equation (
0.0.2
) to be constant (see, e.g.,
[
CL55
]
).
This circumstance gives a relation between the canonical Dirac system and the Sturm-Liouville equation
-y^{\prime\prime}+q(x)y=\lambda y
, whose Wronskian of any two solutions is also a constant due to the absence of the first derivative.

Dirac’s equation appeared in 1929, when P.A.M. Dirac introduced the equation (later named after him), modeling the evolution of spin
-\frac{1}{2}
particles in relativistic quantum mechanics.
In the original equation, the unknown is a four-component vector-function
\varphi=\left(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3},\varphi_{4}\right)^{T}
(
T
is the sign of transponation), and the equation has the form

\left(\alpha_{1}p_{1}+\alpha_{2}p_{2}+\alpha_{3}p_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\varphi+r(x)\varphi=\lambda\varphi,

(0.0.3)

where
p_{k}=\frac{1}{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}
,
r(x)
is a scalar potential of the external field, and
\alpha_{k}
are fourth-order square Hermitian matrices, satisfying the conditions (the sign
*
is for conjugate)

\begin{cases}\alpha_{k}^{*}=\alpha_{k},&\text{(self-adjointness, Hermitianness)},\\
\alpha_{k}^{2}=E_{4},&(E_{4}\text{ is an identity matrix)},\\
\alpha_{k}\alpha_{j}=-\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k},&\text{(anti-commutativity)}~k\neq j.\\
\end{cases}

(0.0.4)

In paper
[
Sto70
]
it is proved, that the set
\gamma_{N}
of square numerical matrices of an order
N
, satisfying conditions (
0.0.4
), consists of
2k+1
matrices, where
N=2^{k}r
,
r=1(mod2)
, i.e.
r
is an odd number (obviously, any natural number
N
can be represented in this way).
Thus, there are only 3 Pauli matrices (see (
0.0.1
)) and only 5 Dirac matrices
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}
.
For example, they can be represented by second-order identity
E_{2}
and Pauli
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}
matrices in the following way

\alpha_{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}E_{2}&0\\
0&-E_{2}\\
\end{array}\right),\quad\alpha_{k}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\sigma_{k}\\
-\sigma_{k}&0\\
\end{array}\right),~k=1,2,3,\quad\alpha_{4}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&E_{2}\\
E_{2}&0\\
\end{array}\right).

In some cases (see, e.g.,
[
Sar05
]
), the system of partial differential equations (
0.0.3
) can be reduced to the system of ordinary differential equations (
0.0.2
).

We will not refer to the physical aspects of the models described by the Dirac equation (we can only recommend the book
[
Tha92
]
).
We will study the direct and inverse problems of the spectral theory of the canonical Dirac system (
0.0.2
).

Under the norm
|A|
of the two-dimensional matrix
A
, we understand the quantity
|A|=\sqrt{\lambda_{2}(A^{*}A)}
, where
\lambda_{2}(A^{*}A)
is the largest eigenvalue of a positive definite matrix
A^{*}A
.
It is easy to check that if
A=a\sigma_{2}+b\sigma_{3}
or
A=aE+bB
(where
a
and
b
are arbitrary complex numbers), then

|A|\leq|a|+|b|.

(0.0.5)

There are other norms for two-dimensional matrix
A=(a_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{2}
, for example
|A|=\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}|a_{ij}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}
, or
|A|=\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}|a_{ij}|
. We must note that all these norms are equivalent.

In what follows, by
c(x)
we denote

c(x)=\int_{0}^{x}|p(s)|ds+\int_{0}^{x}|q(s)|ds.

(0.0.6)

In Chapter
1
we consider the Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}\ell y\equiv\left\{\sigma_{1}\dfrac{1}{i}\dfrac{d}{dx}+\sigma_{2}p(x)+\sigma_{3}q(x)\right\}y=\lambda y\\
y(0)=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}~\sin\alpha\\
-\cos\alpha\end{array}\right),\qquad\alpha\in\mathbb{C},\end{cases}

under conditions
p,q\in L_{loc}^{1}[0,\infty)
(
p
and
q
are complex-valued, summable on arbitrary finite interval functions).
In this chapter, we prove the existence and uniqueness of solution
y=\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
of this Cauchy problem and its analytical dependence on
\lambda
and
\alpha
.

Chapter
2
is devoted to the proof of the existence and some properties of the so-called transformation operators.
In particular, we prove the existence of a kernel
K_{0}(x,t,\alpha)
(matrix-function), such that

\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin(\lambda x+\alpha)\\
-\cos(\lambda x+\alpha)\end{array}\right)+\int^{x}_{0}K_{0}(x,t,\alpha)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin(\lambda t+\alpha)\\
-\cos(\lambda t+\alpha)\end{array}\right)\,dt\;.

In Chapter
3
we consider the boundary value problem
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)

\begin{cases}\ell y=\lambda y,\\
y_{1}(0)\cos\alpha+y_{2}(0)\sin\alpha=0,\\
y_{1}(\pi)\cos\beta+y_{2}(\pi)\sin\beta=0.\end{cases}

with summable coefficients
p
and
q
(
p,q\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}[0,\pi]

1
1

1

p\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}[0,\pi]
means, that
p
is complex-valued and
\int_{0}^{\pi}|p|dx<\infty
, and
p\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
means, that
p
is real-valued.

) and complex parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
in boundary conditions.
We prove that the problem
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
has a countable set of eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}=\lambda_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, (with corresponding eigenfunctions
\varphi_{n}(x)=\varphi(x,\lambda_{n},\alpha)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
), which have the asymptotics

\lambda_{n}=n+\dfrac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}+r_{n},

where
r_{n}=r_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)=o(1)
(when
n\to\pm\infty
) uniformly by
p
and
q
from bounded subsets of
L^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}[0,\pi]
and uniformly by all complex
\alpha
and
\beta
with bounded imaginary parts.
To study in more detail the dependence of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
on all arguments, we introduce its gradient by the formula

\grad\,\lambda_{n}=\left(\frac{\partial\lambda_{n}}{\partial\alpha},\,\frac{\partial\lambda_{n}}{\partial\beta},\,\frac{\partial\lambda_{n}}{\partial p\left(x\right)},\,\frac{\partial\lambda_{n}}{\partial q\left(x\right)}\right).

By
a_{n}=a_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
we denote the square of the
L^{2}
-norm of eigenfunction
\varphi_{n}
:

a_{n}=\int^{\pi}_{0}|\varphi_{n}(x)|^{2}dx.

In the same Chapter
3
we obtain the asymptotics

a_{n}=\pi+\kappa_{n},\qquad\kappa_{n}\to 0,\quad\mbox{when}\ n\to\pm\infty,

and also the representation of
a_{n}
by two spectra
\{\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon,\beta)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, where
\epsilon\in\left(\alpha,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)
.

Chapter
4
is devoted to the eigenfunction expansion theorems.

It is known that the problem
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
by a unitary transformation reduces to a problem of the form
L(\tilde{p},\tilde{q},\tilde{\alpha},0)
.
That is why, when in Chapter
5
, we introduce the concept of an eigenvalue function (EVF) of a family of Dirac operators, we are talking about a family
\left\{L(p,q,\alpha,0),\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right]\right\}
, where
p,q,\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
.

In Chapter
6
, we investigate inverse problems.
Here we prove four uniqueness theorems in inverse problems.

In Chapter
7
, we turn to the description of isospectral operators, i.e. knowing that, in general, one spectrum does not uniquely determine a potential matrix
\Omega(x)=p(x)\sigma_{2}+q(x)\sigma_{3}
, we pose the following questions:

”How many different matrix-function can generate the same spectrum?”

Moreover, the second, more precise question:

”Is it possible to describe the set of all potential matrix-functions that generate the same spectrum?”

In Theorem
7.2
, we give the description of all canonical isospectral potentials.

In the theory of inverse problems for Sturm-Liouville boundary-value problem

\begin{cases}-y^{\prime\prime}+q(x)y=\lambda y,\\
y_{1}(0)\cos\alpha+y_{2}(0)\sin\alpha=0,\\
y_{1}(\pi)\cos\beta+y_{2}(\pi)\sin\beta=0,\end{cases}

the eigenvalues of which we denote by
\lambda_{n}(q,\alpha,\beta),\ n=0,1,2,\ldots
, V.A. Ambarzumyan’s theorem (see
[
Amb29
]
) is well-known:

Theorem
.

If
\lambda_{n}(q,\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2})=n^{2},\ n=0,1,2,\ldots
, then
q(x)\equiv 0
.

It should be noted, that this theorem is laid in the foundations of the study of inverse problems.

In Chapter
8
, we answer the question:

”Is there an analog of Ambarzumyan’s theorem in the case of a boundary-value problem for the canonical Dirac system?”

In the general case, the answer to this question is negative.
We even give an example of a two-parameter family of canonical potentials that generate the same spectrum as a zero potential problem.

Besides this, in the same chapter, we describe cases when the inverse problem for the canonical Dirac system can be solved with a smaller set of spectral data, than the set required in the general case.
Here we prove three theorems, the essence of which we tried to express in the title of the chapter: ”Cases of symmetry”.

In Chapter
9
, we solve the inverse problem in terms of eigenvalue functions (EVF).
More precisely, we prove that the properties 1)-4) described in Theorem
5.1
are not only necessary but also sufficient for a function to be an EVF of a certain family of operators
\left\{L(p,q,\alpha,0),\ \alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right]\right\}
, with
p,q\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0.\pi]
.

In Chapter
10
, we consider the canonical Dirac system on the half-axis
(0,\infty)
and whole-axis
(-\infty,\infty)
.

Section
10.1
is devoted to obtaining an asymptotic formula for the so-called Weyl-Titchmarsh function
m(\lambda)
for
\lambda=\nu+i\mu
, when
\mu\to\pm\infty
.

In Section
10.2
we assume that coefficients
p
and
q
satisfy conditions that ensure the pure discreteness of the spectrum of the corresponding
selfadjoint operator on the half-axis (semi-axis).
Under these conditions, we obtain a representation of norming constants in terms of two spectra.
This problem is similar to the same problem for the regular operator, which is solved in Chapter
3
.
However, the technical problems are related to the fact that, unlike in the regular case, here we do not have an asymptotic formula for the eigenvalues, which were essential in the study of questions of convergence of infinite products (in formulas expressing norming constants in terms of two spectra).
Here we use asymptotics of the Weyl-Titchmarsh function.

Section
10.3
is devoted to obtaining explicit formulas for recalculating the coefficients of the Dirac operator when a finite number of eigenvalues and (or) norming constants are changed.
It is impossible to add or subtract eigenvalues for a regular operator (due to the mandatory asymptotics), but in the singular case, it is allowed.

In Section
10.4
, we introduce the concept of eigenvalues function (EVE) of the family of singular Dirac operators on the semi-axis with purely discrete spectra and study the properties of this function.

In Section
10.5
, we consider the Dirac operator with linear potentials (
q(x)=x,p(x)\equiv 0
) on the whole axis and half-axis, the eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of which we compute explicitly.
Considering this operator on the half-axis as a model operator, we change its discrete spectrum in an (almost) arbitrary way.
As a result, we get an operator on the half-axis with in advance given spectrum.

At the end of each chapter, we give notes and references to the literature.
We have a three-digit numbering of formulas, i.e., number (3.2.8) means the eight formula in Section 2 of Chapter 3.

Yerevan, September 25, 2022,  T.N. Harutyunyan

Yu.A. Ashrafyan

CHAPTER
1
The Cauchy problem for the Dirac system

The system of ordinary differential equations

\ell y\equiv\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right)\frac{d}{dx}+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}p(x)&q(x)\\
q(x)&-p(x)\end{array}\right)\right\}y=\lambda y,\quad y=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{array}\right)\;,

(1.0.1)

where
p
and
q
are some given scalar functions, and
\lambda
is a complex parameter known as a canonical Dirac equation.

The Cauchy problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\ell y=\lambda y\\
y(0)=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}~\sin\alpha\\
-\cos\alpha\end{array}\right),\end{array}\right.

(1.0.2)

was considered by Titchmarsh in
[
Tit44
]
.
Under conditions that
p
and
q
are continuous functions and
\alpha
is a real number, he proved the existence and uniqueness of the solution to the problem (
1.0.2
) and, moreover, that this solution is an entire function of the parameter
\lambda
.

If the coefficients
p
and
q
of the system (
1.0.1
) are not continuous, but, for example, are functions from
L^{1}(a,b)
, then the classical definition of the solution to the system (
1.0.1
) becomes meaningless, since after the substitution, the left-hand side of (
1.0.1
) is defined only almost everywhere, however the right-hand side is defined everywhere, and so we can not speak about identity.
For this reason, we give a more general definition of a solution of system (
1.0.1
) (see, e.g.
[
Nai69
]
, p. 183): a function
y=y(x)
is called the solution to (
1.0.1
), defined on the interval
(a,b)
, if it is absolutely continuous on each closed subinterval
[a_{1},b_{1}]\subset(a,b)
and satisfies to (
1.0.1
) almost everywhere on
(a,b)
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
p,q\in L^{1}_{loc}(0,\infty)
, i.e.
p
and
q
be measurable, complex-valued functions, absolutely summable on each finite interval
(0,a)
,
a>0

\int\limits^{a}_{0}\left|p(s)\right|\,ds,\qquad\int\limits^{a}_{0}\left|q(s)\right|\,ds<\infty\,.

(1.0.3)

Then Cauchy problem (
1.0.2
) has a unique solution
y=\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
.
The components
\varphi_{1}(x,\lambda,\alpha)
and
\varphi_{2}(x,\lambda,\alpha)
of this solution (for every fixed
x\in[0,a]
) are entire functions of parameters
\lambda
and
\alpha
.

To prove this theorem, we use three lemmas.

Lemma 1.1
.

The Cauchy problem (
1.0.2
) is equivalent to the integral equation

\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}~\sin\alpha\\
-\cos\alpha\end{array}\right)+\int\limits^{x}_{0}A(s,\lambda)\varphi(s,\lambda,\alpha)\,ds\,,

(1.0.4)

where

A(x,\lambda)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}q(x)&-p(x)-\lambda\\
\lambda-p(x)&-q(x)\end{array}\right).

(1.0.5)

Proof.

Let
y
be the solution to (
1.0.2
).
It means that
y
is an absolutely continuous function on
(0,\pi)
(therefore, its derivative,
y^{\prime}
, is a function from
L^{1}(0,\pi)
) and equality

By^{\prime}(x)=\lambda y(x)-\Omega(x)y(x)=\left\{\lambda E-\Omega(x)\right\}y(x),

(1.0.6)

holds almost everywhere on
(0,\pi)
.
Since both sides are summable functions, we can integrate the equality (
1.0.6
), but before integration, we multiply both sides from the left by
-B
:

-B^{2}y^{\prime}(x)=\left\{B\Omega(x)-\lambda B\right\}y(x).

Because
B^{2}=-E
and
B\Omega(x)-\lambda B=A(x,\lambda)
, we get that
y
is the solution of the normal system ordinary, linear differential equations:

y^{\prime}(x)=A(x,\lambda)y(x),

(this equality holds almost everywhere on
(0,\pi)
).
If in this equality, write
s
instead of
x
and integrate with respect to
s
from
0
to
x
, we will obtain

y(x)-y(0)=\int_{0}^{x}A(s,\lambda)y(s)ds.

Since both sides of the last equality are absolutely continuous functions, then the equality holds not only almost everywhere, but everywhere, i.e., it is an identity.
Thus, if
y=\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
is the solution to the Cauchy problem (
1.0.2
), then
\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
is the solution to the integral equation (
1.0.4
).

Now, let
\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
be a solution to the integral equation (
1.0.4
).
Since the right-side of (
1.0.4
) is an upper limit function, it has a derivative equal to the integrand function at the point
x
, and therefore, the left-hand side, i.e.
\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
, also has a derivative, and

\varphi^{\prime}(x,\lambda,\alpha)=A(x,\lambda)\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)

almost everywhere on
(0,\pi)
.
∎

Thus, instead of the Cauchy problem (
1.0.2
), we will solve the integral equation (
1.0.4
), which can be written in the form of two scalar integral equations:

\varphi_{1}(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\sin\alpha+\int\limits^{x}_{0}\left[q(s)\varphi_{1}(s,\lambda,\alpha)-(p(s)+\lambda)\varphi_{2}(s,\lambda,\alpha)\right]\,ds,

\varphi_{2}(x,\lambda,\alpha)=-\cos\alpha+\int\limits^{x}_{0}\left[(\lambda-p(s))\varphi_{1}(s,\lambda,\alpha)-q(s)\varphi_{2}(s,\lambda,\alpha)\right]\,ds.

We will solve the integral equation (
1.0.4
) by the method of successive approximations.
To this end, we construct the sequence of vector-functions:

\varphi^{(0)}(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}~\sin\alpha\\
-\cos\alpha\end{array}\right),

(1.0.7)

and

\varphi^{(n)}(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\varphi^{(0)}(x,\lambda,\alpha)+\int\limits^{x}_{0}A(s,\lambda)\varphi^{(n-1)}(s,\lambda,\alpha)\,ds,\quad n\geq 1.

(1.0.8)

Also, we set

u^{(n)}(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\varphi^{(n)}(x,\lambda,\alpha)-\varphi^{(n-1)}(s,\lambda,\alpha),\quad n\geq 1.

(1.0.9)

Then

u^{(n)}(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\int\limits^{x}_{0}A\left(s,\lambda\right)u^{(n-1)}(s,\lambda,\alpha)ds,

(1.0.10)

which, in its turn, can be rewritten as

u_{1}^{(n)}(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\int\limits^{x}_{0}\left\{q(s)u_{1}^{(n-1)}(s,\lambda,\alpha)-p(s)u^{(n-1)}_{2}(s,\lambda,\alpha)-\lambda u^{(n-1)}_{2}(s,\lambda,\alpha)\right\}\,ds,

(1.0.11)

u_{2}^{(n)}(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\int\limits^{x}_{0}\left\{\lambda u_{1}^{(n-1)}(s,\lambda,\alpha)-p(s)u^{(n-1)}_{1}(s,\lambda,\alpha)-q(s)u^{(n-1)}_{2}(s,\lambda,\alpha)\right\}\,ds.

(1.0.12)

We also introduce functions:

\displaystyle q_{0}(x)=\max\limits_{0\leq\xi\leq x}\left|\int\limits^{\xi}_{0}q(s)\,ds\right|,\quad\displaystyle q_{1}(x)=\int\limits^{x}_{0}\left|q(s)\right|\,ds,

(1.0.13)

and

\displaystyle p_{0}(x)=\max\limits_{0\leq\xi\leq x}\left|\int\limits^{\xi}_{0}p(s)\,ds\right|,\quad\displaystyle p_{1}(x)=\int\limits^{x}_{0}\left|p(s)\right|\,ds.

(1.0.14)

It is easy to see, that all functions
q_{0}
,
q_{1}
,
p_{0}
,
p_{1}
are monotone increasing (non-decreasing) and non-negative.

Lemma 1.2
.

For both components
u_{1}^{(n)}
and
u_{2}^{(n)}
of the vector-functions
u^{(n)}
, the following estimates hold:

\left|u^{(n)}_{1,2}(x,\lambda,\alpha)\right|\leq\left[q_{0}(x)+p_{0}(x)+|\lambda|x\right]\cdot\frac{\left[q_{1}(x)+p_{1}(x)+|\lambda|x\right]^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\cdot e^{|{\rm Im}\,\alpha|}\,.

(1.0.15)

Proof.

From definitions (
1.0.7
), (
1.0.8
), (
1.0.9
) and (
1.0.10
) for
u^{(1)}(x,\lambda,\alpha)
we get:

u^{(1)}_{1}(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\int\limits^{x}_{0}q(s)\,ds\cdot\sin\alpha+\int\limits^{x}_{0}p(s)\,ds\cdot\cos\alpha+\lambda x\cos\alpha,

u^{(1)}_{2}(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\int\limits^{x}_{0}q(s)\,ds\cdot\cos\alpha+\int\limits^{x}_{0}p(s)\,ds\cdot\sin\alpha+\lambda x\sin\alpha.

Taking into account, that
\left|\sin\alpha\right|\leq e^{|{\rm Im}\,\alpha|}
and
\left|\cos\alpha\right|\leq e^{|{\rm Im}\,\alpha|}
, from the last two equalities and definitions (
1.0.13
), (
1.0.14
) we obtain the estimates

\left|u^{(1)}_{1}(x,\lambda,\alpha)\right|\leq\left[q_{0}(x)+p_{0}(x)+|\lambda|x\right]e^{|{\rm Im}\,\alpha|}\,,

\left|u^{(1)}_{2}(x,\lambda,\alpha)\right|\leq\left[q_{0}(x)+p_{0}(x)+|\lambda|x\right]e^{|{\rm Im}\,\alpha|}\,,

which are consistent with (
1.0.15
), for
n=1
.
Now, by induction, assuming that estimates (
1.0.15
) are valid for
u_{1,2}^{(n)}
, we prove that they are valid for
u_{1,2}^{(n+1)}
.
According to (
1.0.11
) and (
1.0.15
), we have

\displaystyle\left|u^{(n+1)}_{1}(x,\lambda,\alpha)\right|\leq\int\limits^{x}_{0}\left\{\left|q(s)\right|\,\cdot\,\left|u_{1}^{(n)}(s,\lambda,\alpha)\right|\,\cdot\,\left|u^{(n)}_{2}(s,\lambda,\alpha)\right|+|\lambda|\,\cdot\left|u^{(n)}_{2}(s,\lambda,\alpha)\right|\right\}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq e^{|{\rm Im}\,\alpha|}\int_{0}^{x}\left\{\left|q(s)\right|+\left|p(s)\right|+|\lambda|\right\}\cdot\left\{q_{0}(s)+p_{0}(s)+|\lambda|\cdot s\right\}\cdot\frac{(q_{1}(s)+p_{1}(s)+|\lambda|s)^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq e^{|{\rm Im}\,\alpha|}\left\{q_{0}(x)+p_{0}(x)+|\lambda|x\right\}\int\limits^{x}_{0}\left\{\frac{d}{ds}(q_{1}(s)+p_{1}(s)+|\lambda|\cdot s)\right\}\frac{(q_{1}(s)+p_{1}(s)+|\lambda|\cdot s)^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\,ds

\displaystyle=e^{|{\rm Im}\,\alpha|}\left\{q_{0}(x)+p_{0}(x)+|\lambda|x\right\}\frac{1}{(n-1)!n}\int\limits^{x}_{0}\frac{d}{ds}\left\{q_{1}(s)+p_{1}(s)+|\lambda|\cdot s\right\}^{n}ds

\displaystyle=e^{|{\rm Im}\,\alpha|}\left\{q_{0}(x)+p_{0}(x)+|\lambda|x\right\}\cdot\frac{(q_{1}(x)+p_{1}(x)+|\lambda|\cdot x)^{n}}{n!}\;.

Here we took into account, that
p_{0}
and
q_{0}
are monotone increasing, non-negative functions, the facts that
\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}q_{1}(s)=\left|q(s)\right|
,
\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}p_{1}(s)=\left|p(s)\right|
, and also that
p_{1}(0)=q_{1}(0)=0
.
It is easy to see that the estimate for the second component
u^{(n+1)}_{2}
is being obtained from (
1.0.12
), exactly in the same way.
∎

Lemma 1.3
.

The sequences
\left\{\varphi^{(n)}_{1}\right\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
and
\left\{\varphi^{(n)}_{2}\right\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
(see (
1.0.7
), (
1.0.8
)) converge uniformly on the set
M=\left\{(x,\lambda,\alpha):\;x\in[0,a],|\lambda|\leq w,\;|\alpha|\leq v\right\},
where
a
,
w
,
v
are arbitrary positive numbers.

Proof.

In order the sequences
\left\{\varphi^{(n)}_{1}\right\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
and
\left\{\varphi^{(n)}_{2}\right\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
to converge uniformly on the set
M
, it is sufficient (see, e.g.,
[
Pon65
]
, pg. 155), that the inequalities

\displaystyle\left\|\varphi^{(n)}_{j}-\varphi_{j}^{(n-1)}\right\|=\left\|u^{(n)}_{j}\right\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}\max\limits_{(x,\lambda,\alpha)\in M}\left|u_{j}^{(n)}(x,\lambda,\alpha)\right|\leq a_{n},\quad j=1,2,

hold, where the numbers
a_{n}
form a convergent series.
Using estimates (
1.0.15
) and properties of the functions
p_{0}
,
q_{0}
,
p_{1}
,
q_{1}
, we get

\displaystyle\left|u_{j}^{(n)}(x,\lambda,\alpha)\right|\leq e^{\left|{\rm Im}\,\alpha\right|}\cdot\left[q_{0}(x)+p_{0}(x)+|\lambda|x\right]\frac{\left[q_{1}(x)+p_{1}(x)+|\lambda|x\right]^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}

\displaystyle\leq e^{v}\cdot\left[q_{0}(a)+p_{0}(a)+wa\right]\cdot\frac{\left[q_{1}(a)+p_{1}(a)+wa\right]^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}=C_{0}\frac{C_{1}^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}=a_{n}\,,

where
C_{0}=e^{v}\cdot\left[q_{0}(a)+p_{0}(a)+wa\right]
,
C_{1}=q_{1}(a)+p_{1}(a)+wa
are finite numbers, according to conditions (
1.0.3
) of Theorem
1.1
.
Since the series
\displaystyle\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=1}a_{n}=C_{0}e^{C_{1}}
converges, the proof is complete.
∎

From the definition (
1.0.8
) of successive approximations
\varphi^{(n)}
, it follows that components
\varphi_{1}^{(n)}
and
\varphi_{2}^{(n)}
are absolutely continuous functions with respect to
x
.
From their uniform convergence, it follows that the limit function of this sequence exists, and it is a continuous function.
Hence in (
1.0.8
), we can pass to the limit as
n\to\infty
.
It follows that the limit vector-function
\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
is a solution of the integral equation (
1.0.4
) and, therefore, the Cauchy problem (
1.0.2
).
Thus, the existence of a solution is proved.

Again from the construction (
1.0.8
) of successive approximations
\varphi^{(n)}
, it follows that the components
\varphi_{1,2}^{(n)}
are entire functions of parameters
\lambda
and
\alpha
(for every fixed
x
).
In order to prove that the limit function is also entire of parameters
\lambda
and
\alpha
, we recall the well-known theorem of Weierstrass (see, e.g.,
[
Bib81
]
, p. 166).

Theorem 1.2

(Weierstrass)
.

If a sequence of functions
\left\{f^{(n)}\right\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
, analytic on the domain
U\subseteq C^{m}
, converges uniformly on every compact subset of
U
, then the limit function is analytic on
U
.

Taking
\varphi^{(n)}(x_{0},\lambda,\alpha)=f^{(n)}(\lambda,\alpha)
,
U=\mathbb{C}^{2}
, from the Weierstrass theorem and Lemma
1.3
(since, for fixed
x
, the set
M
covers any compact subset in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
), we get that the solution
\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
to the Cauchy problem is an entire function of parameters
\lambda
and
\alpha
, for any fixed
x
.

To complete the proof of the Theorem
1.1
, it remains to prove the uniqueness of the solution
\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
.
Assume, that there is another solution
\Phi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
, and denote their difference by
u(x)=\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)-\Phi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
.
It is enough to show that the integral equation

u(x)=\int\limits^{x}_{0}A(s,\lambda)u(s)\,ds,

(1.0.16)

has only a trivial solution.
Because
u(x)
is a continuous function, then the quantity
K(x)=\max\limits_{0\leq s\leq x}\left\{\left|u_{1}(s)\right|,\left|u_{2}(s)\right|\right\}
is finite for any finite
x
.
Moreover, it is obvious that
K(x)
is a non-decreasing function.
Then, from (
1.0.16
), for the first component
u_{1}(x)
, we have an estimate

\displaystyle\left|u_{1}(x)\right|=\left|\int\limits^{x}_{0}\left\{q(s)u_{1}(s)-p(s)u_{2}(s)-\lambda u_{2}(s)\right\}\,ds\right|

\displaystyle\leq K(x)\int\limits^{x}_{0}\left\{\left|q(s)\right|+\left|p(s)\right|+|\lambda|\right\}\,ds=K(x)\left\{q_{1}(x)+p_{1}(x)+|\lambda|x\right\}\,.

The exact estimate is valid for
|u_{2}(x)|
.
Applying these estimates again, from (
1.0.16
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left|u_{1,2}(x)\right|\leq\int\limits^{x}_{0}\left\{\left|q(s)\right|+\left|p(s)\right|+|\lambda|\right\}\cdot K(s)\left\{q_{1}(s)+p_{1}(s)+|\lambda|s\right\}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq K(x)\int\limits^{x}_{0}\left\{q_{1}(s)+p_{1}(s)+|\lambda|s\right\}\cdot\frac{d}{ds}\left\{q_{1}(s)+p_{1}(s)+|\lambda|s\right\}\,ds

\displaystyle=K(x)\frac{1}{2}\int\limits^{x}_{0}\frac{d}{ds}\,\left[q_{1}(s)+p_{1}(s)+|\lambda|s\right]^{2}ds=K(x)\,\frac{\left[q_{1}(x)+p_{1}(x)+|\lambda|x\right]^{2}}{2!}\;.

Continue substituting these estimates into (
1.0.16
), we get

\left|u_{1,2}(x)\right|\leq K(x)\,\frac{\left[q_{1}(x)+p_{1}(x)+|\lambda|x\right]^{n}}{n!}

for any
n\geq 1
.
Therefore,
\left|u_{1,2}(x)\right|\equiv 0
.
This completes the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Notes and references

Even though the question of the existence and uniqueness of the solution to the Cauchy problem for the normal system of ordinary differential equations, under the condition of local summability of the coefficients (as well as the analytical dependence of the solution on the spectral parameter and initial conditions), is classical, we did not find a complete presentation of them.
At the same time, we recommend monographs
[
Nai69
]
and
[
Atk64
]
.

In our case, the strict proof of these questions, under the condition of
local summability
of the coefficients, was published in
[
Har04
]
.

The surname Harutyunyan is spelled in Russian as ”Arutyunyan” due to the absence of the letter ”H” in the Russian alphabet.

CHAPTER
2
Transformation operators

2.1
Introduction and statements of the main results.

As we have already noted, the canonical Dirac system

By^{\prime}+\Omega(x)y=\lambda y,

(2.1.1)

is a normal system

y^{\prime}=-\lambda By+B\Omega(x)y=A(x,\lambda)y,

where the matrix
A(x,\lambda)
has the form (
1.0.5
).
An important role in the theory of normal systems plays the ”fundamental matrix”
\Phi(x,\lambda)
, i.e., the
2\times 2
matrix-valued solution to the Cauchy problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}y^{\prime}=A(x,\lambda)y,\\
y(0,\lambda)=E.\end{array}\right.

If
y=\Phi(x,\lambda)
is a fundamental matrix, then the solution to the Cauchy problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}y^{\prime}=A(x,\lambda)y,\\
y(0,\lambda)=C,\end{array}\right.

is given by the formula
y=\Phi(x,\lambda)C
for arbitrary
2\times m
matrix
C
(
C\in\mathfrak{M}^{2,m}
)
1
1

1

\mathfrak{M}^{n,m}
is the set of all matrices, which have
n
rows and
m
columns.
.
Thus, if we know the fundamental matrix
\Phi(x,\lambda)
, then we know all the solutions of the system (
2.1.1
).
So, our goal is to get as much information as possible about the fundamental matrix
\Phi(x,\lambda)
of the system (
2.1.1
).

The existence of the fundamental matrix
\Phi(x,\lambda)
follows from Theorem
1.1
.
Indeed

\Phi(x,\lambda)=\left(\begin{matrix}\varphi_{1}(x,\lambda,\pi/2)&\varphi_{1}(x,\lambda,\pi)\\
\varphi_{2}(x,\lambda,\pi/2)&\varphi_{2}(x,\lambda,\pi)\end{matrix}\right),

where
\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\left(\varphi_{1}(x,\lambda,\alpha)\;\varphi_{2}(x,\lambda,\alpha)\right)^{T}
is the solution to the Cauchy problem (
1.0.2
), the existence and uniqueness of which are proved in Theorem
1.1
.

If
\Omega(x)\equiv 0
, then the system (
2.1.1
) has the form
By^{\prime}=\lambda y
or the same as

y^{\prime}=-\lambda By,

(2.1.2)

i.e., this is a system with constant coefficients and, therefore, we know the explicit form of its fundamental matrix, which is

\Phi_{0}(x,\lambda)=e^{-B\lambda x}.

(2.1.3)

Let us remind that for a numerical square matrix of order N, the exponent
e^{A}
is defined as a series

e^{A}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\dfrac{A^{n}}{n!}.

Thus, the fundamental matrix (
2.1.3
) has the form

\Phi_{0}(x,\lambda)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\dfrac{(-B\lambda x)^{n}}{n!}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\dfrac{(-1)^{n}(\lambda x)^{n}}{n!}B^{n}.

From the form of matrix
B=\frac{1}{i}\sigma_{1}
and the properties of Pauli matrices, we get

\begin{array}[]{cc}B=B,&B^{2}=-E,\\
B^{3}=-B,&B^{4}=E,\end{array}

and more general

B^{2k}=(-1)^{k}E,\qquad B^{2k+1}=(-1)^{k}B.

So, for
\Phi_{0}(x,\lambda)
, we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}(x,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\dfrac{(-1)^{2k}(\lambda x)^{2k}}{(2k)!}B^{2k}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\dfrac{(-1)^{2k+1}(\lambda x)^{2k+1}}{(2k+1)!}B^{2k+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\dfrac{(-1)^{k}(\lambda x)^{2k}}{(2k)!}E-\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\dfrac{(-1)^{k}(\lambda x)^{2k+1}}{(2k+1)!}B

\displaystyle=\cos(\lambda x)E-\sin(\lambda x)B=\left(\begin{matrix}\cos(\lambda x)&-\sin(\lambda x)\\
\sin(\lambda x)&\cos(\lambda x)\end{matrix}\right).

We will consider the canonical Dirac system (
2.1.1
) as a perturbation of the system (
2.1.2
).
We hope that the fundamental matrix of (
2.1.1
) will be some perturbation of the fundamental matrix
\Phi(x,\lambda)=e^{-B\lambda x}
.

The method of variations of constants suggests that we can look for the fundamental matrix
\Phi(x,\lambda)
in the form

\Phi(x,\lambda)=\Phi_{0}(x,\lambda)~U(x,\lambda)=e^{-B\lambda x}~U(x,\lambda)

(2.1.4)

According to Theorem
1.1
, the entries of
\Phi(x,\lambda)
are entire functions of parameter
\lambda
(for arbitrary fixed
x
).
Therefore, the entries of the matrix
U(x,\lambda)=e^{B\lambda x}\Phi(x,\lambda)
also have this property.

Substituting the expression (
2.1.4
) into (
2.1.1
) and taking into account the properties of Pauli matrices, for the matrix
U(x,\lambda)
, we obtain the Cauchy problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}U^{\prime}(x,\lambda)=e^{2B\lambda x}B\Omega(x)U(x,\lambda),\\
U(0,\lambda)=E\end{array}\right.

which is equivalent to the integral equation

U(x,\lambda)=E+\int_{0}^{x}e^{2B\lambda t}B\Omega(t)U(t,\lambda)dt.

As indicated in
[
Mar77
]
(page 30), if we look for a solution to this integral equation in the form

U(x,\lambda)=E+\int_{0}^{x}e^{2B\lambda t}Q(x,t)dt,

i.e. look for a solution
\Phi(x,\lambda)
in the form

\Phi(x,\lambda)=e^{-B\lambda x}\left(E+\int^{x}_{0}e^{2B\lambda t}Q(x,t)\,dt\right),

(2.1.5)

then, for the (new unknown) matrix-function
Q(x,t)
, we obtain the equation

Q(x,t)=B\Omega(t)+\int^{x-t}_{0}B\Omega(t+\xi)Q(t+\xi,\xi)\,d\xi,\quad 0\leq t\leq x<a,

(2.1.6)

the solvability of which must be proved by the method of successive approximations.

In the case of continuous
p
and
q
(the continuous matrix
\Omega
), the specified path is implemented by classical methods.
If we abandon the continuity condition (and assume that
p,q\in L^{1}_{loc}
), then under solution of (
2.1.6
), we must understand the matrix
Q(x,t)
, such that the equality (
2.1.6
) holds almost everywhere in
t\in[0,x]
and uniformly in
x\in[0,a]
.
More precisely, under solution of (
2.1.6
), we will mean a matrix
Q(x,t)
such that

\sup_{x\in[0,a]}\int^{x}_{0}\left|Q(x,t)-B\Omega(t)-\int^{x-t}_{0}B\Omega(t+\xi)Q(t+\xi,\xi)\,d\xi\right|\,dt=0,

(2.1.7)

where
a
is an arbitrary finite positive number.

Thus, our plan for studying the properties of the fundamental matrix
\Phi(x,\lambda)
is as follows:

1.

We prove the existence and uniqueness of the solution of the equation (
2.1.6
).

2.

We prove that if
Q(x,t)
is the solution of equation (
2.1.6
), then
\Phi(x,\lambda)
, defined by formula (
2.1.5
), is the fundamental matrix of the system (
2.1.1
).

3.

Using the integral representation (
2.1.5
), we prove Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
(see below), which are our main statements about transformation operators.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
p,q\in L^{1}_{loc}(0,\infty)
, i.e.
p,q
be complex-valued, local summable functions.
Then there exists a matrix
K(x,t)
such that the matrix solution
\Phi(x,\lambda)
of the Cauchy problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\ell\,\Phi=\lambda\Phi,\\
\Phi(0,\lambda)=E,\end{array}\right.

(2.1.8)

can be represented as

\Phi(x,\lambda)=e^{-B\lambda x}+\int^{x}_{-x}K(x,t)e^{-B\lambda t}dt\,,

(2.1.9)

and the norm of the matrix
K
satisfies the estimation

\int^{x}_{-x}|K(x,t)|dt\leq e^{c(x)}-1

(2.1.10)

and almost everywhere for
x\in[0,a]
(where
a>0
is an arbitrary finite number), the following equalities

\displaystyle K_{A}(x,x)B-BK_{A}(x,x)=\Omega(x),

(2.1.11)

\displaystyle K_{C}(x,-x)B+BK_{C}(x,-x)=0\,,

(2.1.12)

hold, where
K_{A}(x,t)
and
K_{C}(x,t)
are anti-commutative and commutative with
B
parts of
K(x,t)
, respectively.

From Theorem
2.1
and representation of the vector-solution of system (
2.1.1
), by the fundamental matrix, we obtain the following assertion.

Theorem 2.2
.

For
p,q\in L^{1}_{loc}(0,\infty)
, the vector-solution
y=\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
of the Cauchy problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\ell y=\lambda y\,,\\
\displaystyle{y(0)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\alpha\\
-\cos\alpha\end{array}\right),\quad\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}\,,}\end{array}\right.

(2.1.13)

can be represented in the form

\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda,\alpha)+\int^{x}_{0}K_{0}(x,t,\alpha)\varphi_{0}(t,\lambda,\alpha)\,dt\;,

(2.1.14)

where
\displaystyle{\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin(\lambda x+\alpha)\\
-\cos(\lambda x+\alpha)\end{array}\right)}
, and

K_{0}(x,t,\alpha)=K(x,t)-K(x,-t)\left(\sigma_{2}\cos 2\alpha+\sigma_{3}\sin 2\alpha\right).

(2.1.15)

Moreover, for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
, almost everywhere for
x\in[0,a]

K_{0A}(x,x,\alpha)B-BK_{0A}(x,x,\alpha)=\Omega(x).

(2.1.16)

The case
p,q\in L^{2}_{loc}(0,\infty)
is especially important in the applications.
For this reason, we are studying this case separately.

Theorem 2.3
.

If
p,q\in L^{2}_{loc}(0,\infty)
, then
K_{ij}(x,\cdot)\in L^{2}(-x,x)
, and
\left[K_{0}(x,\cdot,\alpha)\right]_{ij}\in L^{2}(0,x)
,
i,j=1,2
(
K_{ij}
are the elements of matrix
K
).

When studying the boundary value problem for the system (
2.1.1
) on the segment
[0,\pi]
, in addition to transformation operators (
2.1.9
) and (
2.1.14
) preserving the initial conditions at the point
0
, there are other useful transformation operators which preserve the initial conditions at the point
\pi
(”tied” to the point
\pi
).

Theorem 2.4
.

1.

If
p,q\in L^{1}(0,\pi)
, then there exists a matrix
H(x,t)
such that the matrix-solution
\Psi(x,\lambda)
of the Cauchy problem
\ell\Psi=\lambda\Psi
,
\Psi(\pi,\lambda)=E
that can be represented in the form

\Psi(x,\lambda)=e^{B\lambda(\pi-x)}-\int^{2\pi-x}_{x}H(x,t)e^{B\lambda(\pi-t)}dt.

(2.1.17)

In this case, the estimate

\int^{2\pi-x}_{x}|H(x,t)|\,dt\leq e^{r(x)}-1,

(2.1.18)

takes place for the norm of matrix
H
, where
r(x)=\int^{\pi}_{x}|p(s)|\,ds+\int^{\pi}_{x}|q(s)|\,ds
.
Besides, the equalities

\displaystyle H_{A}(x,x)B-BH_{A}(x,x)=\Omega(x),

(2.1.19)

\displaystyle H_{C}(x,2\pi-x)B+BH_{C}(x,2\pi-x)=0.

(2.1.20)

hold almost everywhere on
[0,\pi]
.

2.

Vector-solution
y=\psi(x,\lambda,\beta)
of the Cauchy problem
\ell y=\lambda y
,

y(\pi)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\beta\\
-\cos\beta\end{array}\right)
can be represented as

\psi(x,\lambda,\beta)=\psi_{0}(x,\lambda,\beta)-\int^{\pi}_{x}H_{\pi}(x,t,\beta)\psi_{0}(t,\lambda,\beta)dt

(2.1.21)

where
\psi_{0}(x,\lambda,\beta)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin(\beta-\lambda(\pi-x))\\
-\cos(\beta-\lambda(\pi-x))\end{array}\right)
, and

H_{\pi}(x,t,\beta)=H(x,t)-H(x,2\pi-t)\cdot\left(\sigma_{2}\cos 2\beta+\sigma_{3}\sin 2\beta\right).

Moreover, for arbitrary
\beta\in\mathbb{C}
almost everywhere for
x\in[0,\pi]

H_{\pi A}(x,x,\beta)B-BH_{\pi A}(x,x,\beta)=\Omega(x).

(2.1.22)

3.

when
p,q\in L^{2}(0,\pi)
,
H_{ij}(x,\cdot)\in L^{2}(x,2\pi-x)
and
\left[H_{\pi}(x,\cdot,\beta)\right]_{ij}\in L^{2}(x,\pi)
,
i,j=1,2
.

2.2
The solution of equation (
2.1.6
).

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
p,q\in L_{loc}^{1}(0,\infty)
.
Then the integral equation (
2.1.6
) (with respect to unknown function
Q(x,t)
):

Q(x,t)=B\Omega(t)+\int^{x-t}_{0}B\Omega(t+\xi)Q(t+\xi,\xi)\,d\xi,\quad 0\leq t\leq x<a,

has a unique solution.
This solution is absolutely continuous by
x
and summable by
t
on
t\in[0,x]
(
Q(x,\cdot)\in L^{1}(0,x)
), more precisely

\int^{x}_{0}\left|Q(x,t)\right|\,dt\leq e^{c(x)}-1\;.

(2.2.1)

We will solve the integral equation (
2.1.6
) by the method of successive approximation.
To this end, we define the sequence of matrices (so far formally)

\displaystyle Q_{0}(x,t)=B\Omega(t)=q(t)\sigma_{2}-p(t)\sigma_{3}

(2.2.2)

\displaystyle Q_{n}(x,t)=\int^{x-t}_{0}B\Omega(t+\xi)Q_{n-1}(t+\xi,\xi)\,d\xi,\quad n\geq 1.

Lemma 2.1
.

The matrices defined in (
2.2.2
) have the following structure

\displaystyle Q_{2n}(x,t)=\alpha_{2n}(x,t)\sigma_{2}+\beta_{2n}(x,t)\sigma_{3},

\displaystyle Q_{2n+1}(x,t)=\alpha_{2n+1}(x,t)E+\beta_{2n+1}(x,t)B,\quad n=0,1,2,\ldots

where the scalar functions
\alpha_{k}(x,t)
and
\beta_{k}(x,t)
are determined from recurrent relations (which hold almost everywhere by
t\in[0,x]
)

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}(x,t)=q(t),\quad\beta_{0}(x,t)=-p(t),

(2.2.3)

\displaystyle\alpha_{m}(x,t)=\int_{0}^{x-t}\left[q(t+\xi)\alpha_{m-1}(t+\xi,\xi)+(-1)^{m}p(t+\xi)\beta_{m-1}(t+\xi,\xi)\right]d\xi,

(2.2.4)

\displaystyle\beta_{m}(x,t)=\int_{0}^{x-t}\left[q(t+\xi)\beta_{m-1}(t+\xi,\xi)-(-1)^{m}p(t+\xi)\alpha_{m-1}(t+\xi,\xi)\right]d\xi,

(2.2.5)

for
m\geq 1
and also the following estimates hold

\int^{x}_{0}\left(|\alpha_{m}(x,t)|+|\beta_{m}(x,t)|\right)\,dt\leq\frac{c^{m+1}(x)}{(m+1)!}\,.

(2.2.6)

Proof.

First, we prove that estimates (
2.2.6
) are valid.
For
m=0
the estimate (
2.2.6
) follows from the definition (
0.0.6
) and (
2.2.3
).
Further steps are by induction: let the estimate (
2.2.6
) is true for some
m
, and we will show that it is also true for
m+1
.
It follows from the definitions (
2.2.3
), (
2.2.4
) and (
2.2.5
) that

\displaystyle\int^{x}_{0}\left(|\alpha_{m+1}(x,t)|+|\beta_{m+1}(x,t)|\right)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\int^{x}_{0}\int^{x-t}_{0}\left(|p(t+\xi)|+|q(t+\xi)|\right)\cdot\left(|\alpha_{m}(t+\xi,\xi)|+|\beta_{m}(t+\xi,\xi)|\right)\,d\xi\,dt\,.

Changing the order of integration in the last integral and changing the variable
s=t+\xi
, we get that it is equal to

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\displaystyle\int^{x}_{0}\int^{x}_{\xi}\left(|p(s)|+|q(s)|\right)\left(|\alpha_{m}(s,\xi)|+|\beta_{m}(s,\xi)|\right)\,ds\,d\xi}

(2.2.7)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\displaystyle=\int^{x}_{0}\left\{\int^{s}_{0}\left(|\alpha_{m}(s,\xi)|+|\beta_{m}(s,\xi)|\right)\,d\xi\right\}\left(|p(s)|+|q(s)|\right)\,ds},

(the last equality was obtained by one more time changing the order of integration).
Now using the induction hypothesis (
2.2.6
) and the fact that
c^{\prime}(s)=|p(s)|+|q(s)|
(see (
0.0.6
)), we obtain that the integral (
2.2.7
) can be estimated from above by the integral

\int^{x}_{0}\dfrac{c^{m+1}(s)}{(m+1)!}\cdot c^{\prime}(s)\,ds=\dfrac{c^{m+2}(x)}{(m+2)!}\,.

Since we have proved the convergence of (
2.2.7
), then, according to the Fubini-Tonelli theorem (see, for example,
[
Ios65
]
), changing the order of integration and the variable under the integral sign are justified.
Thus, the estimate (
2.2.6
) is proved.

The relations (
2.2.4
) and (
2.2.5
) are also should be proved by induction (starting from formula (
2.2.2
) using the properties of Pauli matrices (note that
\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}=B
,
\sigma_{2}B=\sigma_{3}
,
B\sigma_{3}=\sigma_{2}
)

\displaystyle Q_{2n+1}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x-t}Q_{0}(t+\xi,\xi)Q_{2n}(t+\xi,\xi)d\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x-t}\left(q(t+\xi)\sigma_{2}-p(t+\xi)\sigma_{3}\right)\left(\alpha_{2n}(t+\xi,\xi)\sigma_{2}+\beta_{2n}(t+\xi,\xi)\sigma_{3}\right)\,d\xi

\displaystyle=\alpha_{2n+1}(x,t)E+\beta_{2n+1}(x,t)B.

This completes the proof of Lemma
2.1
.
∎

As it is noted in the inequality (
0.0.5
) for the norms of matrices of the form
Q_{2n}=\alpha_{2n}\,\sigma_{2}+\beta_{2n}\,\sigma_{3}
and
Q_{2n+1}=\alpha_{2n+1}\,E+\beta_{2n+1}\,B
, the following estimate is valid (almost everywhere by
t\in[0,x]
)

|Q_{k}(x,t)|\leq|\alpha_{k}(x,t)|+|\beta_{k}(x,t)|.

So, according to (
2.2.6
),

\int_{0}^{x}|Q_{k}(x,t)|\,dt\leq\frac{c^{k+1}(x)}{(k+1)!}.

Let us consider the series

Q(x,t)=\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}Q_{k}(x,t).

Since the series

\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\int_{0}^{x}|Q_{k}(x,t)|\,dt\leq\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\frac{c^{k+1}(x)}{(k+1)!}=e^{c(x)}-1

converge uniformly with respect to
x
on an arbitrary finite interval
[0,a]
, then

\int_{0}^{x}|Q(x,t)|\,dt\leq\int_{0}^{x}\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}|Q_{k}(x,t)|\,dt=\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\int_{0}^{x}|Q_{k}(x,t)|\,dt\leq e^{c(x)}-1,

i.e. matrix
Q(x,t)
(and all its entries) is summable by
t\in[0,x]
, where
x
is an arbitrary finite positive number (so, the estimate (
2.2.1
) is proved).

Since successive approximations

\displaystyle S_{N}(x,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}\sum^{N}_{k=0}Q_{k}(x,t)

converge to
Q(x,t)
in the sense

\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{x\in[0,a]}\int^{x}_{0}|Q(x,t)-S_{N}(x,t)|\,dt=0,

then from the equality

S_{N}(x,t)=B\Omega(t)+\int^{x-t}_{0}B\Omega(t+\xi)S_{N-1}(t+\xi,\xi)\,d\xi,

which holds almost everywhere by
t\in[0,x]
, by passing to the limit, when
N\to\infty
, we obtain (
2.1.7
) :

\sup_{x\in[0,a]}\int^{x}_{0}\left|Q(x,t)-B\Omega(t)-\int^{x-t}_{0}B\Omega(t+\xi)Q(t+\xi,\xi)\,d\xi\right|\,dt=0,

Thus,
Q(x,t)
is the solution of integral equation (
2.1.6
) in the sense (
2.1.7
), and the existence of the solution of (
2.1.6
) is proved.

Since the right-hand side of the equation

Q(x,t)=B\Omega(t)+\int^{x}_{0}B\Omega(t+\xi)Q(t+\xi,\xi)\,d\xi,

depends on
x
as a function of the upper limit, the solution
Q(x,t)
is an absolutely continuous function with respect to
x
, and the equalities

\displaystyle\frac{\partial Q(x,t)}{\partial x}=B\Omega(x)Q(x,x-t),\quad 0\leq t\leq x<a,

(2.2.8)

\displaystyle Q(x,x)=B\Omega(x)

(2.2.9)

hold almost everywhere.

Now we want to prove the uniqueness of the solution of the equation (
2.1.6
).

Lemma 2.2
.

The integral equation (
2.1.6
) has a unique solution.

Proof.

Assume that there exist two solutions
Q_{1}(x,t)
and
Q_{2}(x,t)
and denote their difference by
\Delta Q(x,t):=Q_{1}(x,t)-Q_{2}(x,t)
.
We will prove that for arbitrary
x>0
,

\int_{0}^{x}|\Delta Q(x,t)|\,dt=0,

which will give the uniqueness of the solution of the equation (
2.1.6
) in the class
L^{1}(0,x)
(or in the sense of (
2.1.7
).

Obviously, the difference
\Delta Q(x,t)
satisfies the homogeneous integral equation

\Delta Q(x,t)=\int_{0}^{x-t}Q_{0}(t+\xi)\Delta Q(t+\xi,\xi)d\xi.

Since for arbitrary
x>0
, the quantities
\int_{0}^{x}|Q_{i}(x,t)|\,dt
, for
i=1,2
are finite, so the integral
\int_{0}^{x}|\Delta Q(x,t)|\,dt
as well.
Let us denote

G(x)=\sup_{0\leq s\leq x}\int_{0}^{s}|\Delta Q(s,t)|\,dt.

(2.2.10)

Further, note that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}|\Delta Q(x,t)|\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{x}\int_{0}^{x-t}|Q_{0}(t+\xi)|\,|\Delta Q(t+\xi,\xi)|\,d\xi dt=

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{s}|\Delta Q(s,\xi)|\,d\xi\right)|Q_{0}(s)|\,ds\leq G(x)c(x),

since

\int_{0}^{x}|Q_{0}(s)|\,ds\leq\int_{0}^{x}|p(s)|\,ds+\int_{0}^{x}|q(s)|\,ds=c(x)

(see (
2.2.2
) and (
2.2.10
) ).
Repeating this estimate, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}|\Delta Q(x,t)|\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{x}\left(\int_{0}^{s}|\Delta Q(s,\xi)|\,d\xi\right)|Q_{0}(s)|\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{x}G(s)c(s)c^{\prime}(s)\,ds\leq G(x)\int_{0}^{x}\frac{\left(c^{2}(s)\right)^{\prime}}{2}\,ds=G(x)\frac{c^{2}(x)}{2}.

Repeating these estimates (i.e., applying the induction method), we arrive at the inequality

\int_{0}^{x}|\Delta Q(x,t)|\,dt\leq G(x)\frac{c^{n}(x)}{n!},\quad n\geq 0.

Since the left-hand side does not depend on
n
, and the right-hand side tends to zero as
n\to\infty
(uniformly by
x\in[0,a]
), then

\int_{0}^{x}|\Delta Q(x,t)|\,dt\equiv 0,

for arbitrary finite
x
.
This completes the proof of Lemma
2.2

∎

Lemma 2.3
.

If
Q(x,t)
is the solution of (
2.1.6
), then
\Phi(x,\lambda)
, defined by formula (
2.1.5
):

\Phi(x,\lambda)=e^{-B\lambda x}\left(E+\int^{x}_{0}e^{2B\lambda t}Q(x,t)\,dt\right),

is the solution of the Cauchy problem (
2.1.8
).

Proof.

Since
Q(x,t)
is absolutely continuous by
x
, then the right-hand side in (
2.1.6
) is an absolutely continuous function.
Using the equalities (
2.2.8
), (
2.2.9
) and the properties of the Pauli
matrices, we obtain that for derivatives
\Phi^{\prime}(x,\lambda)
, the following equalities hold almost everywhere:

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(x,\lambda)

\displaystyle=-\lambda Be^{-B\lambda x}\left(E+\int_{0}^{x}e^{2B\lambda t}Q(x,t)dt\right)+

\displaystyle+e^{-B\lambda x}\left(e^{2B\lambda x}B\Omega(x)+\int_{0}^{x}e^{2B\lambda t}B\Omega(x)Q(x,x-t)dt\right)

\displaystyle=-\lambda B\Phi(x,\lambda)+B\Omega(x)e^{-B\lambda x}+B\Omega(x)\int_{0}^{x}e^{B\lambda(x-2t)}Q(x,x-t)dt

\displaystyle=-\lambda B\Phi(x,\lambda)+B\Omega(x)e^{-B\lambda x}\left(E+\int_{0}^{x}e^{B\lambda(2x-2t)}Q(x,x-t)dt\right)

\displaystyle=-\lambda B\Phi(x,\lambda)+B\Omega(x)\Phi(x,\lambda).

Multiplying both sides of the last equality, from the left, by the matrix
B
and taking into account that
B^{2}=-E
, we obtain that the matrix
\Phi(x,\lambda)
is the solution of (
2.1.1
).
The equality
\Phi(0,\lambda)=E
follows from form (
2.1.6
).
Lemma
2.3
is proved.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

Since we were looking for the fundamental matrix
\Phi(x,\lambda)
in the form (
2.1.5
), so we have

\displaystyle\Phi(x,\lambda)

\displaystyle=e^{-B\lambda x}+\int_{0}^{x}e^{B\lambda(2t-x)}Q(x,t)dt

(2.2.11)

\displaystyle=e^{-B\lambda x}+\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{-x}^{x}e^{B\lambda s}Q\left(x,\frac{x+s}{2}\right)ds,

where we performed a change of the variable
s=2t-x
.
Let us represent the matrix
Q(x,\lambda)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}Q_{k}(x,t)
in the form

Q(x,t)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}Q_{2k}(x,t)+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}Q_{2k+1}(x,t)=Q_{+}(x,t)+Q_{-}(x,t).

According to Lemma
2.1

Q_{+}(x,t)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}Q_{2k}(x,t)=\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{2k}(x,t)\right)\sigma_{2}+\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta_{2k}(x,t)\right)\sigma_{3}

(2.2.12)

and, therefore, it anti-commutes with matrix
B
, i.e.
Q_{+}B=-BQ_{+}
, and

Q_{-}(x,t)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}Q_{2k+1}(x,t)=\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{2k+1}(x,t)\right)E+\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta_{2k+1}(x,t)\right)B,

(2.2.13)

which commutes with matrix
B
, i.e.,
Q_{-}B=BQ_{-}
.
In particular, it follows that

e^{B\lambda t}Q_{+}(x,t)=Q_{+}(x,t)e^{-B\lambda t}

and

e^{B\lambda t}Q_{-}(x,t)=Q_{-}(x,t)e^{B\lambda t}.

Therefore, the representation (
2.2.11
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\Phi(x,\lambda)

\displaystyle=e^{-B\lambda x}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{-x}^{x}e^{B\lambda s}\left(Q_{+}\left(x,\frac{x+s}{2}\right)+Q_{-}\left(x,\frac{x+s}{2}\right)\right)\,ds

\displaystyle=e^{-B\lambda x}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{-x}^{x}Q_{+}\left(x,\frac{x+s}{2}\right)e^{-B\lambda s}\,ds+\frac{1}{2}\int_{-x}^{x}Q_{-}\left(x,\frac{x+s}{2}\right)e^{B\lambda s}ds.

Changing the variable
s
by
-s
, only in the last integral, we obtain

\Phi(x,\lambda)=e^{-B\lambda x}+\int_{-x}^{x}K(x,s)e^{-B\lambda s}ds,

where by
K(x,s)
we denote the matrix

K(x,s)=\dfrac{1}{2}\left[Q_{+}\left(x,\frac{x+s}{2}\right)+Q_{-}\left(x,\frac{x-s}{2}\right)\right].

(2.2.14)

Thus, the representation (
2.1.9
) is proved.
To prove the estimate (
2.1.10
), first, note that according to (
2.2.12
) and (
0.0.5
)

\displaystyle\int_{-x}^{x}\left|Q_{+}\left(x,\frac{x+s}{2}\right)\right|\,ds=2\int_{0}^{x}|Q_{+}(x,t)|\,dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\int_{0}^{x}\left(\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{2k}(x,t)\right|+\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta_{2k}(x,t)\right|\right)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{x}\left(|\alpha_{2k}(x,t)|+|\beta_{2k}(x,t)|\right)\,dt.

Similarly, according to (
2.2.13
) and (
0.0.5
)

\displaystyle\int_{-x}^{x}\left|Q_{-}\left(x,\frac{x-s}{2}\right)\right|\,ds=2\int_{0}^{x}|Q_{-}(x,t)|\,dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{x}\left(\left|\alpha_{2k+1}(x,t)\right|+|\beta_{2k+1}(x,t)|\right)\,dt.

Therefore, it follows from (
2.2.14
) and (
2.2.6
) that

\displaystyle\int_{-x}^{x}|K(x,t)|\,dt\leq\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{-x}^{x}\left|Q_{+}\left(x,\frac{x+s}{2}\right)\right|\,ds+\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{-x}^{x}\left|Q_{-}\left(x,\frac{x-s}{2}\right)\right|\,ds

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{x}(|\alpha_{k}(x,t)|+|\beta_{k}(x,t)|)\,dt\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{c^{k+1}(x)}{(k+1)!}=e^{c(x)}-1.

Thus, the estimate (
2.1.10
) is proved.

To prove the equalities (
2.1.11
) and (
2.1.12
), we write the expression for the kernel
K(x,t)
in the form

\displaystyle K(x,t)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{2k}\left(x,\frac{x+t}{2}\right)\right)\sigma_{2}+\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta_{2k}\left(x,\frac{x+t}{2}\right)\right)\sigma_{3}+\right.

(2.2.15)

\displaystyle+\left.\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{2k+1}\left(x,\frac{x-t}{2}\right)\right)E+\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta_{2k+1}\left(x,\frac{x-t}{2}\right)\right)B\right]

Note that from (
2.2.4
) and (
2.2.5
), it follows

\alpha_{m}(x,x)=\beta_{m}(x,x)\equiv 0,\qquad\text{ for }\;m\geq 1,

(2.2.16)

whence, in its turn, it follows that

\alpha_{m}(x,0)=\int^{x}_{0}\left[q(\xi)\alpha_{m-1}(\xi,\xi)+(-1)^{m}p(\xi)\beta_{m-1}(\xi,\xi)\right]d\xi=0,

\beta_{m}(x,0)=\int^{x}_{0}\left[q(\xi)\beta_{m-1}(\xi,\xi)+(-1)^{m}p(\xi)\alpha_{m-1}(\xi,\xi)\right]d\xi=0,

for
m\geq 2
.
Besides these, from the same (
2.2.3
)–(
2.2.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}(x,x)=q(x),\quad\beta_{0}(x,x)=-p(x),

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(x,0)=\int^{x}_{0}\left[q^{2}(\xi)+p^{2}(\xi)\right]\ d\xi,

\displaystyle\beta_{1}(x,0)=-2\int^{x}_{0}p(\xi)q(\xi)d\xi.

It follows from (
2.2.14
) that

K(x,x)=\frac{1}{2}\left[Q_{+}(x,x)+Q_{-}(x,0)\right],

and according to (
2.2.12
), (
2.2.13
), (
2.2.15
) and other above-obtained relations, we get

\displaystyle K(x,x)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\alpha_{2k}(x,x)\right)\sigma_{2}+\left(\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\beta_{2k}(x,x)\right)\sigma_{3}\right]+

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\alpha_{2k+1}(x,0)\right)E+\left(\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\beta_{2k+1}(x,0)\right)B\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{0}(x,x)\sigma_{2}+\beta_{0}(x,x)\sigma_{3})+\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}(x,0)E+\beta_{1}(x,0)B)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(q(x)\sigma_{2}-p(x)\sigma_{3})+

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left[\int^{x}_{0}(q^{2}(\xi)+p^{2}(\xi))\,d\xi\cdot E-2\int^{x}_{0}p(\xi)q(\xi)\,d\xi\cdot B\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{A}(x,x)+K_{C}(x,x)

In the case of smooth potentials and even for
p,q\in L^{2}_{loc}(0,\infty)
, the expression
K(x,x)
has a precise meaning.
But in the case
p,q\in L^{1}_{loc}(0,\infty)
, the part of
K(x,x)
that commutes with
B
, i.e.

K_{C}(x,x)=\frac{1}{2}\left[\int^{x}_{0}\left(p^{2}(\xi)+q^{2}(\xi)\right)\,d\xi\cdot E-2\int^{x}_{0}p(\xi)q(\xi)\,d\xi\cdot B\right],

may not make sense due to the divergence of the integrals.
But for the anti-commuting part

K_{A}(x,x)=\frac{1}{2}[q(x)\sigma_{2}-p(x)\sigma_{3}],

due to the equalities

\sigma_{2}B=\sigma_{3},\quad\sigma_{3}B=-\sigma_{2},

one easily obtains the quantity (which takes place almost everywhere)

K_{A}(x,x)B-BK_{A}(x,x)=q(x)\sigma_{3}+p(x)\sigma_{2}=\Omega(x),

thus, (
2.1.11
) is proved.

To prove (
2.1.12
), again from (
2.2.14
) we obtain the expression

K(x,-x)=\frac{1}{2}\left[Q_{+}(x,0)+Q_{-}(x,x)\right],

which, according to (
2.2.12
), (
2.2.13
) and (
2.2.3
), (
2.2.15
), (
2.2.16
) takes the form

\displaystyle K(x,-x)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\alpha_{2k}(x,0)\right)\sigma_{2}+\left(\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\beta_{2k}(x,0)\right)\sigma_{3}\right]+

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\alpha_{2k+1}(x,x)\right)E+\left(\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\beta_{2k+1}(x,x)\right)B\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\alpha_{0}(x,0)\sigma_{2}+\beta_{0}(x,0)\sigma_{3}\right]=\frac{1}{2}\left[q(0)\sigma_{2}-p(0)\sigma_{3}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{A}(x,-x).

In the case of potential, for which
p(0)
and
q(0)
are uniquely determined (in particular, for smooth
p
and
q
), the statements

K(x,-x)B+BK(x,-x)=0

and

K_{C}(x,-x)B+BK_{C}(x,-x)=0

are equivalent.
Nevertheless, if we set only the condition
p,q\in L^{1}_{loc}(0,\infty)
, then they are not equivalent, because, in spite of formally (algebraically)
K_{A}(x,-x)B+BK_{A}(x,-x)=0
, the expression
K_{A}(x,-x)
itself may not have a clear meaning.
Thus, the statement (
2.1.12
), which is easier to write in the form

K_{C}(x,-x)=0

almost everywhere, is proved.
Note that (
2.1.11
) can be replaced by a simpler one
2K_{A}(x,x)B=\Omega(x)
, but we wrote these statements in that way to emphasize the analogy with the smooth case.
Theorem
2.1
is completely proved.
∎

2.3
Proof of Theorem
2.2
.

It is known that the vector solution of the Cauchy problem is represented through the fundamental matrix
\Phi(x,\lambda)
in the form

y(x)=\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\Phi(x,\lambda)y(0)=\Phi(x,\lambda)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\alpha\\
-\cos\alpha\end{array}\right)

Since
e^{-B\lambda x}=E\cos(\lambda x)-B\sin(\lambda x)
, whence it easily follows that

e^{-B\lambda x}\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\alpha\\
-\cos\alpha\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin(\lambda x+\alpha)\\
-\cos(\lambda x+\alpha)\end{array}\right)

then from the representation (
2.1.5
), we have

\displaystyle\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\Phi(x,\lambda)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\alpha\\
-\cos\alpha\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin(\lambda x+\alpha)\\
-\cos(\lambda x+\alpha)\end{array}\right)+

(2.3.1)

\displaystyle+\left(\int_{-x}^{0}K(x,t)e^{-B\lambda t}dt+\int_{0}^{x}K(x,t)e^{-B\lambda t}dt\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\alpha\\
-\cos\alpha\end{array}\right)

Noticing that the square of the orthogonal matrix

A_{\alpha}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\cos 2\alpha&\sin 2\alpha\\
\sin 2\alpha&-\cos 2\alpha\end{array}\right)=\sigma_{2}\cos 2\alpha+\sigma_{3}\sin 2\alpha

is equal to the identity matrix, i.e.
A_{\alpha}^{2}=E
and that
A_{\alpha}
anti-commutes with
B
, whence it follows that
A_{\alpha}e^{B\lambda t}=e^{-B\lambda t}A_{\alpha}
, as well as the fact

A_{\alpha}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\sin\alpha\\
-\cos\alpha\end{array}\right)=-\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\alpha\\
-\cos\alpha\end{array}\right),

we write down the part
\int_{-x}^{0}
of the expression (
2.3.1
) in the form

\displaystyle\int_{-x}^{0}K(x,t)e^{-B\lambda t}dt\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\alpha\\
-\cos\alpha\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=-\int_{x}^{0}K(x,-t)A_{\alpha}A_{\alpha}e^{B\lambda t}dt\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\alpha\\
-\cos\alpha\end{array}\right)=\int_{0}^{x}K(x,-t)A_{\alpha}A_{\alpha}e^{B\lambda t}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\alpha\\
-\cos\alpha\end{array}\right)dt

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{x}K(x,-t)A_{\alpha}e^{-B\lambda t}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\alpha\\
-\cos\alpha\end{array}\right)dt=-\int_{0}^{x}K(x,-t)A_{\alpha}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin(\lambda t+\alpha)\\
-\cos(\lambda t+\alpha)\end{array}\right)dt.

Thus, (
2.3.1
) gets the form

\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda,\alpha)+\int_{0}^{x}(K(x,t)-K(x,-t)A_{\alpha})\varphi_{0}(t,\lambda,\alpha)dt,

i.e. the representation (
2.1.14
) with the kernel
K_{0}(x,t,\alpha)
of the form (
2.1.15
) is proved.
To prove equality (
2.1.16
), note that using (
2.1.11
) and (
2.1.12
) for
K_{0}
of the form (
2.1.15
), we obtain (taking into account the previous reasoning) that

\displaystyle K_{0}(x,x,\alpha)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}[q(x)\sigma_{2}-p(x)\sigma_{3}]+

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left[p(0)\sin 2\alpha-q(0)\cos 2\alpha+\int^{x}_{0}[p^{2}(\xi)+q^{2}(\xi)]d\xi\right]E-

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left[p(0)\cos 2\alpha+q(0)\sin 2\alpha-2\int^{x}_{0}p(\xi)q(\xi)d\xi\right]B\,.

In particular, this implies that

K_{0A}(x,x,\alpha)=\frac{1}{2}\,q(x)\sigma_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\,p(x)\sigma_{3}=K_{A}(x,x)

whence it follows that (
2.1.16
) is the corollary of (
2.1.11
).
In the case when the second and third terms in the expression for
K_{0}(x,x,\alpha)
, i.e.,
K_{0C}(x,x,\alpha)
make sense (in particular for smooth
p
and
q
), (
2.1.16
) can be written in the form
K_{0}(x,x,\alpha)B=BK_{0}(x,x,\alpha)=\Omega(x)
.
Thus, Theorem
2.2
is proved.

2.4
The case
p,q\in L^{2}_{loc}
. Proof of Theorem
2.3
.

Here we will prove that for
p,q\in L_{loc}^{2}(0,\infty)
the entries
K_{ij}
of kernels
K(x,t)
and
K_{0}(x,t)
are also square- summable functions of
t
, more precisely:
K_{ij}(x,\cdot)\in L^{2}(-x,x)
and
(K_{0}(x,\cdot,\alpha))_{ij}\in L^{2}(0,x)
, (
i,j=1,2
).

For this purpose, we introduce a notation

d(x)=\left(\int_{0}^{x}|p(s)|^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\int_{0}^{x}|q(s)|^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

and prove the following assertion.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
p,q\in L_{loc}^{2}(0,\infty)
, then for the functions
\alpha_{k}(x,t)
and
\beta_{k}(x,t)
, defined by the equalities (
2.2.4
) and (
2.2.5
), the estimates

\displaystyle|\alpha_{2k}(x,t)|,\,|\beta_{2k}(x,t)|\leq d^{2k+1}(x)\frac{t^{\frac{k-1}{2}}(x-t)^{\frac{k}{2}}}{(k-1)!\sqrt{k}},\qquad k=1,2,\dots.

(2.4.1)

\displaystyle|\alpha_{2k+1}(x,t)|,\,|\beta_{2k+1}(x,t)|\leq d^{2k+2}(x)\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}}(x-t)^{\frac{k}{2}}}{k!},\quad k=0,1,2,\dots.

(2.4.2)

hold.

Proof.

According to the definition (
2.2.3
)
|\alpha_{0}(x,t)|=|q(t)|
,
|\beta_{0}(x,t)|=|p(t)|
.
For
|\alpha_{1}(x,t)|
and
|\beta_{1}(x,t)|
we have (see (
2.2.4
) and (
2.2.5
)):

\displaystyle|\alpha_{1}(x,t)|\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x-t}\left(|q(t+\xi)|\,|q(\xi)|+|p(t+\xi)|\,|p(\xi)|\right)d\xi

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}|q(t+\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}|q(\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+

\displaystyle+\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}|p(t+\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}|p(\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}|q(s)|^{2}ds+\int_{0}^{x}|p(s)|^{2}ds\leq d^{2}(x),

\displaystyle|\beta_{1}(x,t)|\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x-t}\left(|q(t+\xi)||p(\xi)|+|p(t+\xi)||q(\xi)|\right)d\xi

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}|q(t+\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}|p(\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+

\displaystyle+\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}|p(t+\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}|q(\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\left(\int_{0}^{x}|q(s)|^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{x}|p(s)|^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}|q(s)|^{2}ds+\int_{0}^{x}|p(s)|^{2}ds\leq d^{2}(x).

Let us check the validity of estimates (
2.4.1
) and (
2.4.2
) also for
\alpha_{2}(x,t)
and
\beta_{2}(x,t)
:

\displaystyle|\alpha_{2}(x,t)|\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x-t}\left(|q(t+\xi)||\alpha_{1}(t+\xi,\xi)|+|p(t+\xi)||\beta_{1}(t+\xi,\xi)|\right)d\xi

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}|q(t+\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}|\alpha_{1}(t+\xi,\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+

\displaystyle+\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}|p(t+\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}|\beta_{1}(t+\xi,\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{x}|q(s)|^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}d^{4}(t+\xi)d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+

\displaystyle+\left(\int_{0}^{x}|p(s)|^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}d^{4}(t+\xi)d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{x}|q(s)|^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}d^{2}(x)(x-t)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\int_{0}^{x}|p(s)|^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}d^{2}(x)(x-t)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d^{3}(x)(x-t)^{\frac{1}{2}}

Here we use the fact that
d(x)
is a monotone increasing, continuous function:

\int_{0}^{x-t}d^{4}(t+\xi)d\xi\leq\max_{0\leq\xi\leq x-t}d^{4}(t+\xi)\int_{0}^{x-t}d\xi=d^{4}(x)(x-t)

Because the above-obtained estimates for
\alpha_{1}(x,t)
and
\beta_{1}(x,t)
are the same, the estimate for
\beta_{2}(x,t)
will be the same as for
\alpha_{2}(x,t)
.
The same remark lets us conclusion that the estimates for
\alpha_{k}(x,t)
and
\beta_{k}(x,t)
will be the same.

Further by induction: let the estimates(
2.4.1
) and (
2.4.2
) hold, for
n=2k
, then

\displaystyle|\alpha_{2k+1}(x,t)|\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x-t}\left(|q(t+\xi)|\,|\alpha_{2k}(t+\xi,\xi)|\right)+|p(t+\xi)||\beta_{2k}(t+\xi,\xi)|d\xi

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left[\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}|q(t+\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}|p(t+\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right]\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}\left[d^{2k+1}(t+\xi)\frac{\xi^{\frac{k-1}{2}}t^{\frac{k}{2}}}{(k-1)!\sqrt{k}}\right]^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(x)d^{2k+1}(x)t^{\frac{k}{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{(k-1)!\sqrt{k}}\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}\xi^{k-1}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d^{2k+2}(x)\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}}(x-t)^{\frac{k}{2}}}{k!}

Likewise for
\beta_{2k+1}
.
To go from an odd number to an even number, it suffices to note that

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}\left[d^{2k+2}(t+\xi)\frac{\xi^{\frac{k}{2}}t^{\frac{k}{2}}}{k!}\right]^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq d^{2k+2}(x)\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}}}{k!}\left(\int_{0}^{x-t}\xi^{k}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq d^{2k+2}(x)\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}}(x-t)^{\frac{k+1}{2}}}{k!\sqrt{k+1}}.

Lemma
2.4
is proved.
∎

The representations (
2.2.12
), (
2.2.13
) and (
2.2.14
) immediately yield the following assertion.

Lemma 2.5
.

The entries of the matrix
K(x,t)
have the following representations:

\displaystyle K_{11}(x,t)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\alpha_{2k}\left(x,\frac{x+t}{2}\right)+\alpha_{2k+1}\left(x,\frac{x-t}{2}\right)\right),

\displaystyle K_{12}(x,t)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\beta_{2k}\left(x,\frac{x+t}{2}\right)+\beta_{2k+1}\left(x,\frac{x-t}{2}\right)\right),

\displaystyle K_{21}(x,t)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\beta_{2k}\left(x,\frac{x+t}{2}\right)-\beta_{2k+1}\left(x,\frac{x-t}{2}\right)\right),

\displaystyle K_{22}(x,t)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\alpha_{2k+1}\left(x,\frac{x-t}{2}\right)-\alpha_{2k}\left(x,\frac{x+t}{2}\right)\right).

To prove the quadratic summability of
K_{ij}(x,t)
in
t
(i.e.
K_{ij}(x,t)\in L^{2}(-x,x)
) we need estimates
|K_{ij}(x,t)|^{2}
.
To this end, we first obtain estimates for
|K_{ij}(x,t)|
.
From lemmas
2.4
and
2.5
, we have

\displaystyle|K_{11}(x,t)|\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left|\alpha_{2k}\left(x,\frac{x+t}{2}\right)\right|+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left|\alpha_{2k+1}\left(x,\frac{x-t}{2}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|\alpha_{0}\left(x,\frac{x+t}{2}\right)\right|+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left|\alpha_{2k}\left(x,\frac{x+t}{2}\right)\right|+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left|\alpha_{2k+1}\left(x,\frac{x-t}{2}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|q\left(\frac{x+t}{2}\right)\right|+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left|d^{2k+1}(x)\frac{\left(\frac{x+t}{2}\right)^{\frac{k-1}{2}}{\left(\frac{x-t}{2}\right)}^{\frac{k}{2}}}{(k-1)!\sqrt{k}}\right|+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left|d^{2k+2}(x)\frac{\left(\frac{x-t}{2}\right)^{\frac{k}{2}}{\left(\frac{x+t}{2}\right)}^{\frac{k}{2}}}{k!}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left|q(\frac{x+t}{2})\right|+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left|d^{2n+3}(x)\frac{\left(\frac{x+t}{2}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}{\left(\frac{x-t}{2}\right)}^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}{n!\sqrt{n+1}}\right|+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left|d^{2n+2}(x)\frac{\left(\frac{x^{2}-t^{2}}{4}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}}{n!}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left|q\left(\frac{x+t}{2}\right)\right|+\frac{1}{2}d(x)\left(\frac{x-t}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}d^{2n+2}(x)\frac{\left(\frac{x^{2}-t^{2}}{4}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}}{n!\sqrt{n+1}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}d^{2n+2}(x)\frac{\left(\frac{x^{2}-t^{2}}{4}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}}{n!}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|q\left(\frac{x+t}{2}\right)\right|+\frac{1}{2}\left(d(x)\left(\frac{x-t}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+1\right)d^{2}(x)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left(d^{2}(x)\left(\frac{x^{2}-t^{2}}{4}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{n}}{n!}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left|q\left(\frac{x+t}{2}\right)\right|+\frac{d^{2}(x)}{2}\left(d(x)\left(\frac{x-t}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+1\right)e^{\frac{d^{2}(x)\sqrt{x^{2}-t^{2}}}{2}}.

Since
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2(a^{2}+b^{2})
, then

|K_{11}(x,t)|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|q\left(\frac{x+t}{2}\right)\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}d^{4}(x)\left[d(x)\left(\frac{x-t}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+1\right]^{2}e^{d^{2}(x)\sqrt{x^{2}-t^{2}}}

(2.4.3)

It obviously follows from
2.4
and
2.5
, that exactly the same estimate holds for
|K_{22}(x,t)|^{2}
.
Moreover, for
|K_{12}(x,t)|^{2}
and
|K_{21}(x,t)|^{2}
, the estimates differ only that in the first term
{\dfrac{1}{2}\left|q\left(\frac{x+t}{2}\right)\right|^{2}}
in (
2.4.3
) is replaced by
{\dfrac{1}{2}\left|p\left(\frac{x+t}{2}\right)\right|^{2}}
.
From (
2.4.3
) remark above, one can obtain estimates for the integrals

\displaystyle\int_{-x}^{x}|K_{ii}(x,t)|^{2}dt\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{-x}^{x}\left|q\left(\frac{x+t}{2}\right)\right|^{2}dt+\frac{1}{2}d^{4}(x)\int_{-x}^{x}\left[d(x)\left(\frac{x-t}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+1\right]^{2}\times

(2.4.4)

\displaystyle\times e^{d^{2}(x)\sqrt{x^{2}-t^{2}}}dt\leq\int_{0}^{x}|q(s)|^{2}ds+2d^{4}(x)\left[x^{2}d^{2}(x)+x\right]e^{xd^{2}(x)},

\int_{-x}^{x}|K_{ij}(x,t)|^{2}dt\leq\int_{0}^{x}|p(s)|^{2}ds+2d^{4}(x)[x^{2}d^{2}(x)+x]e^{xd^{2}(x)},

(2.4.5)

(i,j=1,2,i\neq j)
.
Thus, the following lemma is proved.

Theorem 2.6
.

For
p,q\in L_{loc}^{2}(0,\infty)
, the elements
K_{ij}(x,t)
of the matrix-kernel
K(x,t)
of the transformation operator (
2.1.9
) satisfy the estimates (
2.4.3
)–(
2.4.5
) and, particularly,
K_{i,j}(x,\cdot)\in L^{2}(-x,x)

(i,j=1,2)
.

The assertion of Theorem
2.3
regarding the kernel
K_{0}(x,t,\alpha)
, that is
(K_{0}(x,\cdot,\alpha))_{ij}\in L^{2}(0,x)
, (
i,j=1,2
), is a consequence of similar statements for the kernel
K(x,t)
and representation (
2.1.15
).

2.5
Transformation operators connected to the point
\pi
.

It is known that the fundamental matrix
\Psi(x,\lambda)
, normalized at the point
\pi
, i.e. satisfying the condition
\Psi(\pi,\lambda)=E
, can be represented through the fundamental matrix
\Phi(x,\lambda)
by the formula

\Psi(x,\lambda)=\Phi(x,\lambda)\Phi^{-1}(\pi,\lambda).

One can find the form of the inverse matrix
\Phi^{-1}(\pi,\lambda)
, since there is an identity
\Phi^{-1}(x,\lambda)\equiv-B\Phi^{*}(x,\bar{\lambda})B
(see
[
GK67
]
, p. 299) and try to represent the product
\Phi(x,\lambda)\Phi^{-1}(\pi,\lambda)
as (
2.1.17
).
But this way seems to be technically more complicated, than the application of the same methods that we have already applied in the proof of Theorems
2.1
,
2.2
,
2.3
.
Therefore, in order not to burden the reader, we present here the steps of the proof of theorem
2.4
(which are Lemmas
2.6
–
2.11
) without proofs.

We will look for the fundamental matrix
\Psi(x,\lambda)
in the form
\Psi(x,\lambda)=e^{B\lambda(\pi-x)}V(x,\lambda)
.
For the matrix-function
V(x,\lambda)
, we obtain a Cauchy problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}V^{\prime}=e^{2B\lambda(x-\pi)}B\Omega(x)V,\\
V(\pi,\lambda)=E,\end{array}\right.

(2.5.1)

which is equivalent to the integral equation

V(x,t)=E-\int_{x}^{\pi}e^{2B\lambda(s-\pi)}B\Omega(s)V(s)ds.

(2.5.2)

Lemma 2.6
.

If the matrix
R(x,t)
is a solution to the integral equation

R(x,t)=B\Omega(t)-\int_{\pi-(t-x)}^{\pi}B\Omega(t+s-\pi)R(t+s-\pi,s)ds,\;0\leq x\leq t\leq\pi,

(2.5.3)

i.e. (
2.5.3
) holds almost everywhere in
t\in[x,\pi]
, then the matrix-function
V(x,\lambda)
, defined by the formula

V(x,\lambda)=E-\int_{x}^{\pi}e^{2B\lambda(s-\pi)}R(x,s)ds

(2.5.4)

is a solution to the integral equation (
2.5.2
) or (which is the same) the Cauchy problem (
2.5.1
).

The integral equation (
2.5.3
) will be solved by successive approximations.
Let us construct a sequence of matrices:

R_{0}(x,t)=B\Omega(t)=q(t)\sigma_{2}-p(t)\sigma_{3},

(2.5.5)

R_{n}(x,t)=-\int_{\pi-(t-x)}^{\pi}B\Omega(t+s-\pi)R_{n-1}(t+s-\pi,s)ds,\quad n=1,2,\dots\quad.

(2.5.6)

Lemma 2.7
.

The matrices
R_{n}(x,t)
, defined by the formulas (
2.5.5
) and (
2.5.6
), have the following structure:

\quad R_{2n}(x,t)=\gamma_{2n}(x,t)\sigma_{2}+\delta_{2n}(x,t)\sigma_{3},\quad n=0,1,2,\dots

(2.5.7)

R_{2n+1}(x,t)=\gamma_{2n+1}(x,t)E+\delta_{2n+1}(x,t)B,\quad n=0,1,2,\dots\;,

(2.5.8)

where the scalar functions
\gamma_{k}(x,t)
and
\delta_{k}(x,t)
are determined from the recurrence relations:

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}(x,t)=

\displaystyle q(t),\quad\delta_{0}(x,t)=-p(t),

(2.5.9)

\displaystyle\gamma_{m}(x,t)=

\displaystyle\int_{\pi-(t-x)}^{\pi}q(t+s-\pi)\gamma_{m-1}(t+s-\pi,s)-(-1)^{m}p(t+s-\pi)\delta_{m-1}(t+s-\pi,s)ds,

(2.5.10)

\displaystyle\delta_{m}(x,t)=

\displaystyle\int_{\pi-(t-x)}^{\pi}q(t+s-\pi)\delta_{m-1}(t+s-\pi,s)-(-1)^{m}p(t+s-\pi)\gamma_{m-1}(t+s-\pi,s)ds.

(2.5.11)

Lemma 2.8
.

For
p,q\in L^{1}(0,\pi)
the following estimates hold:

\int_{x}^{\pi}\left(|\gamma_{k}(x,t)|+|\delta_{k}(x,t)|\right)dt\leq\frac{r^{(k+1)}(x)}{(k+1)!},\quad k=0,1,2,\ldots\;.

(2.5.12)

Since, according to (
2.5.7
), (
2.5.8
) and (
0.0.5
)

|R_{k}(x,t)|\leq|\gamma_{k}(x,t)|+|\delta_{k}(x,t)|,

(2.5.13)

for the series

R(x,t)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}R_{k}(x,t)

(2.5.14)

we obtain the estimate:

\int_{x}^{\pi}|R(x,t)|dt\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{x}^{\pi}|R_{k}(x,t)|dt\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{r^{k+1}(x)}{(k+1)!}=e^{r(x)}-1.

(2.5.15)

Lemma 2.9
.

Integral equation (
2.5.3
) ha a unique solution in
L^{1}(x,\pi)
.

Thus, the series (
2.5.14
) is the unique solution to the equation (
2.5.3
), and the elements
R_{ij}
of the matrix
R
are summable in
t\in(x,\pi)
functions, i.e.
R_{ij}(x,\cdot)\in L^{1}(x,\pi)
.

Let us denote

R_{+}(x,t)=\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\gamma_{2n}(x,t)\right)\sigma_{2}+\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\delta_{2n}(x,t)\right)\sigma_{3},

(2.5.16)

R_{-}(x,t)=\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\gamma_{2n+1}(x,t)\right)E+\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\delta_{2n+1}(x,t)\right)B.

(2.5.17)

According to (
2.5.7
), (
2.5.8
), (
2.5.14
), (
2.5.16
) and (
2.5.17
)

R(x,t)=R_{+}(x,t)+R_{-}(x,t),

(2.5.18)

where
R_{+}
anti-commutes, and
R_{-}
commutes with
B
, i.e.

R_{+}(x,t)B=-BR_{+}(x,t),\quad R_{-}(x,t)B=BR_{-}(x,t).

(2.5.19)

Lemma 2.10
.

The fundamental matrix
\Psi(x,\lambda)
is represented in the form

\Psi(x,\lambda)=e^{B\lambda(\pi-x)}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{x}^{2\pi-x}\left[R_{+}\left(x,\frac{x+t}{2}\right)-R_{-}\left(x,\pi+\frac{x-t}{2}\right)\right]e^{B\lambda(\pi-t)}dt.

The proof of this lemma follows from the fact that
\Psi(x,\lambda)=e^{B\lambda(\pi-x)}V(x,\lambda)
, and also from (
2.5.4
), (
2.5.18
) and (
2.5.19
).

Denoting now

H(x,t)=\frac{1}{2}\left[R_{+}\left(x,\frac{x+t}{2}\right)-R_{-}(x,\pi+\frac{x-t}{2})\right],

(2.5.20)

we obtain the representation (
2.1.17
).
The estimate (
2.1.18
) follows from (
2.5.16
), (
2.5.17
), (
2.5.18
) and estimates (
2.5.12
) and (
2.5.13
).
Note that for
m\geq 1
for the functions
\gamma_{m}(x,t)
and
\delta_{m}(x,t)
the identities
\gamma_{m}(x,x)\equiv\delta_{m}(x,x)\equiv 0
hold (see (
2.5.10
) and (
2.5.11
)), and
\gamma_{0}(x,x)=q(x)
,
\delta_{0}(x,x)=-p(x)
.
Taking into account (
2.5.19
), from (
2.5.20
) we obtain

\displaystyle H_{A}(x,x)B-BH_{A}(x,x)=

\displaystyle R_{+}(x,x)B=(q(x)\sigma_{2}-p(x)\sigma_{3})B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}p(x)+\sigma_{3}q(x)=\Omega(x),

i.e. formula (
2.1.19
) is proved.
Consider now

H(x,2\pi-x)=H_{A}(x,2\pi-x)+H_{C}(x,2\pi-x)=\dfrac{1}{2}\,R_{+}(x,\pi)-\dfrac{1}{2}\,R_{-}(x,x).

Since according to (
2.5.17
) and the fact that
\gamma_{m}(x,x)=\delta_{m}(x,x)=0
when
m\geqslant 1
,
H_{C}(x,2\pi-x)=-\dfrac{1}{2}\,R_{-}(x,x)\equiv 0
, then

H_{A}(x,2\pi-x)=\dfrac{1}{2}\,R_{+}(x,\pi)=\dfrac{1}{2}\,[q(\pi)\sigma_{2}-p(\pi)\sigma_{3}].

In the case of potentials for which
p(\pi)
and
q(\pi)
are uniquely determined (in particular, for smooth
p(\cdot)
and
q(\cdot)
) the assertions

H(x,2\pi-x)B+BH(x,2\pi-x)=0

and (
2.1.20
) are equivalent.
In our (general) case (
p,q\in L^{1}
) (
2.1.20
) is valid.

Since the vector-solution
\psi(x,\lambda,\beta)
is represented through the fundamental matrix
\Psi(x,\lambda)
in the form

\psi(x,\lambda,\beta)=\Psi(x,\lambda)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\beta\\
-\cos\beta\end{array}\right)
, then from (
2.1.17
), we have

\displaystyle\psi(x,\lambda,\beta)=

\displaystyle\psi_{0}(x,\lambda,\beta)-\int_{x}^{\pi}H(x,t)e^{B\lambda(\pi-t)}dt\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\beta\\
-\cos\beta\end{array}\right)-

(2.5.21)

\displaystyle--\int_{\pi}^{2\pi-x}H(x,t)e^{B\lambda(\pi-t)}dt\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\beta\\
-\cos\beta\end{array}\right)

In the integral
\int_{\pi}^{2\pi-x}
by changing the variable of integration
t_{1}=2\pi-t
we get:

\displaystyle\int_{x}^{2\pi-x}H(x,t)e^{B\lambda(\pi-t)}dt=-\int_{\pi}^{x}H(x,2\pi-t_{1})e^{B\lambda(t_{1}-\pi)}dt_{1}

(2.5.22)

\displaystyle=\int_{x}^{\pi}H(x,2\pi-t_{1})A_{\beta}A_{\beta}e^{-B\lambda(\pi-t_{1})}dt_{1}=\int_{x}^{\pi}H(x,2\pi-t)A_{\beta}e^{B\lambda(\pi-t)}dt\cdot A_{\beta},

where the orthogonal matrix
A_{\beta}=\sigma_{2}\cos 2\beta+\sigma_{3}\sin 2\beta
anti-commutes with the matrix
B
,
A_{\beta}^{2}=E
and

A_{\beta}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\beta\\
-\cos\beta\end{array}\right)=-\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\beta\\
-\cos\beta\end{array}\right)
.
Therefore, from (
2.5.21
) and (
2.5.22
), we get the representation (
2.1.21
) with the kernel
H_{\pi}(x,t,\beta)=H(x,t)-H(x,2\pi-t)A_{\beta}
.

From (
2.5.16
) and (
2.5.20
), we have

H_{\pi}(x,x,\beta)=\dfrac{1}{2}[R_{+}(x,x)-R_{-}(x,\pi)]-\dfrac{1}{2}[R_{+}(x,\pi)-R_{-}(x,x)]A_{\beta}.

Taking into account (
2.5.16
), (
2.5.17
), the form of the matrix
A_{\beta}
, and the fact that
R_{-}(x,x)\equiv 0
, we obtain that the part of
H_{\pi}(x,x,\pi)
, which anti-commutes with
B
is

H_{\pi,A}(x,x,\beta)=\dfrac{1}{2}R_{+}(x,x)=H_{A}(x,x)=q(x)\sigma_{2}-p(x)\sigma_{3}.

This yields (
2.1.22
).

To obtain estimates of the elements of kernels
H(x,t)
and
H_{\pi}(x,t,\beta)
for
p,q\in L^{2}[0,\pi]
, we introduce the function

g(x)=\left(\int_{x}^{\pi}|p(s)|^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\int_{x}^{\pi}|q(s)|^{2}ds\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
p,q\in L^{2}[0,\pi]
.
Then for the function
\gamma_{m}(x,t)
and
\delta_{m}(x,t)
, defined by the equalities (
2.5.10
) and (
2.5.11
), the estimates

|\gamma_{2k}(x,t)|\,,|\delta_{2k}(x,t)|\leq g^{2k+1}(x)\frac{(\pi-t)^{\frac{k-1}{2}}(t-x)^{\frac{k}{2}}}{(k-1)!\sqrt{k}}\quad k=1,2,\dots,

|\gamma_{2k+1}(x,t)|\,,|\delta_{2k+1}(x,t)|\leq g^{2k+2}(x)\frac{(\pi-t)^{\frac{k}{2}}(t-x)^{\frac{k}{2}}}{(k)!}\quad k=0,1,2,\dots.

hold.

Further, proceeding from (
2.5.20
), (
2.5.16
), (
2.5.17
) and the representations for the elements
H_{ij}(x,t)
(similarly to Lemma
2.5
), we obtain estimates for
|H_{ij}(x,t)|^{2}
, similar to the estimates (
2.4.3
), (
2.4.4
) and (
2.4.5
).
The assertions of part 3 of Theorem
2.4
are immediate consequences of these estimates.

2.6
The case
p,q\in W^{1,k}_{loc}
,
k\geq 1
.

If
p
and
q
have absolutely continuous derivatives up to the order
k-1
and derivatives of the
k
-th order belong to
L^{1}_{loc}
(i.e.,
p,q\in W^{1,k}_{loc}
) or
L^{2}_{loc}
(i.e.
p,q\in W^{2,k}_{loc}
), then it is easy to verify that the functions
\alpha_{n}(x,t)
and
\beta_{n}(x,t)
, constructed by the formulas (
2.2.3
)-(
2.2.5
), will have absolutely continuous derivatives with respect to
x
, up to the order
k
and absolutely continuous derivatives with respect to
t
, up to the order
k-1
.
Moreover, the derivative of the
k
-th order
\frac{\partial^{k}\alpha_{n}(x,t)}{\partial t^{k}}\in L^{1,2}_{loc}
, respectively.

Then the same properties will be possessed by the matrix
Q(x,t)
and, accordingly, the matrix
K(x,t)
, constructed by the formula (
2.2.14
).

2.7
Invertibility and weak boundedness of transformation operators

As it is noted in the inequalities (
2.4.3
)-(
2.4.5
) if
p,q\in L^{2}[0,\pi]
, then
\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}|K(x,t)|^{2}dx<\infty
and
\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}|K_{0}(x,t)|^{2}dx<\infty
.
As a consequence of the inequalities (
2.4.3
)-(
2.4.5
), we also have

\|K\|^{2}=\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}|K(x,t)|^{2}dtdx<\infty,\quad\|K_{0}\|^{2}=\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}|K_{0}(x,t)|^{2}dtdx<\infty.

It is known (see
[
Mar52
,
Mik59
,
AHM05
]
) that, in this case, the Volterra integral operator

\left[Wf\right](x)=\left[(E+\mathbb{K})f\right](x)=f(x)+\int_{-x}^{x}K(x,t)f(t)dt

(2.7.1)

and

\left[Vf\right](x)=\left[(E+\mathbb{K}_{0})f\right](x)=f(x)+\int_{0}^{x}K_{0}(x,t)f(t)dt

(2.7.2)

defined on
L^{2}([-\pi,\pi];\mathbb{C}^{2}]
and
L^{2}([0,\pi];\mathbb{C}^{2}]
respectively, are invertible, and their inverses are also Volterra integral operators.

Let us recall some notations and inequalities:
