\|h\|^{2}_{[0,x]}:=\int_{0}^{x}|h(t)|^{2}dt\leq\int_{0}^{\pi}|h(t)|^{2}dt=\|h\|^{2}.

Since
\int_{0}^{x}|p(t)|^{2}dt
,
\int_{0}^{x}|q(t)|^{2}dt
,
x
and
d(x)
are monotonically increasing functions for
x\in[0,\pi]
, then it follows from (
2.4.5
) that

\int_{-x}^{x}|K_{ij}(x,t)|^{2}dt\leq\int_{0}^{\pi}|p(t)|^{2}dt+2d^{4}(\pi)[\pi^{2}d^{2}(\pi)+\pi]e^{\pi d^{2}(\pi)}=M_{p}.

Similarly, from (
2.4.4
), it follows that

\int_{-x}^{x}|K_{ii}(x,t)|^{2}dt\leq M_{q}.

Let
M=\max\{M_{p},M_{q}\}
.
Since

|K_{0}(x,t)|^{2}\leq|K_{11}(x,t)|^{2}+|K_{12}(x,t)|^{2}+|K_{21}(x,t)|^{2}+|K_{22}(x,t)|^{2}

then, it follows that

\int_{0}^{\pi}|K_{0}(x,t)|^{2}dt\leq 4M\pi=M_{0}

From (
2.7.2
), we get

\displaystyle|\left[Vf\right](x)|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle 2|f(x)|^{2}+2\left|\int_{0}^{x}K_{0}(x,t)f(t)dt\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2|f(x)|^{2}+2\int_{0}^{x}|K_{0}(x,t)|^{2}dt\int_{0}^{x}|f(t)|^{2}dt

where in the first line we used well-known inequality
(|a|+|b|)^{2}\leq 2|a|^{2}+2|b|^{2}
.
And, therefore

\|Vf\|^{2}_{[0,x]}=\int_{0}^{x}|\left[Vf\right](s)|^{2}ds\leq 2\int_{0}^{x}|f(s)|^{2}ds+2M_{0}\int_{0}^{x}|f(s)|^{2}ds=(2+2M_{0})\|f\|^{2}_{[0,x]}.

The property expressed by this inequality is called the weak boundedness of the operator
V
.
Which directly implies the following property.

A.

If the sequence of functions
\{f_{k}\}
converges in
L^{2}[0,\pi]
to a function
f
, then the sequence
\{Vf_{k}\}
converges to
Vf
in
L^{2}[0,\pi]
.

In particular, since arbitrary function
f\in L^{2}([0,\pi];\mathbb{C}^{2})
can be represented in the form

f=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{a_{n}}\left(f,\varphi_{n}\right)\varphi_{n},

then, the operator
V
can be applied to the series component-wise

Vf=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{a_{n}}\left(f,\varphi_{n}\right)V\varphi_{n}.

Similar properties have the integral operators (
2.1.17
) and (
2.1.21
).

Notes and references

Transformation operators for the Sturm-Liouville equation were deeply studied in the work of V.A. Marchenko in 1952 (see
[
Mar52
]
).

In 1966, in work
[
GL66
]
, M.G. Gasymov and B.M. Levitan formulated a theorem on the existence of a transformation operator of the form

Vf=(E+\mathbb{K})f=f(x)+\int_{0}^{x}K(x,t)f(t)dt,

under the condition
p,q\in L^{1}_{loc}
.
There was also asserted the connection
K(x,x)B-BK(x,x)=\Omega(x)
under the condition of the absolute continuity of the potentials
p
and
q
, i.e.
p,q\in AC_{loc}
.
However, the details of the proof were not available.

In 1972, in monograph
[
Mar72
]
(and then in 1977 in monograph
[
Mar77
]
), Marchenko formulated a theorem on the existence of a transformation operator of the form

Wf=(E+\mathbb{K})f=f(x)+\int_{-x}^{x}K(x,t)f(t)dt,

under the condition of continuous
p
and
q
.

In 1977 F.E. Melik-Adamyan, in paper
[
MA77
]
, again formulated a theorem on the existence of a transformation operator under the condition
p,q\in L^{1}_{loc}
.
But again, not all the details of the proof were complete.
This suggests that the issue was technically tricky enough.
A detailed proof was published in the paper
[
Har08
]
.
Another proof was given in
[
AHM05
]
.
See also
[
LM21
]
and references therein.

CHAPTER
3
The Boundary value problem

3.1
Existence and asymptotics of eigavalues.

By
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
we will denote the boundary value problem

\displaystyle\ell y=\lambda y,

(3.1.1)

\displaystyle y_{1}(0)\cos\alpha+y_{2}(0)\sin\alpha=0,

(3.1.2)

\displaystyle y_{1}(\pi)\cos\beta+y_{2}(\pi)\sin\beta=0.

(3.1.3)

Definition 3.1
.

The values of the parameter
\lambda
for which the problem
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
has nontrivial solutions are called eigenvalues of that problem, and the corresponding solutions are called the eigenfunctions.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
p,q\in L_{\mathbb{C}}^{1}[0,\pi]
and
\alpha,\beta
are complex numbers (
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
).
Then problem
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
has countable set of eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}=\lambda_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, which form an unbounded sequence, and which has asymptotics

\lambda_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)=n+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}+h_{n},

(3.1.4)

where
h_{n}=o(1)
, when
n\to\pm\infty
, and also the estimate of remainder is uniform with respect to
p
and
q
from the bounded subsets of
L_{\mathbb{C}}^{1}[0,\pi]
and is uniform over all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
, which have bounded imaginary part.
If
p,q\in L_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}[0,\pi]
, then
\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}|h_{n}|^{2}<\infty
, and again uniformly with respect to
p
and
q
from the bounded subsets of
L_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}[0,\pi]
and above mentioned
\alpha
and
\beta
.

Proof.

By
y=\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
in Chapter
2
we denoted the solution of Cauchy problem (
2.1.13
).
It follows from initial conditions in (
2.1.13
) that
\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
satisfies the boundary conditions (
3.1.2
) for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
and to be the eigenfunction must satisfy to boundary condition (
3.1.3
).
Thus the eigenvalues are solutions to the equation (with respect to the unknown
\lambda
)

\chi(\lambda)\equiv\chi_{\alpha,\beta}(\lambda)\equiv\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda,\alpha)\cos\beta+\varphi_{2}(\pi,\lambda,\alpha)\sin\beta=0,

(3.1.5)

i.e. the eigenvalues are zeros of entire function
\chi(\lambda)
.

Since the solution
\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
represented by fundamental matrix
\Phi(x,\lambda)
by formula
\varphi\left(x,\lambda,\alpha\right)=\Phi\left(x,\lambda\right)\left({\sin\alpha},\,{-\cos\alpha}\right)^{T}
and

e^{-B\lambda x}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\sin\alpha}\\
{-\cos\alpha}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\sin(\lambda x+\alpha)}\\
{-\cos\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)}\end{array}\right)=\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda,\alpha)

(3.1.6)

(it is easy to see that
\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda,\alpha)
is the solution of Cauchy problem (
2.1.13
) for
p(x)\equiv q(x)\equiv 0
), then from (
2.1.9
) for
\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
we obtain the representation

\varphi\left(x,\lambda,\alpha\right)=\varphi_{0}\left(x,\lambda,\alpha\right)+\int_{-x}^{x}K\left(x,t\right)\varphi_{0}\left(t,\lambda,\alpha\right)dt.

(3.1.7)

Therefore the equation (
3.1.5
) will be rewritten in the form

\displaystyle\chi\left(\lambda\right)=

\displaystyle\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)+\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}K_{11}\left(\pi,t\right)\sin\left(\lambda t+\alpha\right)dt\cos\beta-

(3.1.8)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}K_{12}\left(\pi,t\right)\cos\left(\lambda t+\alpha\right)dt\cos\beta+\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}K_{21}\left(\pi,t\right)\sin\left(\lambda t+\alpha\right)dt\sin\beta-

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}K_{22}\left(\pi,t\right)\cos\left(\lambda t+\alpha\right)dt\sin\beta=\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)+g_{1}\left(\lambda\right)+

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle g_{2}\left(\lambda\right)+g_{3}\left(\lambda\right)+g_{4}\left(\lambda\right)=\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)+g\left(\lambda\right)=0,

where by
g(\lambda)
, we denote the sum of the last four integrals.
It is obvious that
g(\lambda)
is an entire function.

Since the eigenvalues are the solutions of (
3.1.8
), we must prove the existence of the solution of this equation.
Let us note that in the case of zero potential (
\Omega(x)\equiv 0
)
g(\lambda)\equiv 0
and the eigenvalues of the problem
L(0,0,\alpha,\beta)
are the solutions of the equation
\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)=0
, i.e.
\lambda_{n}\left(0,0,\alpha,\beta\right)=n+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
In general case, to prove the existence of the solutions of equation (
3.1.8
), we use Rouche theorem (see, e.g., Titchmarsch
[
Tit80
]
, page 125).

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
f
and
g
are analytic functions in the domain
D
and let
\Gamma
be a simple, closed, piecewise smooth curve, which, together with domain
G
, bounded by
\Gamma
, belong to
D
and the inequality

|f(z)|>|g(z)|

holds on all points of
\Gamma
.
Then in domain
G
the sum
f(z)+g(z)
has as many zeros as
f(z)
.

Let us denote
\lambda+\frac{\alpha-\beta}{\pi}=z
.
Then equation (
3.1.8
) can be written in the form

\sin\pi z+\tilde{g}\left(z\right)=0,

(3.1.9)

and if we prove that on contours
C_{n}=\left\{z;\,\,\left|z\right|=n+\frac{1}{2}\right\}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the inequalities
\left|\tilde{g}\left(z\right)\right|<\left|\sin\pi z\right|
hold, then, according to Rouche theorem, the number of roots of the equation (
3.1.9
) (which is the same as the number of eigenvalues) are equal to number of roots (zeros) of function
\sin\pi z
, i.e.
2n+1
.
To this end, we bring the formulation of the well-known lemma (let us note that it is the generalization of the famous Riemann-Lebesgue lemma to the complex case).

Lemma 3.1

(
[
Mar77
]
page 36)
.

For arbitrary function
f\in L^{1}\left(a,b\right)
,
\left(-\infty<a<b<\infty\right)
, the following equalities
\left(d=\max\left\{\left|a\right|,\left|b\right|\right\}\right)

\lim_{\left|\lambda\right|\to\infty}e^{-\left|\im\lambda\right|d}\int_{a}^{b}f\left(x\right)\cos\lambda x\,dx=\lim_{\left|\lambda\right|\to\infty}e^{-\left|\im\lambda\right|d}\int_{a}^{b}f\left(x\right)\sin\lambda x\,dx=0.

(3.1.10)

hold.

In what follows, the following lemma will be useful to us:

Lemma 3.2

(
[
PT87
]
)
.

If
|z-\pi n|\geq\frac{\pi}{4}
, for all integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then

\left|\sin z\right|>\frac{1}{4}e^{\left|Imz\right|}.

(3.1.11)

Let us now estimate the terms that make up the function
g(\lambda)
.
According to Lemma
3.1
, for arbitrary
\epsilon>0
there exists
A_{ij}>0
, such that for
|\lambda|>A_{ij}(i,j=1,2)
the inequalities

e^{-|\im\lambda|\pi}\left|\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}K_{ij}\left(\pi,t\right)\sin\lambda t\,dt\right|<\epsilon,\quad e^{-|\im\lambda|\pi}\left|\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}K_{ij}\left(\pi,t\right)\cos\lambda t\,dt\right|<\epsilon

hold.
Therefore

\displaystyle\left|\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}K_{ij}\left(\pi,t\right)\sin\lambda t\,dt\cos\alpha+\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}K_{ij}\left(\pi,t\right)\cos\lambda t\,dt\sin\alpha\right|\cdot\left|\cos\beta\right|

(3.1.12)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\epsilon\,e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\pi}\cdot e^{\left|\im\alpha\right|}+\epsilon e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\pi}\cdot e^{\left|\im\alpha\right|}\right)e^{\left|\im\beta\right|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\,\epsilon\,e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\pi+\left|\im\alpha\right|+\left|\im\beta\right|}

On the other hand, according to (
3.1.11
), if
\left|\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta-\pi n\right|\geq\dfrac{\pi}{4}
, then

\displaystyle\left|\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)\right|>

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}e^{\left|\im\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)\right|}=\frac{1}{4}e^{\left|\pi\im\lambda+\im\alpha-\im\beta\right|}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\pi-\left|\im\alpha\right|-\left|\im\beta\right|}.

Thus, in order the inequality

\left|g\left(\lambda\right)\right|<\left|\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)\right|

(3.1.13)

hold on
C_{n}
, and it is enough that the inequality

\displaystyle\left|g\left(\lambda\right)\right|\leq

\displaystyle 8\,\varepsilon\,e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\pi+\left|\im\alpha\right|+\left|\im\beta\right|}\leq\frac{1}{4}e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\pi-\left|\im\alpha\right|-\left|\im\beta\right|}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{4}e^{\left|\im\lambda\pi+\im\alpha-\im\beta\right|}<\left|\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)\right|,

hold, and this is true when
|\lambda|>A=\max\left\{A_{ij},\,i,j=1,2\right\}
and
\epsilon<\dfrac{1}{32}e^{-2\left|\im\alpha\right|-2\left|\im\beta\right|}
.

Therefore, for sufficiently large
n
on the contours
C_{n}=\left\{\lambda:\,\left|\lambda+\frac{\alpha-\beta}{\pi}\right|=n+\frac{1}{2}\right\}
the inequality (
3.1.13
) holds, whence it follows that the number of eigenvalues of the problem
L\left(p,q,\alpha,\beta\right)
inside the contour
C_{n}
coincides with the number of zeros of the function
\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)
, i.e. equal to
2n+1
.
Thus, the existence and countability of the eigenvalues of the problem
L\left(p,q,\alpha,\beta\right)
are proved.

Let us now consider the bound
P_{n,\frac{1}{4}}=\left\{z:\,\left|z-n\right|=\frac{1}{4}\right\}
of the circle centered at a point
z=n
with radius
\dfrac{1}{4}
.
Inequality (
3.1.11
) holds on
P_{n,\frac{1}{4}}
.
At the same time, the inequality
\left|\lambda\right|+\left|\dfrac{\alpha-\beta}{\pi}\right|\geq\left|\lambda+\dfrac{\alpha-\beta}{\pi}\right|=\left|z\right|\geq\left|n\right|-\frac{1}{4}
, i.e.
\left|\lambda\right|\geq\left|n\right|-\left|\dfrac{\alpha-\beta}{\pi}\right|-\dfrac{1}{4}
fulfilled on this boundary.

Therefore, for sufficiently large
n
(and, therefore, for sufficiently large
|\lambda|
), inequality (
3.1.13
) also holds.
Hence, according to Ruche’s theorem, in a circle bounded by
P_{n,\frac{1}{4}}
, the number of zeros of the functions
\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)
and
\chi\left(\lambda\right)=\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)+g\left(\lambda\right)
coincides, i.e. both are 1.
This means that the eigenvalues of the Dirac boundary value problem
L\left(p,q,\alpha,\beta\right)
of large modulus, all are simple and lie inside the circles
\left|\lambda+\frac{\alpha-\beta}{\pi}-n\right|<\frac{1}{4}
.

If we denote by
\lambda_{n}
the eigenvalue lying in these circles, we will get

\lambda_{n}=n+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}+h_{n},\qquad\mbox{where}\;\;\left|h_{n}\right|<\frac{1}{4}\quad(\mbox{for}\;\;n\geqslant n_{0})\,.

(3.1.14)

Let us prove that
h_{n}\to 0
, when
n\to\infty
.
Since

\chi\left(\lambda_{n}\right)=\sin\left(\pi\lambda_{n}+\alpha-\beta\right)+g\left(\lambda_{n}\right)=\sin\left(\pi n+\pi h_{n}\right)+g\left(\lambda_{n}\right)=\left(-1\right)^{n}\sin\pi h_{n}+g\left(\lambda_{n}\right)=0,

and from Lemma
3.1
, the representation (
3.1.8
) for
g(\lambda)
, and (
3.1.14
) it follows that
g\left(\lambda_{n}\right)\to 0
, we obtain that
\sin\pi h_{n}\to 0
, when
n\to\infty
.
The uniformity of estimate with respect to
p
and
q
from the bounded subsets of
L^{1}\left[0,\,\pi\right]
follows from the estimate (
2.1.10
).
Thus, Theorem
3.1
is proved for the case
p,q\in L^{1}[0,\pi]
.
∎

Let us now turn to the case
p,q\in L^{2}\left[0,\pi\right]
.
Since
L^{2}\left[0,\pi\right]\subset L^{1}\left[0,\pi\right]
, then the asymptotics (
3.1.4
) is valid, but in this case we would like to clarify the rate at which the remainder
h_{n}
tends to zero.
For this purpose, we use the following assertion, obtained by Levin and Ostrovsky in
[
LO80
]
(see also
[
AHM05
]
and
[
AI95
]
).

Lemma 3.3
.

Assume that
z_{n}=n+\xi_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
are the zeros of function

F(z)=\sin\pi z+\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f(t)e^{izt}dt,

(3.1.15)

where
f\in L^{p}(-\pi,\pi)
with
p\in(1,2]
.
Then the sequence
\{\xi_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
belogs to
l^{q}
with
q=\dfrac{p}{p-1}

It follows that if
f\in L^{2}(-\pi,\pi)
, then
\{\xi_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in l^{2}
, i.e.
\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}|\xi_{n}|^{2}<\infty
.
Since the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
are determined through zeros
z_{n}
of the equation (
3.1.9
) by the formula
z_{n}=\lambda_{n}+\dfrac{\alpha-\beta}{\pi}
, i.e.
\lambda_{n}=z_{n}+\dfrac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}
, then from
z_{n}=n+\xi_{n}
it follows
\lambda_{n}=n+\dfrac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}+\xi_{n}
.
Thus, to prove the asymptotics (
3.1.4
) in the case
p,q\in L^{2}(-\pi,\pi)
it suffices to prove that equation (
3.1.9
) in this case has the form (
3.1.15
) with
f
from
L^{2}(-\pi,\pi)
.

Lemma 3.4
.

If
p,q\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}\left[0,\pi\right]
, then the eigenvalues of problem
L\left(p,q,\alpha,\beta\right)
have the asymptotics
\lambda_{k}=k+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}+h_{k},
, where
\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\left|h_{k}\right|^{2}<\infty
and the estimate is uniform with respect to
p
and
q
from bounded subsets of
L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}\left[0,\,\pi\right]
and is uniform over all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
with bounded imaginary part.

Proof.

Let us note that

\displaystyle g_{1}\left(\lambda,\alpha,\beta\right)=

\displaystyle\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}K_{11}\left(\pi,t\right)\frac{e^{i\left(\lambda t+\alpha\right)}-e^{-i\left(\lambda t+\alpha\right)}}{2i}dt\cdot\cos\beta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}K_{11}\left(\pi,t\right)\frac{e^{i\alpha}}{2i}\cos\beta\cdot e^{i\lambda t}dt-\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}K_{11}\left(\pi,\,t\right)\frac{e^{-i\alpha}}{2i}\cos\beta\cdot e^{-i\lambda t}dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f_{11}\left(t\right)e^{i\lambda t}dt+\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f_{12}\left(t\right)e^{i\lambda t}dt=\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f_{1}\left(t\right)e^{i\lambda t}dt

where
f_{11}\left(t\right)=K_{11}\left(\pi,\,t\right)\dfrac{e^{i\alpha}}{2i}\cos\beta
,
f_{12}\left(t\right)=K_{11}\left(\pi,-t\right)\dfrac{e^{-i\alpha}}{2i}\cos\beta\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}\left[-\pi,\pi\right]
, and
f_{1}\left(t\right)=f_{11}\left(t\right)+f_{12}\left(t\right)
.
Similarly,
g_{k}\left(\lambda\right)=\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f_{k}\left(t\right)e^{i\lambda t}dt
, where
f_{k}\in L^{2}\left[-\pi,\pi\right]
,
k=2,3,4
, according to Theorem
2.6
.
Therefore,
g\left(\lambda\right)=\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f\left(t\right)e^{i\lambda t}dt
, where
f\in L^{2}\left[-\pi,\pi\right]
.

The uniformity of the estimates with respect to
p,q
from the bounded subsets of
L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}\left[0,\,\pi\right]
and
\alpha,\beta
from
\mathbb{C}
with bounded imaginary part follows from the estimates (
2.4.3
)-(
2.4.5
) and from (
3.1.4
).

Theorem
3.1
is completely proved.
∎

3.2
Asymptotics of eigenvalues in the ”smooth” case.

Let us now consider the case when
p,q\in W^{m}_{k}[0,\pi]
, i.e.
p
,
q
have absolutely continuous derivatives up to the the order
k-1
, and the derivatives of the
k
-th order belong to
L^{m}[0,\pi]
, where
m=1
or
m=2
(
k\geq 1
).

In this case, the entries
K_{ij}(x,t)
of kernel of the transformation operator (
2.1.9
) are also smooth, in particular
\frac{\partial^{l}}{\partial t^{l}}K_{ij}(\pi,\cdot)\in L^{m}(-\pi,\pi)
for
l=0,1,\ldots,k
.
Therefore the integrals
\int^{\pi}_{-\pi}K_{ij}(\pi,t)\sin(\lambda t+\alpha)\,dt
and
\int^{\pi}_{-\pi}K_{ij}(\pi,t)\cos(\lambda t+\alpha)\,dt
, in the formula (
3.1.8
), can be integrated by parts
k
times.
For example, for
p,q\in W^{2}_{2}[0,\pi]
, we get:

\displaystyle\int^{\pi}_{-\pi}K_{ij}(\pi,t)\sin(\lambda t+\alpha)\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\lambda}\int^{\pi}_{-\pi}K_{ij}(\pi,t)\,d\cos(\lambda t+\alpha)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\lambda}\left[K_{ij}(\pi,\pi)\cos(\lambda\pi+\alpha)-K_{ij}(\pi,-\pi)\cos(-\lambda\pi+\alpha)\right]+

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\lambda}\int^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{\partial K_{ij}(\pi,t)}{\partial t}\,\cos(\lambda t+\alpha)\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{c_{1}(\lambda)}{\lambda}+\int^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{\partial K_{ij}(\pi,t)}{\partial t}\,d\sin(\lambda t+\alpha)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{c_{1}(\lambda)}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\left[\frac{\partial K_{ij}(\pi,\pi)}{\partial t}\,\sin(\lambda\pi+\alpha)-\frac{\partial K_{ij}(\pi,-\pi)}{\partial t}\,\sin(-\lambda\pi+\alpha)\right]-

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\int^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{\partial^{2}K_{ij}(\pi,t)}{\partial t^{2}}\,\sin(\lambda t+\alpha)\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{c_{1}(\lambda)}{\lambda}+\frac{c_{2}(\lambda)}{\lambda^{2}}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\int^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{\partial^{2}K_{ij}(\pi,t)}{\partial t^{2}}\,\sin(\lambda t+\alpha)\,dt\,.

If
\frac{\partial^{2}K_{ij}(\pi,\cdot)}{\partial t^{2}}\in L^{2}[-\pi,\pi]
, then the last integral for
\lambda=\lambda_{n}=n+O(1)
is a quantity
c_{n}
, such that
\sum^{\infty}_{n=-\infty}|c_{n}|^{2}<\infty
.
In the general case, the right-hand side has the form

\frac{c_{1}(\lambda)}{\lambda}+\frac{c_{2}(\lambda)}{\lambda^{2}}+\frac{c_{3}(\lambda)}{\lambda^{3}}+\cdots+\frac{c_{k}(\lambda)}{\lambda^{k}}+\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{\lambda^{k}}\int^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{\partial^{k}K_{ij}(\pi,t)}{\partial t^{k}}\,\sin(\lambda t+\alpha)\,dt

(3.2.1)

if
k
is even.
If
k
is odd, then under the last integral we have
\cos(\lambda t+\alpha)
).

Hence, for solutions of the equation (
3.1.8
), i.e. for the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)=n+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}+h_{n}
we get, as above, the relation
\pi h_{n}\sim g(\lambda_{n})
, where
g(\lambda)
has the form (
3.2.1
).
Thus, the following theorem is true

Theorem 3.3
.

If
p,q\in W^{m}_{k}[0,\pi]

(k\geq 1)
, then for the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}
the following formula is valid

\lambda(p,q,\alpha,\beta)=n+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}+\frac{\alpha_{1}(\lambda_{n})}{n}+\cdots\frac{\alpha_{k}(\lambda_{n})}{n^{k}}+\frac{c_{k}(\lambda_{n})}{n^{k}}\;,

where
\alpha_{1}(\lambda_{1}),\ldots,\alpha_{k}(\lambda_{n})
are constants, uniformly bounded over all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
c_{k}(\lambda_{n})
has the property

\sum^{\infty}_{n=-\infty}|c_{k}(\lambda_{n})|^{2}<\infty,

for
m=2
, and for
m=1
about
c_{k}(\lambda_{n})
we can say that
c_{k}(\lambda_{n})=o(1)
when
n\to\pm\infty
.

3.3
Operators in Hilbert space.

It is often useful to consider the boundary value problems as problems of spectral theory of operators in Hilbert space.
So let us remind you of some concepts from the theory of operators in Hilbert space.
Let
H
is a Hilbert space, generated by scalar product
(f,g)
and norm
\|f\|=(f,f)^{1/2}
.

Definition 3.2
.

An operator
A
in
H
is called linear, if its domain of definition
\mathcal{D}_{A}
is a linear subspace of
H
and for any
x
and
y
from
\mathcal{D}_{A}
and arbitrary numbers
\alpha
and
\beta
from
\mathbb{C}
, the equality

A(\alpha x+\beta y)=\alpha A(x)+\beta A(y)

holds.

Definition 3.3
.

A linear operator
A
in
H
is called Hermitian, if for any
x
and
y
from
\mathcal{D}_{A}
the equality

(Ax,y)=(x,Ay)

holds.

Definition 3.4
.

A linear operator
A
in
H
is called symmetric, if it is Hermitian and its domain
\mathcal{D}_{A}
is everywhere dense in
H
, i.e.
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{A}}=H
(
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{A}}
is the closure of
\mathcal{D}_{A}
).

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{A}}=H
.
Consider the set

\mathcal{D}^{*}:=\left\{y\in H:\exists z\in H\text{ s.t. }(Ax,y)=(x,z),\,\forall x\in D_{A}\right\}

and define the operator
A^{*}
on the domain
\mathcal{D}^{*}
by the equality

A^{*}y=z,\qquad\forall y\in\mathcal{D}^{*}.

Operator
A^{*}
is called adjoint to
A
.

Lemma 3.5
.

The condition
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{A}}=H
ensures the correctness of the definition of the adjoint operator (i.e.,
z
is uniquely determined through
y
).

Proof.

Let
Ay=z_{1}
and
Ay=z_{2}
, i.e.
(Ax,y)=(x,z_{1})
and
(Ax,y)=(x,z_{2})
for all
x\in D_{A}
.
Hence
(x,z_{1}-z_{2})=0
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}_{A}
and since
\overline{\mathcal{D}_{A}}=H
, it follows that
z_{1}-z_{2}
is orthogonal to
H
and hence
z_{1}-z_{2}=0
.
∎

Definition 3.6
.

A linear operator
A
in
H
is called self-adjoint, if
A^{*}=A
, i.e
\mathcal{D}^{*}=\mathcal{D}_{A}
and
Ax=A^{*}x
,
\forall x\in\mathcal{D}_{A}
.

The self-adjoint Dirac operators.

By
\langle f,g\rangle=f_{1}\bar{g_{1}}+f_{2}\bar{g_{2}}
we denote the scalar product in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
In Hilbert space
L^{2}([0,\pi],\mathbb{C}^{2})
of two-component vector functions
f=(f_{1},f_{2})^{T}
, with a scalar product

(f,g)=\int_{0}^{\pi}\langle f(x),g(x)\rangle dx=\int_{0}^{\pi}\left[f_{1}(x)\bar{g}_{1}(x)+f_{2}(x)\bar{g}_{2}(x)\right]dx

(3.3.1)

we consider differential operators generated by differential expression

\ell=\sigma_{1}\dfrac{1}{i}\dfrac{d}{dx}+\sigma_{2}p(x)+\sigma_{3}q(x)=B\dfrac{d}{dx}+\Omega(x).

(3.3.2)

How should one choose the domain of the definition of the operator, generated by expression (
3.3.2
), in order for it to be self-adjoint?

At first let us denote by
\mathcal{D}
the following set

\mathcal{D}:=\left\{u=(u_{1},u_{2})^{T}:u_{1,2}\in AC[0,\pi],\,[\ell u]_{1,2}\in L^{2}([0,\pi])\right\}.

(3.3.3)

It is obvious that
\mathcal{D}
is the biggest domain of the definition, on which an operator can be defined (generated by expression (
3.3.2
), since it must act from
L^{2}([0,\pi],\mathbb{C}^{2})
to
L^{2}([0,\pi],\mathbb{C}^{2})
).

With the boundary value problem
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
(see (
3.1.1
)-(
3.1.3
)) we associate the domain
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}
:

\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}:=\left\{u\in\mathcal{D}:u_{1}(0)\cos\alpha+u_{2}(0)\sin\alpha=0,\,u_{1}(\pi)\cos\beta+u_{2}(\pi)\sin\beta=0\right\}.

(3.3.4)

Since the boundary conditions (
3.1.2
) and (
3.1.3
) are periodic by
\alpha
and
\beta
with period
\pi
it is sufficient to consider cases
\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
,
\beta\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
.

Theorem 3.4
.

Operator
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
, generated by differential expression (
3.3.2
) on the domain
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}
, is self-adjoint.

To prove this theorem, we also consider the domain
\mathcal{D}_{0}
:

\mathcal{D}_{0}:=\left\{u\in\mathcal{D}:u_{1,2}(0)=0,\,u_{1,2}(\pi)=0\right\}.

By
L_{0}
, we denote an operator generated by expression
\ell
on the domain
\mathcal{D}_{0}
.
It is clear that

\mathcal{D}_{0}\subset\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}\subset\mathcal{D}.

To prove that the operator
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
is sefl-adjoint it suffices to prove that the set
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}
is everywhere dense in
L^{2}([0,\pi],\mathbb{C}^{2})
and for the set

\mathcal{D}^{*}:=\left\{u\in L^{2}([0,\pi],\mathbb{C}^{2})=H:\exists z\in H\text{ s.t. }(\ell y,u)=(y,z),\,\forall y\in\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}\right\}

we have equality
\mathcal{D}^{*}=\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}
.
To prove this, we need to show that the equality

(\ell u,v)=(u,\ell v)

holds for every
u,v
from
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
p,q\in L_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}[0,\pi]
,
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then for arbitrary
u,v\in\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}
, the equality

(\ell u,v)=(u,\ell v)

hold.

Proof.

Since
\ell u=Bu^{\prime}+\Omega(x)u
, we have that

(\ell u,v)=(Bu^{\prime}+\Omega u,v)=(Bu^{\prime},v)+(\Omega u,v).

Since
p
and
q
are real-valued, i.e.
\bar{p}(x)=p(x)
and
\bar{q}(x)=q(x)
, then the matrix

\Omega\left(x\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}p(x)&q(x)\\
q(x)&-p(x)\end{array}\right)
is self-adjoint, and it follows that

(\Omega u,v)=(u,\Omega v).

For
(Bu^{\prime},v)
we have

\displaystyle(Bu^{\prime},v)=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi}\left\langle\left(\begin{matrix}u_{2}^{\prime}(x)\\
-u_{1}^{\prime}(x)\end{matrix}\right),\left(\begin{matrix}v_{1}(x)\\
v_{2}(x)\end{matrix}\right)\right\rangle dx=\int_{0}^{\pi}u_{2}^{\prime}(x)\bar{v}_{1}(x)-u_{1}^{\prime}(x)\bar{v}_{2}(x)dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi}\bar{v}_{1}(x)du_{2}(x)-\int_{0}^{\pi}\bar{v}_{2}(x)du_{1}(x)=\bar{v}_{1}(\pi)u_{2}(\pi)-\bar{v}_{1}(0)u_{2}(0)-

\displaystyle-\bar{v}_{2}(\pi)u_{1}(\pi)+\bar{v}_{2}(0)u_{1}(0)+\int_{0}^{\pi}u_{1}(x)\bar{v^{\prime}}_{2}(x)dx-\int_{0}^{\pi}u_{2}(x)\bar{v^{\prime}}_{1}(x)dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left.[u,v]\right|_{0}^{\pi}+\int_{0}^{\pi}\left\langle u,Bv^{\prime}\right\rangle dx=\left.[u,v]\right|_{0}^{\pi}+(u,Bv^{\prime}),

where by
\left.[u,v]\right|_{0}^{\pi}
we denote

\left.[u,v]\right|_{0}^{\pi}=\bar{v}_{1}(\pi)u_{2}(\pi)-\bar{v}_{1}(0)u_{2}(0)-\bar{v}_{2}(\pi)u_{1}(\pi)+\bar{v}_{2}(0)u_{1}(0)

It remains to show that

\left.[u,v]\right|_{0}^{\pi}=0.

Since
u_{1}(0)\cos\alpha+u_{2}(0)\sin\alpha=0
and
v_{1}(0)\cos\alpha+v_{2}(0)\sin\alpha=0
, then, in the case
\sin\alpha\neq 0
, we have
u_{2}(0)=u_{1}(0)\cot\alpha
and
v_{2}(0)=v_{1}(0)\cot\alpha
, so

\bar{v}_{2}(0)u_{1}(0)-\bar{v}_{1}(0)u_{2}(0)=\bar{v}_{1}(0)u_{1}(0)\cot\alpha-\bar{v}_{1}(0)u_{1}(0)\cot\alpha=0.

In the case
\sin\alpha=0
, we have
u_{1}(0)=0
and
v_{1}(0)=0
, so

\bar{v}_{2}(0)u_{1}(0)-\bar{v}_{1}(0)u_{2}(0)=0.

Similarly, we can show
\bar{v}_{1}(\pi)u_{2}(\pi)-\bar{v}_{2}(\pi)u_{1}(\pi)=0
.

Thus
(\ell u,v)=(Bu^{\prime},v)+(\Omega u,v)=(u,Bv^{\prime})+(u,\Omega v)=(u,\ell v)
.
This completes the proof of Lemma
3.6
.
∎

In order to prove that the set
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}
is everywhere dense it suffices to show that the set
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is everywhere dense in
L^{2}([0,\pi],\mathbb{C}^{2})
.
To this end, we prove several lemmas.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
f\in L^{2}([0,\pi],\mathbb{C}^{2})
.
In order the equation

\ell u=B\dfrac{du}{dx}+\Omega(x)u=f(x)

to have a solution from
\mathcal{D}_{0}
, it is necessary and sufficient for
f
to be orthogonal to all solutions of the homogeneous equation

\ell u=0.

Proof.

(Necessity).
Let us denote by
u=u(x)
the solution of Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}\ell u=f(x),\\
u(0)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u_{1}(0)\\
u_{2}(0)\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\end{array}\right)=0.\end{cases}

(3.3.5)

Such a solution exists and is unique.
Let
y(x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}(x)\\
y_{2}(x)\end{array}\right)
and
z(x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}z_{1}(x)\\
z_{2}(x)\end{array}\right)
be solutions of the following Cauchy problems, respectively:

\begin{cases}\ell y=0,\\
y(\pi)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
1\end{array}\right),\end{cases}\qquad\begin{cases}\ell z=0,\\
z(\pi)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
0\end{array}\right).\end{cases}

(3.3.6)

It is obvious that the solutions
y
and
z
are linearly independent and any solution of the equation
\ell u=0
can be represented in linear combination
u(x)=c_{1}y(x)+c_{2}z(x)
.
Calculating the scalar product
(f,y)
on
(0,\pi)
gives

(f,y)=(\ell u,y)=\left.[u,y]\right|_{0}^{\pi}+(u,\ell y).

Since
\ell y=0
, we get

\displaystyle(f,y)=

\displaystyle\left.[u,y]\right|_{0}^{\pi}=\int_{0}^{\pi}\left\langle Bu^{\prime},y\right\rangle dx=\int_{0}^{\pi}\left\langle\left(\begin{matrix}u_{2}^{\prime}(x)\\
-u_{1}^{\prime}(x)\end{matrix}\right),\left(\begin{matrix}y_{1}(x)\\
y_{2}(x)\end{matrix}\right)\right\rangle dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi}u_{2}^{\prime}(x)\bar{y}_{1}(x)-u_{1}^{\prime}(x)\bar{y}_{2}(x)dx=\int_{0}^{\pi}\bar{y}_{1}(x)du_{2}(x)-\int_{0}^{\pi}\bar{y}_{2}(x)du_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{y}_{1}(\pi)u_{2}(\pi)-\bar{y}_{1}(0)u_{2}(0)-\bar{y}_{2}(\pi)u_{1}(\pi)+\bar{y}_{2}(0)u_{1}(0)=-u_{1}(\pi).

Similarly

(f,z)=\bar{z}_{1}(\pi)u_{2}(\pi)-\bar{z}_{1}(0)u_{2}(0)-\bar{z}_{2}(\pi)u_{1}(\pi)+\bar{z}_{2}(0)u_{1}(0)=u_{2}(\pi).

Thus, if
u\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
then
u_{1}(\pi)=u_{2}(\pi)=0
and therefore
(f,y)=(f,z)=0
.
Since arbitrary solution of
\ell u=0
has the form
u(x)=c_{1}y(x)+c_{2}z(x)
, then

(f,u)=c_{1}(f,y)+c_{2}(f,z)=0,

i.e.
f
is orthogonal to all solutions of the equation
\ell u=0
.

(Sufficiency).
If
(f,y)=0
and
(f,z)=0
, then
u_{1}(\pi)=0
and
u_{2}(\pi)=0
.
Since
u(x)
is a solution to problem (
3.3.5
), it follows that
u\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
.
Lemma
3.7
is proved.
∎

Let us denote by
\mathit{m}
the set of all solutions of a homogeneous equation
\ell u=0
.
It is obvious that
\dim\mathit{m}=2
and that
\mathit{m}\subset L^{2}([0,\pi],\mathbb{C}^{2})
.
By
R_{0}
we denote the range of operator
L_{0}
, i.e. the set of vector-functions
f
from
L^{2}([0,\pi],\mathbb{C}^{2})
for which there exist
u\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
, such that
f=\ell u=L_{0}u
.

The assertion of Lemma
3.7
means that
f\in R_{0}
if and only if
f
is orthogonal to
\mathit{m}
, in other words

L^{2}([0,\pi],\mathbb{C}^{2})=R_{0}\oplus\mathit{m}.

(3.3.7)

Lemma 3.8
.

\mathcal{D}_{0}
is everywhere dense in
L^{2}([0,\pi],\mathbb{C}^{2})
.

Proof.

It is enough to prove that if
f\in L^{2}([0,\pi],\mathbb{C}^{2})
and
(f,u)=0,\forall u\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
, then
f=0
.
Denote by
v
the solution of equation
\ell v=f
.
Since
f\in L^{2}([0,\pi],\mathbb{C}^{2})
, then
v\in\mathcal{D}
, and for arbitrary
u\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
we have

(v,L_{0}u)=(Lv,u)=(\ell v,u)=(f,u)=0,

i.e.
v\in R_{0}^{\perp}
, and according to (
3.3.7
),
v\in\mathit{m}
, that is
\ell v=0
.
Since
f=\ell v

\Rightarrow

f=0
.
Lemma
3.8
is proved.
∎

3.4
Gradient of eigenvalue.

Here we assume that coefficients
p
and
q
are real and summable on
[0,\pi]
, i.e.
p,q\in L_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}\left[0,\pi\right]
, and parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
from boundary conditions are also real (as it was noted above, it is sufficient to consider the case
\alpha,\beta\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
).
The eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, depend on parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
and coefficients
p,q
, i.e.
\lambda_{n}=\lambda_{n}\left(\alpha,\beta,p,q\right)
.
To study in more details the dependence of eigenvalues on these arguments, we introduce the concept of the gradient of eigenvalues by the following formula

\grad\,\lambda_{n}=\left(\frac{\partial\lambda_{n}}{\partial\alpha},\,\frac{\partial\lambda_{n}}{\partial\beta},\,\frac{\partial\lambda_{n}}{\partial p\left(x\right)},\,\frac{\partial\lambda_{n}}{\partial q\left(x\right)}\right).

(3.4.1)

The first two components of this vector are usual (ordinary) derivatives by numerical arguments, and the last two are defined from the following definition (see, e.g.,
[
IT83
]
).

Definition 3.7
.

The derivative of function
f
with respect to function
q
is the function
\frac{\partial f}{\partial q\left(x\right)}
( function on
x
), which satisfy to equality

\left.\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}f\left(q+\epsilon v\right)\right|_{\epsilon=0}=\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{\partial f}{\partial q\left(x\right)}\cdot v\left(x\right)dx

for arbitrary
v\in L_{R}^{2}\left[0,\,\pi\right]
.

Let us denote by
h_{n}(x)=h_{n}(x,p,q,\alpha,\beta)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, normalized eigenfunctions of the problem
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
, corresponding to eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}=\lambda_{n}\left(p,q,\alpha,\beta\right)
.
Besides the solution
\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
of Cauchy problem (
2.1.13
) let us consider the solution
y=u\left(x,\lambda,\beta\right)
of the Cauchy problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\ell y=\lambda y}\\
{y\left(\pi\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\sin\beta}\\
{-\cos\beta}\end{array}\right).}\end{array}\right.

(3.4.2)

It is obvious, that both
\varphi_{n}\left(x\right)=\varphi\left(x,\lambda_{n},\alpha\right)
and
u_{n}\left(x\right)=u\left(x,\lambda_{n},\beta\right)
are the eigenfunctions of problem
L\left(p,q,\alpha,\beta\right)
.

By
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
we denote the square of their
L^{2}
-norms, i.e.

a_{n}=\int_{0}^{\pi}\left|\varphi_{n}\left(x\right)\right|^{2}dx,\quad b_{n}=\int_{0}^{\pi}\left|u_{n}\left(x\right)\right|^{2}dx.

(3.4.3)

Obviously as
h_{n}\left(x\right)
we can take both

h_{n}\left(x\right)=\frac{\varphi_{n}\left(x\right)}{\sqrt{a_{n}}},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\tilde{h}_{n}\left(x\right)=\frac{u_{n}\left(x\right)}{\sqrt{b_{n}}}.

(3.4.4)

From these definitions, it is easy to see, that
\left|h_{n}\left(0\right)\right|^{2}=\frac{1}{a_{n}}
and
\left|h_{n}\left(\pi\right)\right|^{2}=\frac{1}{b_{n}}
.
From the simplicity of eigenvalues, it follows, that
h_{n}
and
\tilde{h}_{n}
are linearly dependent, i.e.
h_{n}(x)=c_{n}\tilde{h}_{n}(x)
and, since they are normalized,
\left|c_{n}\right|=1
.
In what follows, depending on the context, we omit the notation of some of the arguments of functions.
For example, if we study the dependence of the eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}
on
\alpha
, then
\lambda_{n}\left(\alpha+\Delta\alpha\right)-\lambda_{n}\left(\alpha\right)
means
\lambda_{n}\left(\alpha+\Delta\alpha,\beta,p,q\right)-\lambda_{n}\left(\alpha,\beta,p,q\right)
.

The purpose of this section is to express the components of the gradient (
3.4.1
) through normalized eigenfunctions
h_{n}
of problem
L\left(p,q,\alpha,\beta\right)
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
h_{n}(x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}h_{n_{1}}(x)\\
h_{n_{2}}(x)\end{array}\right)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, are the normalized eigenfunctions of problem
L\left(p,q,\alpha,\beta\right)
.
Then the equalities

\displaystyle\cfrac{\partial\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta,p,q)}{\partial\alpha}=-|h_{n}(0)|^{2},

\displaystyle\cfrac{\partial\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta,p,q)}{\partial\beta}=|h_{n}(\pi)|^{2},

\displaystyle\cfrac{\partial\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta,p,q)}{\partial p(x)}=|h_{n_{1}}(x)|^{2}-|h_{n_{2}}(x)|^{2},

\displaystyle\cfrac{\partial\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta,p,q)}{\partial q(x)}=2h_{n_{1}}(x)\cdot h_{n_{2}}(x)

hold.

Proof.

Let us write down the fact that
h_{n}
is the eigenfunction of problem
L\left(p,q,\alpha,\beta\right)
and
\tilde{h}_{n}
is the eigenfunction of problem
L\left(p,q,\alpha+\Delta\alpha,\beta\right)
:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}Bh^{\prime}_{n}+\Omega\left(x\right)h_{n}\equiv\lambda_{n}\left(\alpha\right)h_{n},\\
h_{n1}\left(0\right)\cos\alpha+h_{n2}\left(0\right)\sin\alpha=0,\\
h_{n1}\left(\pi\right)\cos\beta+h_{n2}\left(\pi\right)\sin\beta=0.\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}B\tilde{h^{\prime}}_{n}+\Omega\left(x\right)\tilde{h}_{n}\equiv\lambda_{n}\left(\alpha+\Delta\alpha\right)\tilde{h}_{n},\\
\tilde{h}_{n1}\left(0\right)\cos\left(\alpha+\Delta\alpha\right)+\tilde{h}_{n2}\left(0\right)\sin\left(\alpha+\Delta\alpha\right)=0,\\
\tilde{h}_{n1}\left(\pi\right)\cos\beta+\tilde{h}_{n2}\left(\pi\right)\sin\beta=0.\end{array}\right.

Let’s multiply (
3.4
) scalarly from the right by function
\tilde{h}_{n}
and (
3.4
) from the left by
h_{n}
.
Taking into account the self-adjointness of the matrix
\Omega(x)
and that the eigenvalues are real, we get:

\displaystyle\left(Bh^{\prime}_{n},\tilde{h}_{n}\right)+\left(\Omega h_{n},\tilde{h}_{n}\right)=\lambda_{n}\left(\alpha\right)\left(h_{n},\tilde{h}_{n}\right),

\displaystyle\left(h_{n},B\tilde{h^{\prime}}_{n}\right)+\left(\Omega h_{n},\tilde{h}_{n}\right)=\lambda_{n}\left(\alpha+\Delta\alpha\right)\left(h_{n},\tilde{h}_{n}\right).

Subtracting the first equation from the second one, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi}\left\langle\begin{array}[]{cc}{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{h_{n1}}\\
{h_{n2}}\end{array}\right),}&{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\tilde{h}^{\prime}_{n2}}\\
{-\tilde{h}^{\prime}_{n1}}\end{array}\right)}\end{array}\right\rangle dx

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\pi}\left\langle\begin{array}[]{cc}{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{h^{\prime}_{n2}}\\
{-h^{\prime}_{n1}}\end{array}\right),}&{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\tilde{h}_{n1}}\\
{\tilde{h}_{n2}}\end{array}\right)}\end{array}\right\rangle dx

(3.4.11)

\displaystyle=\left[\lambda_{n}\left(\alpha+\Delta\alpha\right)-\lambda_{n}\left(\alpha\right)\right]\left(h_{n},\tilde{h}_{n}\right)

Considering that for real coefficients, the components of the solution can be taken real-valued, the integrand on the left-hand side takes the form

h_{n1}\cdot\tilde{h}^{\prime}_{n2}-h_{n2}\cdot\tilde{h}^{\prime}_{n1}-h^{\prime}_{n2}\cdot\tilde{h}_{n1}+h^{\prime}_{n1}\cdot\tilde{h}_{n2}=\frac{d}{dx}\left[h_{n1}\cdot\tilde{h}_{n2}-h_{n2}\cdot\tilde{h}_{n1}\right],

and the integral itself

h_{n1}\left(\pi\right)\cdot\tilde{h}_{n2}\left(\pi\right)-h_{n2}\left(\pi\right)\cdot\tilde{h}_{n1}\left(\pi\right)-h_{n1}\left(0\right)\cdot\tilde{h}_{n2}\left(0\right)+h_{n2}\left(0\right)\cdot\tilde{h}_{n1}\left(0\right).

Since

h_{n}\left(x\right)=\dfrac{\varphi_{n}\left(x,\alpha\right)}{\sqrt{a_{n}\left(\alpha\right)}},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\tilde{h}_{n}\left(x\right)=\dfrac{\varphi_{n}\left(x,\alpha+\Delta\alpha\right)}{\sqrt{a_{n}\left(\alpha+\Delta\alpha\right)}},

then

h_{n}\left(0\right)=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{a_{n}\left(\alpha\right)}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\sin\alpha}\\
{-\cos\alpha}\end{array}\right),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\tilde{h}_{n}\left(0\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a_{n}\left(\alpha+\Delta\alpha\right)}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\sin\left(\alpha+\Delta\alpha\right)}\\
{-\cos\left(\alpha+\Delta\alpha\right)}\end{array}\right).

Therefore, the expression (
3.4.11
) takes the form

-\frac{1}{\sqrt{a_{n}\left(\alpha\right)a_{n}\left(\alpha+\Delta\alpha\right)}}\sin\Delta\alpha=\left[\lambda_{n}\left(\alpha+\Delta\alpha\right)-\lambda_{n}\left(\alpha\right)\right]\left(h_{n},\tilde{h}_{n}\right),

whence, we get that

\frac{\partial\lambda_{n}}{\partial\alpha}=-\frac{1}{a_{n}}=-\left|h_{n}\left(0\right)\right|^{2}.

(3.4.12)

Quite similarly, we obtain the equality

\frac{\partial\lambda_{n}}{\partial\beta}=\frac{1}{b_{n}}=\left|h_{n}\left(\pi\right)\right|^{2}.

(3.4.13)

To obtain equality
\dfrac{\partial\lambda_{n}}{\partial p\left(x\right)}=\left|h_{n1}\left(x\right)\right|^{2}-\left|h_{n2}\left(x\right)\right|^{2}
, we write (
3.4
) in the form

Bh^{\prime}_{n}+\left(\sigma_{2}p\left(x\right)+\sigma_{3}q\left(x\right)\right)h_{n}=\lambda_{n}\left(p\right)\cdot h_{n},

(3.4.14)

and also

B\tilde{h^{\prime}}_{n}+\left(\sigma_{2}\left[p\left(x\right)+\varepsilon v\left(x\right)\right]+\sigma_{3}q\left(x\right)\right)\tilde{h}_{n}=\lambda_{n}\left(p+\varepsilon v\right)\tilde{h}_{n},

(3.4.15)

where by
\tilde{h}_{n}
we denote the eigenfunction of problem
L\left(p+\varepsilon v,q,\alpha,\beta\right)
.

Multiplying (
3.4.14
) scalarly from the right by function
\tilde{h}_{n}
, (
3.4.15
) from the left by
h_{n}
and subtracting from each other, and taking into account that
h_{n}
and
\tilde{h}_{n}
satisfy the same boundary conditions, we obtain

\left(h_{n},\sigma_{2}\left[p\left(x\right)+\varepsilon v\left(x\right)\right]\tilde{h}_{n}\right)-\left(\sigma_{2}p\left(x\right)h_{n},\tilde{h}_{n}\right)=\left[\lambda_{n}\left(p+\varepsilon v\right)-\lambda_{n}\left(p\right)\right]\left(h_{n},\tilde{h}_{n}\right)

whence, it follows that

\frac{\lambda_{n}\left(p+\varepsilon v\right)-\lambda_{n}\left(p\right)}{\varepsilon}=\int_{0}^{\pi}\left(h_{n1}\cdot\bar{\tilde{h}}_{n1}-h_{n2}\cdot\bar{\tilde{h}}_{n2}\right)v\left(x\right)dx.

Passing to the limit when
\epsilon\to 0
and taking into account that
\tilde{h}_{n}\to h_{n}
, when
\epsilon\to 0
, according to the Definition
3.7
, we obtain

\frac{\partial\lambda_{n}}{\partial p\left(x\right)}=\left|h_{n1}\left(x\right)\right|^{2}-\left|h_{n2}\left(x\right)\right|^{2}.

Quite similarly, we get

\frac{\partial\lambda_{n}}{\partial q\left(x\right)}=2h_{n1}\left(x\right)\cdot h_{n2}\left(x\right).

Theorem
3.5
is proved.
∎

3.5
Asymptotics of the norming constants.

Since the solution
\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
of Cauchy problem (
2.1.13
) satisfies the boundary condition (
3.1.2
) for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, and the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)
of the problem
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
are determined from equation (
3.1.5
), the functions
\varphi_{n}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}\varphi(x,\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta),\alpha)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, are eigenfunctions of the problem
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
.
The squares of
L^{2}
-norms of these eigenfunctions, i.e., the quantities

a_{n}=a_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}\int^{\pi}_{0}|\varphi_{n}(x)|^{2}dx=\int^{\pi}_{0}\left\{|\varphi_{n1}(x)|^{2}+|\varphi_{n2}(x)|^{2}\right\}dx

(3.5.1)

are called norming constants.
It follows from the representation (
3.1.7
) that

\displaystyle\varphi_{n1}(x)=\sin(\lambda_{n}x+\alpha)+\int^{x}_{-x}\left[K_{11}(x,t)\sin(\lambda_{n}t+\alpha)-K_{12}(x,t)\cos(\lambda_{n}t+\alpha)dt\right],

(3.5.2)

\displaystyle\varphi_{n2}(x)=-\cos(\lambda_{n}x+\alpha)+\int^{x}_{-x}\left[K_{21}(x,t)\sin(\lambda_{n}t+\alpha)-K_{22}(x,t)\cos(\lambda_{n}t+\alpha)dt\right].

(3.5.3)

According to Theorem
2.1
, for
p,q\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
,
K_{ij}(x,\cdot)\in L^{1}(-x,x)
, whence, according to the Riemann-Lebesgue theorem, it follows that the integrals on the right-hand sides of the last two formulas are quantities
o(1)
for
\lambda_{n}\to\pm\infty
(and according to the asymptotics of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}=n+\dfrac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}+o(1)
, this is equivalent to
n\to\pm\infty
).
Therefore

\displaystyle\varphi^{2}_{n1}(x)=\sin^{2}(\lambda_{n}x+\alpha)+r_{n1},

\displaystyle\varphi^{2}_{n2}(x)=\cos^{2}(\lambda_{n}x+\alpha)+r_{n2},

where
r_{ni}=o(1)
,
i=1,2
, for
n\to\pm\infty
.
Hence, for the norming constants
a_{n}=a_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
(note that we are considering the case of real
p
,
q
and real
\alpha,\beta\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
, for which the components
\varphi_{1}(x,\lambda,\alpha)
and
\varphi_{2}(x,\lambda,\alpha)
are real) we obtain the formulas

a_{n}=\pi+\kappa_{n}\,,

(3.5.4)

where
\kappa_{n}=o(1)
for
p,q\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
and
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\kappa^{2}_{n}<\infty
for
p,q\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
(these follow from Theorem
2.3
and formulas (
3.5.2
) and (
3.5.3
)).

If
p,q\in W^{m}_{k,{\mathbb{R}}}[0,\pi]
(
m=1
or
2
), then the integrals on the right-hand sides of the formulas (
3.5.2
) and (
3.5.3
) can be integrated by parts
k
times (see Section
2.5
) and obtain formulas

\varphi_{n1}(x)=\sin(\lambda_{n}x+\alpha)+\frac{g_{1}(x,\lambda_{n})}{\lambda_{n}}+\frac{g_{2}(x,\lambda_{n})}{\lambda^{2}_{n}}+\cdots+\frac{g_{k}(x,\lambda_{n})}{\lambda^{k}_{n}}+\frac{f_{k}(x,\lambda_{n})}{\lambda^{k}_{n}},

where

f_{k}(x,\lambda_{n})=(-1)^{k}\int^{x}_{-x}\left[\frac{\partial^{k}K_{11}(x,t)}{\partial t^{k}}\,\sin(\lambda_{n}t+\alpha)-\dfrac{\partial^{k}K_{12}(x,t)}{\partial t^{k}}\,\cos(\lambda_{n}t+\alpha)\right]dt

and, according to Section
2.5
,
\int^{\pi}_{-\pi}|f_{k}(x,\lambda_{n})|^{2}dx=c_{n,k}
, where
\sum^{\infty}_{n=-\infty}|c_{n,k}|^{2}<\infty
for
m=2
, and for
m=1

\;c_{n,k}=o(1)
, when
n\to\pm\infty
.

Similarly for
\varphi_{n,2}(x)
.
Hence, in the ”smooth case”, i.e. at
p,q\in W^{m}_{k,{\mathbb{R}}}[0,\pi]
(
k\geq 1
), the following assertion is true:

Theorem 3.6
.

The norming constants
a_{n}=a_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
of the problem
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
have the asymptotics

a_{n}=\pi+\frac{c_{1}}{n}+\cdots+\frac{c_{k}}{n^{k}}+\frac{c_{n,k}}{n^{k}},

where
c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{k}
are some constants, and

\sum^{\infty}_{n=-\infty}c^{2}_{nk}<\infty\qquad\text{ for }\;\;m=2\,.

In the case
p,q\in W^{1}_{k,{\mathbb{R}}}[0,\pi]
about
c_{n,k}
we can assert, that
c_{n,k}=o(1)
, when
n\to\pm\infty
.

3.6
Representation of norming constants by two spectra

The purpose of this subsection is to prove the following statement.

Theorem 3.7
.

For
p,q\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
, the norming constants
a_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)
are determined through two spectra
\left\{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)\right\}^{\infty}_{k=-\infty}
and
\left\{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)\right\}^{\infty}_{k=-\infty}
(where
\epsilon
is any number from
\left(\alpha,\,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
) by the formula

a_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)=\frac{\sin(\epsilon-\alpha)}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq n\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}\,,

where the infinite product is understood in the sense of the principal value, i.e.
\prod\limits^{\infty}_{-\infty}a_{k}=a_{0}\lim_{n\to\infty}\prod\limits^{n}_{k=1}a_{k}\cdot a_{-k}
.

To this end, we will consider a meromorphic function

m_{\alpha,\epsilon}(\lambda)=m(\lambda)=\frac{u_{1}(0,\lambda)\cos\alpha+u_{2}(0,\lambda)\sin\alpha}{u_{1}(0,\lambda)\cos\epsilon+u_{2}(0,\lambda)\sin\epsilon},

(3.6.1)

where
\alpha,\epsilon\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
and
\alpha\neq\epsilon
, and
u(x,\lambda)=u(x,\lambda,\beta,\Omega)
is the solution to the Cauchy problem (
3.4.2
).

Our plan for proof of Theorem
3.7
is as follows.
First, we prove (see below the equality (
3.6.15
)) that for
0<\alpha<\epsilon\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
the function
m(\lambda)
transforms the upper half-plane into the upper one, whence, according to the well-known theorem for such functions (see theorem
3.8
below), we obtain

m(\lambda)=c\,\frac{\lambda-\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}{\lambda-\lambda_{0}(\epsilon)}\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\right)\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)}\right)^{-1},

where
c>0
, and the infinite product is understood in the sense of the principal value, i.e.

m(\lambda)=c\,\frac{\lambda-\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}{\lambda-\lambda_{0}(\epsilon)}\prod^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)\cdot\lambda_{-k}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)\cdot\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\,\frac{\left(\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda\right)\left(\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)-\lambda\right)}{\left(\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)-\lambda\right)\left(\lambda_{-k}(\epsilon)-\lambda\right)}.

(3.6.2)

Second, we prove that the infinite product
\prod^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)\lambda_{-k}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}
converges (see Lemma
3.12
), and the infinite product

\prod^{\infty}_{k=1}\left|\frac{\left(\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu\right)\left(\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)-i\mu\right)}{\left(\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)-i\mu\right)\left(\lambda_{-k}(\epsilon)-i\mu\right)}\right|

converges uniformly in
\mu\geq 1
(see Lemma
3.11
).

Third, we prove (see Lemma
3.12
) that

\lim_{\mu\to\infty}m(i\mu)=e^{i(\epsilon-\alpha)}.

(3.6.3)

Passing to the limit in (
3.6.2
) for
\lambda=i\mu
and
\mu\to\infty
(see details below), we get the value of the constant
c
, more precisely, we get

c\prod^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)\lambda_{-k}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)\cdot\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}=1.

(3.6.4)

Fourth, we prove the equality

\left.\frac{\partial m}{\partial\lambda}\right|_{\lambda=\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}=\frac{a_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)}{\sin(\epsilon-\alpha)}\,,

(3.6.5)

and fifth, we prove the equality

\left.\frac{\partial m(\lambda)}{\partial\lambda}\right|_{\lambda=\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)}\prod^{\infty}_{k=-\infty}\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}.

(3.6.6)

The last two equalities imply the assertion of Theorem
3.7
.

Let us move on to the implementation of our plan.
In what follows, we often denote by ”dot” the derivative of
\lambda
, i.e.
\dot{u}(x,\lambda)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}u(x,\lambda)
or
\dot{u}{}^{\prime}(x,\lambda)=\frac{\partial^{2}u(x,\lambda)}{\partial x\,\partial\lambda}
.
Through
\bar{u}_{1,2}(x,\lambda)
we denote a complex conjugate function to

u_{1,2}(x,\lambda)
.

Lemma 3.9
.

The identities

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}\left[\dot{u}_{1}(x,\lambda)u_{2}(x,\lambda)-u_{1}(x,\lambda)\dot{u}_{2}(x,\lambda)\right]\equiv-\left[u^{2}_{1}(x,\lambda)+u^{2}_{2}(x,\lambda)\right]\,,

(3.6.7)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}\left[u_{2}(x,\lambda)\bar{u}_{1}(x,\lambda)-u_{1}(x,\lambda)\bar{u}_{2}(x,\lambda)\right]\equiv 2i{\im}\,\lambda\cdot\left|u(x,\lambda)\right|^{2}.

(3.6.8)

are hold.

Proof.

Let’s write the identity
\ell u\equiv\lambda u
component-wise in the form (for brevity, we omit the arguments
x
and
\lambda
):

\displaystyle u^{\prime}_{2}+p\cdot u_{1}+qu_{2}\equiv\lambda u_{1}

(3.6.9)

\displaystyle-u^{\prime}_{1}+q\cdot u_{1}-p\cdot u_{2}\equiv\lambda u_{2}\,,

(3.6.10)

and differentiate these identities by
\lambda
:

\displaystyle\dot{u}{}^{\prime}_{2}+p\cdot\dot{u}_{1}+q\cdot\dot{u}_{2}\equiv u_{1}+\lambda\dot{u}_{1}

(3.6.11)

\displaystyle-\dot{u}{}^{\prime}_{1}+q\cdot\dot{u}_{1}-p\cdot\dot{u}_{2}\equiv u_{2}+\lambda\dot{u}_{2}\,.

(3.6.12)

Multiplying both sides (
3.6.9
) by
\dot{u}_{1}
, (
3.6.10
) by
\dot{u}_{2}
, (
3.6.11
) by
u_{1}
, and (
3.6.12
) by
u_{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\quad u^{\prime}_{2}\cdot\dot{u}_{1}+p\cdot u_{1}\cdot\dot{u}_{1}+q\cdot u_{2}\cdot\dot{u}_{1}\equiv\lambda u_{1}\cdot\dot{u}_{1}

\displaystyle-u^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\dot{u}_{2}+q\cdot u_{1}\cdot\dot{u}_{2}-p\cdot u_{2}\cdot\dot{u}_{2}\equiv\lambda u_{2}\cdot\dot{u}_{2}\,,

\displaystyle\dot{u}{}^{\prime}_{2}\cdot u_{1}+p\cdot\dot{u}_{1}u_{1}+q\cdot\dot{u}_{2}\cdot u_{1}\equiv u^{2}_{1}+\lambda\dot{u}_{1}\cdot u_{1}

\displaystyle–\dot{u}{}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot u_{2}+q\cdot\dot{u}_{1}u_{2}-p\cdot\dot{u}_{2}\cdot u_{2}\equiv u^{2}_{2}+\lambda\dot{u}_{2}\cdot u_{2}\,.

Adding the first two identities and subtracting the last two, we get

\frac{d}{dx}\left(\dot{u}_{1}u_{2}-u_{1}\dot{u}_{2}\right)\equiv-\left[u^{2}_{1}+u^{2}_{2}\right],

i.e. (
3.6.7
) is proved.

To prove (
3.6.8
), the identity (
3.6.9
) multiply by
\bar{u}_{1}
, and (
3.6.10
) by
\bar{u}_{2}
:

\displaystyle\quad u^{\prime}_{2}\cdot\bar{u}_{1}+p\cdot|u_{1}|^{2}+q\cdot u_{2}\cdot\bar{u}_{1}\equiv\lambda|u_{1}|^{2}\,,

\displaystyle-u^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\bar{u}_{2}+q\cdot u_{1}\cdot\bar{u}_{2}-p\cdot|u_{2}|^{2}\equiv\lambda|u_{2}|^{2}\,.

Add to these identities their complex conjugates:

\displaystyle\quad\bar{u}{}^{\prime}_{2}\cdot u_{1}+p\cdot|u_{1}|^{2}+q\cdot\bar{u}_{2}\cdot u_{1}\equiv\bar{\lambda}\cdot|u_{1}|^{2}\,,

\displaystyle-\bar{u}{}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot u_{2}+q\cdot\bar{u}_{1}\cdot u_{2}-p\cdot|u_{2}|^{2}\equiv\bar{\lambda}\cdot|u_{2}|^{2}\,.

Adding the first two and subtracting the last two equals, we get:

\frac{d}{dx}\left[u_{2}\cdot\bar{u}_{1}-u_{1}\cdot\bar{u}_{2}\right]=2i{\im}\,\lambda\cdot\left[|u_{1}|^{2}+|u_{2}|^{2}\right]=2i\cdot{\im}\,\lambda\cdot|u|^{2}\,,

i.e. (
3.6.8
) is proved and thus Lemma
3.9
is proved.
∎

Now integrating the identity (
3.6.7
) on
x
from
0
to
\pi
(since
u_{1}(\pi,\lambda)=\sin\beta
,
u_{2}(\pi,\lambda)=-\cos\beta
holds for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, then
\dot{u}_{1}(\pi,\lambda)\equiv\dot{u}_{2}(\pi,\lambda)\equiv 0
), we obtain that

\dot{u}_{1}(0,\lambda)\cdot u_{2}(0,\lambda)-u_{1}(0,\lambda)\dot{u}_{2}(0,\lambda)\equiv\int^{\pi}_{0}\left[u^{2}_{1}(x,\lambda)+u^{2}_{2}(x,\lambda)\right]\,dx\,,

(3.6.13)

and also integrating the identity (
3.6.8
), we obtain

u_{1}(0,\lambda)\bar{u}_{2}(0,\lambda)-u_{2}(0,\lambda)\cdot\bar{u}_{1}(0,\lambda)=2i\int^{\pi}_{0}|u(x,\lambda)|^{2}dx\cdot{\im}\,\lambda\,.

(3.6.14)

It is not difficult to compute that

2i\im\,m(\lambda)=m(\lambda)-\bar{m}(\lambda)=\frac{\left[u_{1}(0,\lambda)\bar{u}_{2}(0,\lambda)-\bar{u}_{1}(0,\lambda)\cdot u_{2}(0,\lambda)\right]\cdot\sin(\epsilon-\alpha)}{\left|u_{1}(0,\lambda)\cos\epsilon+u_{2}(0,\lambda)\sin\epsilon\right|^{2}}.

Taking into account (
3.6.14
), from the latter we get that

\im\{m(\lambda)\}=\frac{\int^{\pi}_{0}|u(x,\lambda)|^{2}dx\cdot\sin(\epsilon-\alpha)}{\left|u_{1}(0,\lambda)\cos\epsilon+u_{2}(0,\lambda)\sin\epsilon\right|^{2}}\,\cdot\im\,\lambda\,,

(3.6.15)

this means that the ”real” (i.e.
\im\{m(\lambda)\}=0
at
\im\,\lambda=0
) meromorphic function
m(\lambda)
at
0<\epsilon-\alpha<\pi
transfer the upper half-plane to itself.
For such functions, the following is known (see
[
Lev56
]
, p. 398)

Theorem 3.8
.

For some real meromorphic function
m(\lambda)
to translate the upper half-plane to the upper half-plane, it is necessary and sufficient that this function is represented as

m(\lambda)=c\,\frac{\lambda-a_{0}}{\lambda-b_{0}}\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{a_{k}}\right)\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{b_{k}}\right)^{-1}\,,

(3.6.16)

where
c>
0 and

b_{k}<a_{k}<b_{k+1},\quad k\in\mathbb{Z},\quad a_{-1}<0<b_{1}\,.

(3.6.17)

For the (
3.6.1
) function,
a_{k}=\lambda_{k}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{k}(\alpha)
,
b_{k}=\lambda_{k}(\Omega,\epsilon,\beta)=\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Therefore, (so that the representation (
3.6.16
) and inequality (
3.6.17
) take place) we will take the following eigenvalues numbering:

First, we enumerate the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}\left(\Omega,\frac{\pi}{2},\beta\right)=\lambda_{n}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)
in ascending order of the index, and through
\lambda_{0}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)
we number the least non-positive eigenvalue, i.e.

\ldots\lambda_{-n}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)<\lambda_{-n+1}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)<\ldots<\lambda_{0}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\leq 0<\lambda_{1}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)<\ldots<\lambda_{n}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)<\ldots,

(3.6.18)

for
n=1,2,\ldots\,
.
To set the numbering for
\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)
, note that if in (
3.6.1
) we take
\epsilon=\frac{\pi}{2}
, i.e. in (
3.6.17
) we take
b_{k}=\lambda_{k}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)
, , we get that

\lambda_{n}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)<\lambda_{n}(\alpha)<\lambda_{n+1}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z},

and if we take
\alpha<\epsilon<\frac{\pi}{2}
, then from (
3.6.17
) (taking into account the previous inequality) we get that

\lambda_{k}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)<\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)<\lambda_{k}(\alpha)<\lambda_{k+1}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right),\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(3.6.19)

Inequalities (
3.6.18
) and (
3.6.19
) give an unambiguous numbering for eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}(\alpha)=\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, for all
\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
and fixed
\beta\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
.
This enumeration shows that each eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{k}(\alpha)
is a decreasing function of the parameter
\alpha
on the segment
\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
.

Thus, we got the representation (
3.6.2
).
Calculating the derivative of the function
m(\lambda)
, based on the formula (
3.6.1
) and applying the equality (
3.6.13
), we obtain that

\frac{\partial m(\lambda)}{\partial\lambda}=\frac{\int^{\pi}_{0}|u(x,\lambda)|^{2}dx\cdot\sin(\epsilon-\alpha)}{\left(u_{1}(0,\lambda)\cos\epsilon+u_{2}(0,\lambda)\sin\epsilon\right)^{2}}\;.

(3.6.20)

On the other hand, due to the simplicity of eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)
, the eigenfunctions
\varphi_{n}(x)=\varphi(x,\lambda_{n},\alpha)
and
u_{n}(x)=u(x,\lambda_{n},\beta)
are linearly dependent, i.e. there are constants
c_{n}=c_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
, such that

u_{n}(x)=c_{n}\varphi_{n}(x),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(3.6.21)

Hence, in particular, we have that at
\lambda=\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)

u_{1}(0,\lambda_{n})=c_{n}\varphi_{1}(0,\lambda_{n})=c_{n}\sin\alpha.

u_{2}(0,\lambda_{n})=c_{n}\varphi_{2}(0,\lambda_{n})=-c_{n}\cos\alpha.

Substituting these formulas in (
3.6.20
) (and taking into account the notation (
3.5.1
)), we get that for any
\epsilon\in\left(\alpha,\,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
:

\left.\frac{\partial m(\lambda)}{\partial\lambda}\right|_{\lambda=\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)}=\frac{a_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)}{\sin(\epsilon-\alpha)}\;,

(3.6.22)

i.e. we proved (
3.6.5
).

Now calculate the derivative
\left.\frac{\partial m}{\partial\lambda}\right|_{\lambda=\lambda_{n}}
, based on the formula (
3.6.2
).
For this (as we have already done) separating the factor

a_{n}=\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta)}\right)\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon,\beta)}\right)^{-1}=\frac{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon,\beta)}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta)}\,\cdot\,\frac{\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta)-\lambda}{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon,\beta)-\lambda}

from the finite product
\prod^{n}_{k=-n}a_{k}
, we write
m(\lambda)
in the form

m(\lambda)=\frac{\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta)-\lambda}{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon,\beta)-\lambda}\,\cdot{\mathcal{P}}(\lambda),

where
\mathcal{P}(\lambda)
is the whole rest of the product.
Hence, it follows that

\frac{\partial m}{\partial\lambda}=\frac{-\left(\lambda_{n}(\epsilon,\beta)-\lambda\right)+\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta)-\lambda}{\left(\lambda_{n}(\epsilon,\beta)-\lambda\right)^{2}}\,\cdot{\mathcal{P}}(\lambda)+\frac{\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta)-\lambda}{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon,\beta)-\lambda}\,\cdot\dot{\mathcal{P}}(\lambda).

For
\lambda=\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{n}(\alpha)
(here
\beta
is fixed) we obtain

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial m(\lambda)}{\partial\lambda}\right|_{\lambda=\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta)}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta)-\lambda_{0}(\epsilon,\beta)}\,\cdot\,{\mathcal{P}}(\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta))

(3.6.23)

\displaystyle=c\frac{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-\lambda_{0}(\epsilon)}\,\cdot\,\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)}\ \cdot\frac{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq n,k\neq 0,\end{subarray}}\frac{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}

if
n\neq 0
and

\left.\frac{\partial m(\lambda)}{\partial\lambda}\right|_{\lambda=\lambda_{0}(\alpha,\beta)}=c\,\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)-\lambda_{0}(\epsilon)}\,\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)-\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}\,.

(3.6.24)

From the formulas (
3.6.22
), (
3.6.23
) and (
3.6.24
) it follows that for expressing
a_{n}
through two spectra we have to find out the value of the constant
c
in the formula (
3.6.2
) (or in (
3.6.23
)).

Lemma 3.10
.

For
p,q\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
infinite product
\displaystyle\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}
, understood in the sense of the main value, i.e.
\displaystyle\prod^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)\cdot\lambda_{-k}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)\cdot\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}
, converges.

Proof.

From the asymptotic formula (
3.1.4
) it follows that

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}(\alpha)\cdot\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)=

\displaystyle\left(k+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}+h_{k}(\alpha)\right)\left(-k+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}+h_{-k}(\alpha)\right)

(3.6.25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}\right)^{2}-k^{2}+k\left(h_{-k}(\alpha)-h_{k}(\alpha)\right)+

\displaystyle+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}\left(h_{-k}(\alpha)-h_{k}(\alpha)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a^{2}-k^{2}+k\cdot b_{k}(\alpha)+ag_{k}(\alpha),

where we denoted
a=\frac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}
,
b_{k}(\alpha)=h_{-k}(\alpha)-h_{k}(\alpha)
,
g_{k}(\alpha)=h_{k}(\alpha)+h_{-k}(\alpha)
, and since
\sum^{\infty}_{k=-\infty}h^{2}_{k}(\alpha)<\infty
, then
\sum^{\infty}_{-\infty}b^{2}_{k},\sum^{\infty}_{-\infty}g^{2}_{k}<\infty
. If we denote
e=\frac{\beta-\epsilon}{\pi}
, then from (
3.6.25
) we will obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)\lambda_{-k}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{2}-k^{2}+kb_{k}(\epsilon)+eg_{k}(\epsilon)}{a^{2}-k^{2}+kb_{k}(\alpha)+eg_{k}(\alpha)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\frac{e^{2}-a^{2}+eg_{k}(\epsilon)-ag_{k}(\alpha)}{a^{2}-k^{2}+kb_{k}(\epsilon)+ag_{k}(\alpha)}+\frac{k\left(b_{k}(\epsilon)-b_{k}(\alpha)\right)}{a^{2}-k^{2}+kb_{k}(\epsilon)+ag_{k}(\alpha)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+r_{k}\,,

where
r_{k}
have the form
r_{k}=\frac{O(1)}{k^{2}}+\frac{\tilde{b}_{k}}{k}
, where
\sum^{\infty}_{-\infty}\tilde{b}^{2}_{k}<\infty
. Convergence of
\sum^{\infty}_{-\infty}\!{}^{{}^{\prime}}\,\frac{\tilde{b}_{k}}{k}
follows from the Cauchy-Bunyakovsky inequality

\left|\sum^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\tilde{b}_{k}}{k}\right|\leq\left(\sum^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\tilde{b}^{2}_{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{1}{k^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}<\infty.

Therefore, the series
\sum^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,r_{k}
converges.
But since
r_{k}=o\left(\frac{1}{k}\right)
,it follows that the series
\sum^{\infty}_{-\infty}r^{2}_{k}
also converges.
And the convergence of these series is a sufficient condition for convergence of the product
\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,(1+r_{k})
(see
[
Fic66
]
, paragraph 401). Lemma
3.10
is proved.
∎

Lemma 3.11
.

For
p,q\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
the infinite product

\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\left|\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)-i\mu}\right|=\prod^{\infty}_{k=1}\left|\frac{\left(\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu\right)\left(\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)-i\mu\right)}{\left(\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)-i\mu\right)\left(\lambda_{-k}(\epsilon)-i\mu\right)}\right|=\prod^{\infty}_{k=1}p_{k},

converges uniformly, where
\mu\geq 1
.

Proof.

Since the convergence of this product is equivalent to the convergence of the series
\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}\ln p_{k}
, and since

p_{k}=\left|\frac{\left(\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu\right)\left(\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)-i\mu\right)}{\left(\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)-i\mu\right)\left(\lambda_{-k}(\epsilon)-i\mu\right)}\right|=\left[\frac{\left(\lambda^{2}_{k}(\alpha)+\mu^{2}\right)\left(\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\alpha)+\mu^{2}\right)}{\left(\lambda^{2}_{k}(\epsilon)+\mu^{2}\right)\left(\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\epsilon)+\mu^{2}\right)}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}=q_{k}^{\frac{1}{2}},

then it is enough to prove the uniform convergence of the series
\frac{1}{2}\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}\ln q_{k}
.
To do this, note that

\displaystyle q_{k}=

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\lambda^{2}_{k}(\alpha)+\mu^{2}\right)\left(\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\alpha)+\mu^{2}\right)}{\left(\lambda^{2}_{k}(\epsilon)+\mu^{2}\right)\left(\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\epsilon)+\mu^{2}\right)}

(3.6.26)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1+\frac{\lambda^{2}_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda^{2}_{k}(\epsilon)}{\lambda^{2}_{k}(\epsilon)+\mu^{2}}\right)\left(1+\frac{\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\alpha)-\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\epsilon)}{\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\epsilon)+\mu^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\frac{\lambda^{2}_{k}(\alpha)\cdot\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\alpha)-\lambda^{2}_{k}(\epsilon)\cdot\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\epsilon)+\mu^{2}\left[\lambda^{2}_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda^{2}_{k}(\epsilon)+\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\alpha)-\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\epsilon)\right]}{\left(\lambda^{2}_{k}(\epsilon)+\ mu^{2}\right)\left(\lambda^{2}_{k}(\alpha)+\mu^{2}\right)}\,.

Based on the formula (
3.6.25
), we have:

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}_{k}(\alpha)\cdot\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\alpha)-

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}_{k}(\epsilon)\cdot\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(a^{2}-k^{2}+kb_{k}(\alpha)+eg_{k}(\alpha)\right)^{2}-\left(e^{2}-k^{2}+kb_{k}(\epsilon)+eg_{k}(\epsilon)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[a^{2}-e^{2}+k\left(b_{k}(\alpha)-b_{k}(\epsilon)\right)+ag_{k}(\alpha)-eg_{k}(\epsilon)\right]\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\left[a^{2}+e^{2}-2k^{2}+k\left(b_{k}(\alpha)-b_{k}(\epsilon)\right)+ag_{k}(\alpha)+eg_{k}(\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{k}\cdot k^{3}+(c+c_{k})\cdot k^{2}+d_{k}\cdot k+\tilde{c}+e_{k}\,,

where
c
,
\tilde{c}
are some constant, and the sequences
\{a_{k}\}^{\infty}_{1}
,
\{c_{k}\}^{\infty}_{1}
,
\{d_{k}\}^{\infty}_{1}
,
\{e_{k}\}^{\infty}_{1}
all are from
\ell^{2}
, i.e.
\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}|a_{k}|^{2}<\infty
, etc.

Referring to the expression
\lambda^{2}_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda^{2}_{k}(\epsilon)+\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\alpha)-\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\epsilon)
, noting that

\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)=a-e+g_{k}(\alpha,\epsilon)
,
\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)=2k-(a-e)+b_{k}(\alpha,\epsilon)
,

\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)-\lambda_{-k}(\epsilon)=a-e+g_{-k}(\alpha,\epsilon)
, and
\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)-\lambda_{-k}(\epsilon)=-2k-(a+e)+b_{-k}(\alpha,\epsilon)
, where
g_{\pm k}
,
b_{\pm k}
small quantities, after careful calculations we get that

\lambda^{2}_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda^{2}_{k}(\epsilon)+\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\alpha)-\lambda^{2}_{-k}(\epsilon)=k\cdot r_{k}+\kappa_{k}\,,

where
\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}r^{2}_{k}<\infty
,
\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}\kappa^{2}_{k}<\infty
.
Therefore, the formula (
3.6.26
) takes the form
q_{k}=1+\nu_{k}
, where

\nu_{k}(\mu)=\frac{a_{k}\cdot k^{3}+(c+c_{k})k^{2}+d_{k}k+\tilde{c}+e_{k}+\mu^{2}(k\cdot r_{k}+\kappa_{k})}{\left[\left(k-\frac{\epsilon}{\pi}+h_{k}(\epsilon)\right)^{2}+\mu^{2}\right]\left[\left(-k-\frac{\epsilon}{\pi}+h_{-k}(\epsilon)\right)^{2}+\mu^{2}\right]}.

Since for
\mu\geq 1
,
k^{2}+\mu^{2}>k^{2}
,
\frac{\mu^{2}}{a^{2}+\mu^{2}}<1
, then dividing the last fraction term-wise (
\nu_{k}(\mu)
), we get that

\left|\nu_{k}(\mu)\right|\leq\frac{a_{k}+r_{k}}{k}+O\left(\frac{1}{k^{2}}\right),

i.e.
\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}\left|\nu_{k}(\mu)\right|<\infty
uniformly on all
\mu\geq 1
. Lemma
3.11
is proved.
∎

Lemma 3.12
.

For
p,q\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]

\;\lim_{\mu\to\infty}m(i\mu)=e^{i(\epsilon-\alpha)}
.

Proof.

From the formulas (
2.1.21
), it follows that

u_{1}(0,\lambda)=\sin(\beta-\lambda\pi)-\int^{\pi}_{0}\left\{H_{11}(0,t)\sin(\beta-\lambda(\pi-t))-H_{12}(0,t)\cos(\beta-\lambda(\pi-t))\right\}\,dt,

u_{2}(0,\lambda)=\cos(\beta-\lambda\pi)-\int^{\pi}_{0}\left\{H_{21}(0,t)\sin(\beta-\lambda(\pi-t))-H_{22}(0,t)\cos(\beta-\lambda(\pi-t))\right\}\,dt.

And according to the Lemma
3.1
, we get

\lim_{\mu\to\infty}e^{-\pi\mu}u_{1}(0,i\mu)=\lim_{\mu\to\infty}e^{-\pi\mu}\left(\frac{e^{i(\beta-i\mu\pi)}-e^{-i(\beta-i\mu\pi)}}{2i}\right)=\frac{e^{i\beta}}{2i}.

Similarly

\lim_{\mu\to\infty}e^{-\pi\mu}u_{2}(0,i\mu)=-\frac{e^{i\beta}}{2}.

Proceeding now from the definition (
3.6.1
), we get that

\displaystyle\lim_{\mu\to\infty}m(i\mu)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\mu\to\infty}\frac{\frac{u_{1}(0,i\mu)}{u_{2}(0,i\mu)}\,\cos\alpha+\sin\alpha}{\frac{u_{1}(0,i\mu)}{u_{2}(0,i\mu)}\,\cos\epsilon+\sin\epsilon}=\frac{i\cos\alpha+\sin\alpha}{i\cos\epsilon+\sin\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\frac{\cos\alpha-i\sin\alpha}{\cos\epsilon-i\sin\epsilon}=\frac{e^{-i\alpha}}{e^{-i\epsilon}}=e^{i(\epsilon-\alpha)},

i.e., we proved the Lemma
3.12
, thus the equality (
3.6.3
) is proved and, in particular,

\lim_{\mu\to\infty}\left|m(i\mu)\right|=1,\qquad\lim_{\mu\to\infty}\arg\{m(i\mu)\}=\epsilon-\alpha.

∎

Write the formula (
3.6.2
) at
\lambda=i\mu

m(i\mu)=c\,\frac{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{0}(\epsilon)-i\mu}\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)\cdot\lambda_{-k}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)\cdot\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)-i\mu}\;\frac{\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{-k}(\epsilon)-i\mu}

and take the logarithm (the principal value of the logarithm) from both sides:

\displaystyle\ln\{m(i\mu)\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ln\left|m(i\mu)\right|+i\arg m(i\mu)

(3.6.27)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln\,\left|c\,\frac{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{0}(\epsilon)-i\mu}\right|+i\arg\left(c\,\frac{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{0}(\epsilon)-i\mu}\right)+\sum^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\ln\left|\frac{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}\right|+

\displaystyle+\sum^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\ln\left|\frac{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)-i\mu}\right|+\sum^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,i\arg\left(\frac{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\,\frac{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)-i\mu}\right)\,.

Since
c>0
and
\frac{\lambda_{k}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}>0
for
k\neq 0
, then these factors under the argument sign can be discarded.
Note also that

\arg\left\{\frac{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\,\frac{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)-i\mu}\right\}=\arg\left\{\frac{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)-i\mu}\right\}

is equal to the angle at which the segment of the real axis
\left[\lambda_{k}(\epsilon),\lambda_{k}(\alpha)\right]
from the point
i\mu
is visible.
Therefore, the sequence
\varphi_{n}(\mu)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}\sum^{n}_{-n}\arg\left\{\frac{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)-i\mu}\right\}
is a positive, monotone increasing and bounded (
\varphi_{n}(\mu)<\pi
uniformly for all
\mu>0
) sequence, i.e. it converges.
Taking all this into account and separating the real and imaginary parts in (
3.6.27
), we get two equalities:

\ln\left|m(i\mu)\right|=\ln\,\left\{c\,\left|\frac{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{0}(\epsilon)-i\mu}\right|\right\}+\sum^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\ln\frac{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}+\sum^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\ln\left|\frac{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)-i\mu}\right|

(3.6.28)

and

\arg\{m(i\mu)\}=\sum^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\arg\frac{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)-i\mu}\,.

Based on the fact that
\prod^{\infty}_{-\infty}\frac{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)-i\mu}
uniformly converges (for
\mu\geq 1
) and that

\lim_{\mu\to\infty}\left|m(i\mu)\right|=1,\qquad\lim_{\mu\to\infty}\arg\{m(i\mu)\}=\epsilon-\alpha\,,

and passing to the limit in (
3.6.28
) for
\mu\to\infty
, we obtain

0=\ln c+\sum^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\ln\frac{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}=\ln\left(c\,\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}\right)\,,

i.e.

c\,\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\lambda_{n}(\epsilon)}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}=1\,.

i.e. equality (
3.6.4
) is proved.
Substituting this value
c
into the formula (
3.6.24
), we get (
3.6.6
).
The Theorem
3.7
is proved.

Notes and references

The boundary value problem (BVP) was first investigated by G.D Birkhoff and R.E. Langer in
[
BL23
]
.
They obtained the asymptotic behavior of eigenvalues and eigenfunctions.
Asymptotic formula

\lambda_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)=n+\cfrac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}+r_{n}

was given in
[
GD75
]
.

The gradient of eigenvalues was investigated for Sturm-Liouville BVP in
[
IT83
]
.
For the Dirac system, it was given in
[
HA06
,
AH17a
]
.
The representation of norming constants by two spectra was given in
[
GD75
,
Har04
,
HA06
]
.

CHAPTER
4
Eigenfunction expansion theorems

4.1
Statements of Theorems.

In the Hilbert space
L^{2}\left([0,\pi],\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)
of two-component vector functions
f=\left(f_{1},f_{2}\right)^{T}
we introduce the pseudoscalar product

\left(f,g\right)=\int_{0}^{\pi}g^{T}\left(\xi\right)f\left(\xi\right)d\xi=\int_{0}^{\pi}\left[f_{1}\left(\xi\right)g_{1}\left(\xi\right)+f_{2}\left(\xi\right)g_{2}\left(\xi\right)\right]d\xi

and devote by
h_{n}\left(x\right)=h_{n}\left(x,p,q,\alpha,\beta\right)
”pseudonormalized” eigenfunctions, determined by the formula

h_{n}\left(x\right)=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\left(\varphi_{n},\varphi_{n}\right)}}\cdot\varphi_{n}(x),\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z}

Bellow we will show (see (
4.2.11
)) that the condition on the eigenvalues to be simple ensures that the quantity
\left(\varphi_{n},\varphi_{n}\right)
differs from zero.
The square root
\sqrt{\left(\varphi_{n},\varphi_{n}\right)}
is taken in main (principal) sense, i.e.
\re\sqrt{\left(\varphi_{n},\varphi_{n}\right)}\geq 0
.
The purpose of this section is to prove Theorems
4.1
,
4.2
,
4.3
and
4.4
.

Theorem 4.1
.

If all the eigenvalues of the problem
L\left(p,q,\alpha,\beta\right)
are simple, then an arbitrary absolutely continuous vector-function
f
(
f_{k}\in AC\left[0,\pi\right]
,
k=1,2
), satisfying the boundary condition (
3.1.2
), (
3.1.3
) can be expanded in a series

f\left(x\right)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{k}\cdot h_{k}\left(x\right)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(f,\,h_{k}\right)\cdot h_{k}\left(x\right)

(4.1.1)

in terms of the eigenfunctions of the problem
L\left(p,q,\alpha,\beta\right)
, which converges to
f
uniformly on
[0,\pi]
, that is

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,m>N\\
N\to\infty\end{subarray}}\sup_{x\in[0,\pi]}\left|f\left(x\right)-\sum_{k=-n}^{m}\left(f,\,h_{k}\right)h_{k}\left(x\right)\right|=0,

where
|f|=\sqrt{|f_{1}|^{2}+|f_{2}|^{2}}
.

We will also prove a theorem on uniform equiconvergence.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
f\in L^{2}\left(\left[0,\pi\right],\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)
.
Then, if all the eigenvalues of the problem
L\left(p,q,\alpha,\beta\right)
are simple, the following equality

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,m>N\\
N\to\infty\end{subarray}}\sup_{x\in[0,\pi]}\left|\sum_{k=-m}^{n}\left(f,\,h_{k}\right)h_{k}\left(x\right)-\sum_{k=-m}^{n}\left(f,h_{k}^{0}\right)h_{k}^{0}\left(x\right)\right|=0

holds, where
h_{k}^{0}\left(x\right)
are ”pseudonormalized” eigenfunctions of the problem
L\left(0,0,\alpha,\beta\right)
.

Roughly speaking, Theorem
4.2
says that adding a potential
\Omega\left(x\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}p(x)&q(x)\\
q(x)&-p(x)\end{array}\right)
to the expression
B\dfrac{d}{dx}
does not change the type of the convergence of the expansion in terms of eigenfunctions of the operator, generated by the expression
l_{0}=B\dfrac{d}{dx}
.
For example, if
f
does not satisfy the boundary conditions (
3.1.2
) and (
3.1.3
), then there may be no uniform convergence in both cases, but the difference (indicated in Theorem
4.2
) converges uniformly.

If
p,q\in L_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}\left[0,\pi\right]
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, then the problem
L\left(p,q,\alpha,\beta\right)
corresponds to the selfadjoint operator (see Section
3.3
).
It is known that the eigenvalues of this operator are all real, simple (see detail in (
4.2.11
)), and also the components of the eigenfunctions can be chosen real.
In particular, in this case
\left\{h_{n}\left(x\right)\right\}_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}
is an orthonormal system.
Therefore, Theorem
4.2
implies

Theorem 4.3
.

If
p,q\in L_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}\left[0,\pi\right]
,
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
then arbitrary vector-function
f
from
D(L)
can be expanded into a uniformly convergent series (
4.1.1
) in terms of the normalized eigenfunctions of operator
L
.

Using the fact that
D(L)
is everywhere dense in
L^{2}\left(\left[0,\pi\right],\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)
, we obtain the following theorem.

Theorem 4.4
.

If
p,q\in L_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}\left[0,\pi\right]
,
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, then for arbitrary vector-function
f
from
L^{2}\left(\left[0,\pi\right],\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)
the series (
4.1.1
) converges to
f
with respect to
L^{2}\left(\left[0,\pi\right],\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)
norm, i.e.

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,m>N\\
N\to\infty\end{subarray}}\left\|f-\sum_{k=-m}^{n}\left(f,\,h_{k}\right)h_{k}\right\|=0

and, besides, Parseval’s equality

\left\|f\right\|^{2}=\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\left|\left(f,h_{k}\right)\right|^{2},\quad\left(\left\|f\right\|^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}\int_{0}^{\pi}\left(\left|f_{1}\left(x\right)\right|^{2}+\left|f_{2}\left(x\right)\right|^{2}\right)dx\right).

holds.

For the first time, a theorem on expansion in terms of an eigenfunction of a regular operator of Dirac type was formulated and proved by Titchmarsh in
[
Tit44
]
in the following form:

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
p,q,r\in C_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}\left[0,\pi\right]
,
f\in L_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}\left[0,\pi\right]
and have bounded variation in the neighborhood of the point
x
.
Let, in addition, all the eigenvalues of the boundary value problem for the system of equations

\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{0}&{1}\\
{-1}&{0}\end{array}\right)\frac{d}{dx}+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{p\left(x\right)}&{q\left(x\right)}\\
{q\left(x\right)}&{r\left(x\right)}\end{array}\right)\right\}y=\lambda y,

(4.1.2)

with boundary conditions (
3.1.2
) and (
3.1.3
), where
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
are simple.
Then the series (
4.1.1
) at the point
x
converges to the value
\dfrac{1}{2}\left(f\left(x+0\right)+f\left(x-0\right)\right)
.

Later (see
[
LS88
]
), it was proved:

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
p,r\in C_{\mathbb{R}}\left[0,\pi\right]
,
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, and
h_{n}
are the normalized eigenfunctions of the boundary value problem for the system (
4.1.2
), where
q\left(x\right)\equiv 0
, with the boundary conditions (
3.1.2
), (
3.1.3
).
If
f_{k}\in C^{1}\left[0,\pi\right]
,
k=1,2
, and
f
satisfies (
3.1.2
) and (
3.1.3
), then the series (
4.1.1
) converges to
f
uniformly on
[0,\pi]
.

Thus, Theorems
4.1
–
4.4
in some sense generalize the known results (for example, in the sense that
p
and
q
can be complex-valued and less smooth and
\alpha
and
\beta
from the boundary conditions are also complex) or supplement them.

4.2
Notations and outline of the proof of Theorem
4.1
.

In addition to the solution
\varphi\left(x,\lambda,\alpha\right)
of Cauchy problem (
2.1.13
) we also consider the solution
u\left(x,\lambda,\beta\right)
of the system (
2.1.1
), satisfying the initial conditions
u_{1}\left(\pi,\lambda,\beta\right)=\sin\beta
,
u_{2}\left(\pi,\lambda,\beta\right)=-\cos\beta
.
The eigenvalues of the problem
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
are the roots of both the equations
\chi(\lambda)=0
(see (
3.1.5
) ) and the equation
\chi_{1}(\lambda)=u_{1}\left(0,\lambda,\beta\right)\cos\alpha+u_{2}\left(0,\lambda,\beta\right)\sin\alpha=0
.
In this case
\varphi_{n}\left(x\right)=\varphi\left(x,\lambda_{n},\alpha\right)
and
u_{n}\left(x\right)=u\left(x,\lambda_{n},\beta\right)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, are eigenfunctions corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}
and, in the case of simplicity of eigenvalues,
\varphi_{n}
and
u_{n}
are linearly dependent, i.e. there exist constants
c_{n}=c_{n}\left(p,q,\alpha,\beta\right)
, such that

u_{n}\left(x\right)=c_{n}\varphi_{n}\left(x\right).
