(4.2.1)

Note also, that the system (
2.1.1
) can be written as a normal system
y^{\prime}=A\left(x,\lambda\right)y
, where the trace of the matrix
A\left(x,\lambda\right)=0
.
According to Liouville’s formula, it follows that the Wronskian
\omega\left(x,\lambda\right)=\omega\left(\lambda\right)=\left|\begin{array}[]{cc}{\varphi_{1}}&{u_{1}}\\
{\varphi_{2}}&{u_{2}}\end{array}\right|
depends only on
\lambda
, and, now it is easy to calculate,
\omega\left(0,\lambda\right)=\chi\left(\lambda\right)=\omega\left(\pi,\lambda\right)=-\chi_{1}\left(\lambda\right)
, i.e. there is a connection

\omega\left(x,\,\lambda\right)=\omega\left(\lambda\right)=\chi_{1}\left(\lambda\right)=-\chi\left(\lambda\right).

(4.2.2)

Lemma 4.1
.

There is equality

\displaystyle\left.\int_{0}^{\pi}\left[\varphi_{1}^{2}\left(x,\lambda,\alpha\right)+\varphi_{2}^{2}\left(x,\lambda,\alpha\right)\right]dx=\left[\varphi_{1}\cdot\dot{\varphi}_{2}-\varphi_{2}\cdot\dot{\varphi}_{1}\right]\,\right|^{\pi}_{0}

(4.2.3)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{1}\left(\pi,\lambda\right)\cdot\dot{\varphi}_{2}\left(\pi,\lambda\right)-\varphi_{2}\left(\pi,\lambda\right)\cdot\dot{\varphi}_{1}\left(\pi,\,\lambda\right).

Remark 4.1
.

In the last equality we look into account that since
\varphi_{1}(0,\lambda)\equiv\sin\alpha
,
\varphi_{2}(0,\lambda)=-\cos\alpha
, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, then

\dot{\varphi}_{1}\left(0,\lambda\right)\equiv\dot{\varphi}_{2}\left(0,\lambda\right)\equiv 0.

(4.2.4)

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

Let us write that
\varphi=\left(\varphi_{1},\,\varphi_{2}\right)^{T}
is the solution of (
2.1.1
):

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}_{2}\left(x,\lambda\right)+p\left(x\right)\varphi_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)+q\left(x\right)\varphi_{2}\left(x,\lambda\right)\equiv\lambda\varphi_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right),

(4.2.5)

\displaystyle-\varphi^{\prime}_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)+q\left(x\right)\varphi_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)-p\left(x\right)\varphi_{2}\left(x,\lambda\right)\equiv\lambda\varphi_{2}\left(x,\lambda\right),

(4.2.6)

and let us differentiate these identities with respect to
\lambda

\left(\dot{f}=\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\lambda}\right)
:

\displaystyle\dot{\varphi^{\prime}}_{2}\left(x,\lambda\right)+p\left(x\right)\dot{\varphi}_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)+q\left(x\right)\dot{\varphi}_{2}\left(x,\lambda\right)\equiv\varphi_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)+\lambda\dot{\varphi}_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right),

(4.2.7)

\displaystyle-\dot{\varphi^{\prime}}_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)+q\left(x\right)\dot{\varphi}_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)-p\left(x\right)\dot{\varphi}_{2}\left(x,\lambda\right)\equiv\varphi_{2}\left(x,\lambda\right)+\lambda\dot{\varphi}_{2}\left(x,\lambda\right).

(4.2.8)

Multiplying (
4.2.5
) by
-\dot{\varphi}_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)
, (
4.2.6
) by
-\dot{\varphi}_{2}\left(x,\lambda\right)
, (
4.2.7
) by
\varphi_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)
, (
4.2.8
) by
\varphi_{2}
and adding all together, we obtain the identity

\dfrac{d}{dx}\left(\varphi_{1}\cdot\dot{\varphi}_{2}-\varphi_{2}\cdot\dot{\varphi}_{1}\right)\equiv\varphi_{1}^{2}+\varphi_{2}^{2},

integrating the latter and taking into account (
4.2.4
), we obtain (
4.2.3
).
Lemma
4.1
is proved.
∎

If now in identity (
4.2.3
) we take
\lambda=\lambda_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)~
, then we obtain

\int_{0}^{\pi}\left[\varphi_{1}^{2}\left(x,\lambda_{n}\right)+\varphi_{2}^{2}\left(x,\lambda_{n}\right)\right]dx=\varphi_{1}\left(\pi,\lambda_{n}\right)\dot{\varphi}_{2}\left(\pi,\lambda_{n}\right)-\dot{\varphi}_{1}\left(\pi,\lambda_{n}\right)\varphi_{2}\left(\pi,\lambda_{n}\right).

(4.2.9)

On the other hand, from (
4.2.1
) (since
\omega(x,\lambda)\equiv\varphi_{1}(x,\lambda)\cdot u_{2}(x,\lambda)-\varphi_{2}(x,\lambda)u_{1}(x,\lambda)\equiv\omega(\pi,\lambda)
)

\dot{\omega}\left(\lambda_{n}\right)=\dot{\varphi}_{1}\left(\pi,\lambda_{n}\right)u_{2}\left(\pi,\lambda_{n}\right)-\dot{\varphi}_{2}\left(\pi,\lambda_{n}\right)u_{1}\left(\pi,\lambda_{n}\right)+\varphi_{1}\left(\pi,\lambda_{n}\right)\dot{u}_{2}\left(\pi,\lambda_{n}\right)-\\
-\varphi_{2}\left(\pi,\lambda_{n}\right)\dot{u}_{1}\left(\pi,\lambda_{n}\right)\equiv c_{n}\left[\dot{\varphi}_{1}\left(\pi,\lambda_{n}\right)\cdot\varphi_{2}\left(\pi,\,\lambda_{n}\right)-\dot{\varphi}_{2}\left(\pi,\lambda_{n}\right)\cdot\varphi_{1}\left(\pi,\lambda_{n}\right)\right].

(4.2.10)

Here we took into account that
\dot{u}_{1}\left(\pi,\lambda\right)\equiv\dot{u}_{2}\left(\pi,\lambda\right)\equiv 0
for arbitrary
\lambda
.
It follows from (
4.2.3
), (
4.2.9
) and (
4.2.10
) that

\int_{0}^{\pi}\left[\varphi_{1}^{2}\left(x,\,\lambda_{n}\right)+\varphi_{2}^{2}\left(x,\,\lambda_{n}\right)\right]dx=-\frac{\dot{\omega}\left(\lambda_{n}\right)}{c_{n}}.

(4.2.11)

It is seen from (
4.2.11
) that from the simplicity of the eigenvalues ( i.e. if
\omega(\lambda_{n})=0
, then
\dot{\omega}(\lambda)\neq 0
) it follows that
(\varphi_{n},\varphi_{n})\neq 0
.
It also follows from (
4.2.11
) that the eigenvalues are simple in case when
p,q\in L_{R}^{1}\left[0,\pi\right]
and
\alpha,\beta\in R
, i.e. in symmetric case.

Indeed, in this case the components
\varphi_{1n}
and
\varphi_{2n}
of eigenfunctions
\varphi_{n}\left(x\right)=\varphi\left(x,\lambda_{n},\alpha\right)
are real-valued an since
\varphi_{n}(x)\not\equiv 0
, then
(\varphi_{n},\varphi_{n})>0
.
It follows from (
4.2.2
) that
\omega\left(\lambda_{n}\right)=0
, and from (
4.2.11
) that
\dot{\omega}\left(\lambda_{n}\right)\neq 0
, i.e. the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}
are simple.
Note that in this case, it is easy to prove also ”algebraic” simplicity of eigenvalues, i.e., if two eigenfunctions correspond to the same eigenvalue, then they are linearly dependent.

Indeed, if we have two eigenfunctions
\varphi
and
\tilde{\varphi}
, then the fact that they satisfy the same boundary condition (
3.1.3
) we can write in the form of equality to zero the scalar product of vectors

\varphi(\pi,\lambda_{n})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{n})\\
\varphi_{2}(\pi,\lambda_{n})\end{array}\right)
and
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\cos\beta\\
\sin\beta\end{array}\right)
in two-dimensional real space
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, i.e.

\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{n})\cos\beta+\varphi_{2}(\pi,\lambda_{n})\sin\beta=\left\langle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{n})\\
\varphi_{2}(\pi,\lambda_{n})\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\cos\beta\\
\sin\beta\end{array}\right)\right\rangle=0.

Similarly for
\tilde{\varphi}(\pi,\lambda_{n})
.
But if in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(i.e., on the plane) two vectors are perpendicular to the same (nonzero) vector
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\cos\beta\\
\sin\beta\end{array}\right)
, then they are linearly dependent, that is
\varphi(\pi,\lambda_{n})=c_{n}\tilde{\varphi}(\pi,\lambda_{n})
.
And since
\varphi
and
\tilde{\varphi}
both are solutions of the same equation
\ell y=\lambda_{n}y
, then it follows from the uniqueness of the solution of the Cauchy problem that the equality
\varphi(x,\lambda_{n})=c_{n}\tilde{\varphi}(x,\lambda_{n})
holds for all
x\in[0,\pi]
.

It is not difficult to check directly (see
[
Tit44
]
, or
[
LS88
]
page 245) that the solution of non-homogeneous boundary value problem

\displaystyle\ell y=\lambda y-f\left(x\right),

\displaystyle\lambda\in C

(4.2.12)

\displaystyle y_{1}\left(0\right)\cos\alpha+y_{2}\left(0\right)\sin\alpha=0,

\displaystyle\alpha\in C

(4.2.13)

\displaystyle y_{1}\left(\pi\right)\cos\beta+y_{2}\left(\pi\right)\sin\beta=0,

\displaystyle\beta\in C

(4.2.14)

for
\lambda\neq\lambda_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and arbitrary
f_{1},f_{2}\in L_{\mathbb{C}}^{1}\left[0,\pi\right]
given by the formula

y\left(x,\lambda\right)=\frac{u\left(x,\,\lambda\right)}{\omega\left(\lambda\right)}\int_{0}^{x}\varphi^{T}\left(\xi,\,\lambda\right)f\left(\xi\right)d\xi+\frac{\varphi\left(x,\lambda\right)}{\omega\left(\lambda\right)}\int_{x}^{\pi}u^{T}\left(\xi,\lambda\right)f\left(\xi\right)d\xi.

(4.2.15)

In the complex plane
\lambda

\left(\lambda\in C\right)
consider a sequence of expanding contours
C_{m,l}
, that intersect the (parallel to the real) axis
\im\lambda=\im\dfrac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}
at the points
-m+\dfrac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}-\dfrac{1}{2}
and
l+\dfrac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}+\dfrac{1}{2}
.
According to the asymptotics of the eigenvalues (
3.1.4
), for sufficiently large
m
and
l
, the eigenvalues
\lambda_{-m},\lambda_{-m+1},\ldots,\lambda_{l-1},\lambda_{l}
will be inside this contour.
By
C_{0,25}
we denote the set of all complex numbers of the plane
\lambda
without circles of radius
0,25
centered at points
\displaystyle n+\dfrac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
(in which, starting from some
n_{0}
, all eigenvalues are found).
For sufficiently large
m
and
l
the contours
C_{m,l}
will lie in
C_{0,25}
, i.e. the distance of any point of the contour
C_{m,l}
to eigenvalue will be more than
0,25
.
For example, as
C_{m,l}
we can take circles of radius
\dfrac{m+l+1}{2}
, centered at the point
\left(\dfrac{l-m}{2}+\re\dfrac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi},\im\dfrac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}\right)
.
Below we prove two lemmas, from which Theorem
4.1
will follow.

Lemma 4.2
.

If
f
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
4.1
, then

\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{C_{m,l}}y\left(x,\lambda\right)d\lambda=-f\left(x\right)+R_{m,l}(x),

where

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,l>N\\
N\to\infty\end{subarray}}\sup_{x\in[0,\,\pi]}\left|R_{m,l}\left(x\right)\right|=0.

(4.2.16)

Lemma 4.3
.

Provided the simplicity of eigenvalues, the residue

\res y\left(x,\lambda_{n}\right)=-h_{n}\left(x\right)\cdot\int_{0}^{\pi}h_{n}^{T}\left(\xi\right)f\left(\xi\right)d\xi=-\left(f,h_{n}\right)\cdot h_{n}(x).

Since the contour integral of a meromorphic function is equal to the sum of the residues at the poles lying inside the contour, from Lemmas
4.2
and
4.3
, we obtain the equality

f\left(x\right)-R_{m,l}\left(x\right)=\sum_{n=-m}^{l}\left(f,h_{n}\right)\cdot h_{n}\left(x\right).

Passing to the limit, when
m
and
n\to\infty
according to (
4.2.16
) we obtain the assertion of Theorem
4.1
.

4.3
Proof of Theorem
4.1

We have proved in Chapter
2
that there exist transformation operators such that the solutions
\varphi\left(x,\lambda,\alpha\right)
and
u\left(x,\lambda,\beta\right)
can be represented in the form

\displaystyle\varphi\left(x,\lambda,\alpha\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\sin\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)}\\
{-\cos\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)}\end{array}\right)+\int_{0}^{x}K\left(x,\xi,\alpha\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\sin\left(\lambda\xi+\alpha\right)}\\
{-\cos\left(\lambda\xi+\alpha\right)}\end{array}\right)d\xi,

\displaystyle u\left(x,\lambda,\beta\right)=\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\sin\left(\lambda\left(x-\pi\right)+\beta\right)}\\
{-\cos\left(\lambda\left(x-\pi\right)+\beta\right)}\end{array}\!\!\!\right)+\int_{x}^{\pi}H\left(x,t,\beta\right)\cdot\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\sin\left(\lambda\left(t-\pi\right)+\beta\right)}\\
{-\cos\left(\!\lambda\left(t-\pi\right)+\beta\!\right)}\end{array}\!\!\!\right)dt

where matrix kernels
K\left(x,t,\alpha\right)
and
H\left(x,t,\beta\right)
have certain properties, in particular, for
p,q\in L_{\mathbb{C}}^{1}\left[0,\pi\right]
,
K_{ij}\left(x,\cdot,\alpha\right)\in L^{1}\left(0,x\right)
,
H_{ij}\left(x,\cdot,\beta\right)\in L^{1}\left(x,\pi\right)
,
\left(i,j=1,2\right)
uniformly with respect to all complex
\alpha
and
\beta
, having bounded imaginary part, which, for short, we will write in the form:

\alpha,\beta\in\Pi_{M}=\left\{\gamma:\;\left|\im\gamma\right|\leq M\right\}

In Chapter
2
we denoted these kernels by
K_{0}(x,t,\alpha)
and
H_{\pi}(x,t,\beta)
.
Here, for brevity, we omit the indexes.
Thus, for components
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
and
u_{1},u_{2}
we have the representations

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}\left(x,\lambda,\alpha\right)

\displaystyle=\sin\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)+\int_{0}^{x}\left[K_{11}\left(x,t,\alpha\right)\sin\left(\lambda t+\alpha\right)-K_{12}\left(x,t,\alpha\right)\cos\left(\lambda t+\alpha\right)\right]dt

(4.3.1)

\displaystyle=\sin\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)+g_{1}\left(x,\lambda,\alpha\right),

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}\left(x,\lambda,\alpha\right)

\displaystyle=-\cos\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)+\int_{0}^{x}\left[K_{21}\left(x,t,\,\alpha\right)\sin\left(\lambda t+\alpha\right)-K_{22}\left(x,t,\alpha\right)\cos\left(\lambda t+\alpha\right)\right]dt=

(4.3.2)

\displaystyle=-\cos\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)+g_{2}\left(x,\lambda,\alpha\right),

\displaystyle u_{1}\left(x,\lambda,\beta\right)

\displaystyle=\sin\left(\lambda\left(x-\pi\right)+\beta\right)+\int_{x}^{\pi}\left[H_{11}\left(x,t,\beta\right)\sin\left(\lambda\left(t-\pi\right)+\beta\right)-H_{12}\left(x,t,\beta\right)\cos\left(\lambda\left(t-\pi\right)+\beta\right)\right]dt

(4.3.3)

\displaystyle=\sin\left(\lambda\left(x-\pi\right)+\beta\right)+r_{1}\left(x,\lambda,\beta\right)

\displaystyle u_{2}\left(x,\lambda,\beta\right)

\displaystyle=-\cos\left(\lambda\left(x-\pi\right)+\beta\right)+\int_{x}^{\pi}\left\langle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{H_{21}\left(x,t,\beta\right)}\\
{H_{22}\left(x,t,\beta\right)}\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\sin\left(\lambda\left(t-\pi\right)+\beta\right)}\\
{-\cos\left(\lambda\left(t-\pi\right)+\beta\right)}\end{array}\right)\right\rangle dt

(4.3.4)

\displaystyle=-\cos\left[\lambda\left(x-\pi\right)+\beta\right]+r_{2}\left(x,\lambda,\beta\right).

According to Lemma
3.1
about
g_{1,2}
and
r_{1,2}
we can say, that

g_{k}\left(x,\lambda,\alpha\right)=\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|x},r_{k}\left(x,\lambda,\beta\right)=\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\left(\pi-x\right)},\quad k=1,2\,,

(4.3.5)

where
\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)\to 0
, when
\left|\lambda\right|\to\infty
.
In what follows, we use the same symbol
\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)
to designate different functions of
\lambda
, such that
\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)\to 0
when
\left|\lambda\right|\to\infty
, i.e.
\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)=o\left(1\right)
at
\left|\lambda\right|\to\infty
.
Denoting

I_{1}=I_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)=\int_{0}^{x}\left[f_{1}\left(\xi\right)\sin\left(\lambda\xi+\alpha\right)-f_{2}\left(\xi\right)\cos\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)\right]d\xi,

I_{2}=I_{2}\left(x,\lambda\right)=\int_{0}^{x}\left[f_{1}\left(\xi\right)\sin\left(\lambda\left(\xi-\pi\right)+\beta\right)-f_{2}\left(\xi\right)\cos\left(\lambda\left(\xi-\pi\right)+\beta\right)\right]d\xi,

and substituting expressions (
4.3.1
)-(
4.3.4
) into (
4.2.15
), for components
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
of the solution of non-homogeneous problem (
4.2.12
)–(
4.2.14
) we get the expressions:

y_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)=\frac{\sin\left[\lambda\left(x-\pi\right)+\beta\right]}{\omega\left(\lambda\right)}I_{1}+\frac{\sin\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)}{\omega\left(\lambda\right)}I_{2}+\frac{r_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)}{\omega\left(\lambda\right)}I_{1}+\frac{g_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)}{\omega\left(\lambda\right)}I_{2},

(4.3.6)

y_{2}\left(x,\lambda\right)=-\frac{\cos\left[\lambda\left(x-\pi\right)+\beta\right]}{\omega\left(\lambda\right)}I_{1}-\frac{\cos\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)}{\omega\left(\lambda\right)}I_{2}+\frac{r_{2}\left(x,\lambda\right)}{\omega\left(\lambda\right)}I_{1}+\frac{g_{2}\left(x,\lambda\right)}{\omega\left(\lambda\right)}I_{2}.

(4.3.7)

About the integrals
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
we note, that

\displaystyle I_{1}\left(x,\lambda,\alpha\right)=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{x}f_{1}\left(\xi\right)d\cos\left(\lambda\xi+\alpha\right)-\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{x}f_{2}\left(\xi\right)d\sin\left(\lambda\xi+\alpha\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\lambda}\left[f_{1}\left(x\right)\cos\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)+\right.\left.+f_{2}(x)\sin\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)-f_{1}(0)\cos\alpha-f_{2}\left(0\right)\sin\alpha\right]+

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\lambda}\left[\int_{0}^{x}f^{\prime}_{1}\left(\xi\right)\cos\left(\lambda\xi+\alpha\right)d\xi+\int_{0}^{x}f^{\prime}_{2}\left(\xi\right)\sin\left(\lambda\xi+\alpha\right)d\xi\right]

If
f
satisfies the condition (
4.2.13
) and is absolutely continuous, then the last two integrals satisfy to estimate
\left|\int_{0}^{x}f^{\prime}_{1}\left(\xi\right)\cos\left(\lambda\xi+\alpha\right)\,d\xi\right|<\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|x}
, uniformly by
x\in\left[0,\pi\right]
and
\alpha\in\Pi_{M}
, and
f_{1}\left(0\right)\cos\alpha+f_{2}\left(0\right)\sin\alpha=0
.
Therefore

I_{1}\left(x,\lambda,\alpha\right)=-\frac{1}{\lambda}\left[f_{1}\left(x\right)\cos\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)+f_{2}\left(x\right)\sin\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)\right]+\frac{\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)}{\lambda}e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|x}

(4.3.8)

Similarly, if
f
satisfies (
4.2.14
), then

\displaystyle I_{2}\left(x,\lambda,\beta\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\left\{f_{1}\left(x\right)\cos\left[\lambda\left(x-\pi\right)+\beta\right]\right.+\left.f_{2}\left(x\right)\sin\left[\lambda\left(x-\pi\right)+\beta\right]\right\}+

(4.3.9)

\displaystyle+\frac{\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)}{\lambda}e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\left(\pi-x\right)}

uniformly by
x\in\left[0,\pi\right]
and
\beta\in\Pi_{M}
.

If we denote
z=\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta
, then the set of all complex number
z
, satisfying the estimates
\left|z-\pi n\right|\geq\dfrac{\pi}{4}
, coincides with the set
C_{0,25}
.

Since the Wronskian (see (
4.3.1
)–(
4.3.4
))

\displaystyle\omega\left(\lambda\right)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{1}\left(\pi,\lambda\right)u_{2}\left(\pi,\lambda\right)-\varphi_{2}\left(\pi,\lambda\right)u_{1}\left(\pi,\lambda\right)

(4.3.10)

\displaystyle=-\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)-g_{1}\left(\pi,\lambda\right)\cos\beta-g_{2}\left(\pi,\lambda\right)\sin\beta

and since for all
\lambda\in C_{0,25}
, according to the Lemma
3.2
, the estimate

\left|\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)\right|>\frac{1}{4}e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\pi+\left|\im\alpha\right|-\left|\im\beta\right|},

hold, and for
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
(
4.3.5
) hold, then we get

\left|\omega\left(\lambda\right)\right|>\frac{1}{4}e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\pi+\left|\im\alpha\right|-\left|\im\beta\right|}-\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\pi}\geq c_{0}e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\pi}

(4.3.11)

(where
c_{0}
is some positive number) for sufficiently large (by absolute value)
\lambda
,
\lambda\in C_{0,25}
.
Besides this, from (
4.3.10
) we have the representation
\frac{1}{\omega\left(\lambda\right)}=-\frac{1}{\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)}+G(\lambda)
, where
G\left(\lambda\right)=-\frac{g_{1}(\pi,\lambda)\cos\beta+g_{2}(\pi,\lambda)\sin\beta}{\sin(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta)\cdot\omega(\lambda)}
and the estimate
\left|G\left(\lambda\right)\right|\cdot\left|\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)\right|\leq\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)=o\left(1\right)
hold for
\lambda\in C_{0,25}
, uniformly by
\alpha,\beta\in\Pi_{M}
.
In particular

\frac{\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)}{\lambda\omega(\lambda)}=-\frac{1}{\lambda}+o\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\right),\quad\text{ for }\left|\lambda\right|\to\infty\text{ and }\lambda\in C_{0,25}\,.

(4.3.12)

Denote by
F=\max_{x\in\left[0,\pi\right]}\left|f_{1,2}\left(x\right)\right|
, and
R_{1,2}\left(x,\lambda\right)
the sum of the last two terms in (
4.3.6
) and (
4.3.7
), respectively.
Taking into account that
\left|\sin\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)\right|
,
\left|\cos\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)\right|\leq e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|x}\cdot e^{\left|\im\alpha\right|}
, and
\left|\sin\left(\lambda\left(x-\pi\right)+\beta\right)\right|
,
\left|\cos\left(\lambda\left(x-\pi\right)+\beta\right)\right|\leq e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\left(\pi-x\right)}\cdot e^{\left|\im\beta\right|}
, and also the estimates (
4.3.5
), (
4.3.8
) and (
4.3.9
) for
R_{1,2}\left(x,\lambda\right)
we obtain the estimate

\left|R_{1,2}\left(x,\lambda\right)\right|\leq\frac{\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)}{\left|\lambda\right|\left|\omega\left(\lambda\right)\right|}\cdot e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\pi}

uniformly by
x\in\left[0,\pi\right]
and
\alpha,\beta\in\Pi_{M}
.
Taking into account (
4.3.11
), we can assert, that on the circles
C_{m,l}
the estimate

\left|R_{1,2}\left(x,\lambda\right)\right|\leq\frac{\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)}{\left|\lambda\right|},

(4.3.13)

hold, i.e. the functions
R_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)
and
R_{2}\left(x,\lambda\right)
are meromorphic functions, which on
C_{m,l}
satisfy the estimate (
4.3.13
) uniformly by
x\in\left[0,\pi\right]
and
\alpha,\beta\in\Pi_{M}
.
Therefore, the contour integral over
C_{m,l}
of these functions tends to zero as
m,l>N\to\infty
.
Substituting (
4.3.8
) and (
4.3.9
) into (
4.3.6
), we can write (
4.3.6
) in the form

\displaystyle y_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)}{\lambda\omega\left(\lambda\right)}f_{1}\left(x\right)+\frac{\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)}{\lambda\omega\left(\lambda\right)}e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|x}\cdot\sin\left(\lambda\left(x-\pi\right)+\beta\right)+

(4.3.14)

\displaystyle+\frac{\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)}{\lambda\omega\left(\lambda\right)}e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\left(\pi-x\right)}\cdot\sin\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)+\tilde{R}_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)

Like the remainder
\tilde{R}_{1}\left(x,\lambda\right)
, the sum of the second and third terms in (
4.3.14
) is a meromorphic function, which, according to (
4.3.11
), on the contours
C_{m,l}
satisfies the estimate

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)}{\lambda\omega\left(\lambda\right)}e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|x}\sin\left(\lambda\left(x-\pi\right)+\beta\right)+\frac{\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)}{\lambda\omega\left(\lambda\right)}e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\left(\pi-x\right)}\sin\left(\lambda x+\alpha\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{\epsilon\left(\lambda\right)}{\lambda\omega\left(\lambda\right)}\right|\left(e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\pi}\cdot e^{\left|\im\beta\right|}+e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\pi}\cdot e^{\left|\im\alpha\right|}\right)

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\epsilon_{1}\left(\lambda\right)}{\lambda\omega\left(\lambda\right)}\right|e^{\left|\im\lambda\right|\pi}\leq\frac{\left|\epsilon_{1}\left(\lambda\right)\right|}{\left|\lambda\right|}=o\left(\frac{1}{\left|\lambda\right|}\right)

uniformly by all
x\in\left[0,\pi\right]
and
\alpha,\beta\in\Pi_{M}
.
Regarding the first term
\frac{\sin\left(\lambda\pi+\alpha-\beta\right)}{\lambda\omega\left(\lambda\right)}f_{1}\left(x\right)
, according to (
4.3.12
), we can say that it is equal to
-\frac{1}{\lambda}f_{1}\left(x\right)+o\left(\frac{1}{\left|\lambda\right|}\right)f_{1}\left(x\right)
.
Quite similarly, for
y_{2}
from (
4.3.7
) we obtain
y_{2}\left(x,\lambda\right)=-\frac{1}{\lambda}f_{2}\left(x\right)+o\left(\frac{1}{\left|\lambda\right|}\right)
, where the reminder is estimated on the contours
C_{m,l}
uniformly by
x\in\left[0,\pi\right]
and
\alpha,\beta\in\Pi_{M}
.
Therefore (since
\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{C_{m,l}}\frac{1}{\lambda}d\lambda=1
)
\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{C_{m,l}}y\left(x,\lambda\right)dx=-f\left(x\right)+R_{m,l}\left(x\right)
, where
\sup_{x\in\left[0,\pi\right]}\left|R_{m,l}\left(x,\alpha,\beta\right)\right|\to 0
for
m,l>N\to\infty
.
Lemma
4.2
is proved.

Turning to the proof of Lemma
4.3
, we note that according to the formula for calculating the residue at a simple pole, from (
4.2.15
) and (
4.2.1
) we have

\left.\res y\left(x,\lambda\right)\right|_{\lambda=\lambda_{n}}=\lim_{\lambda\to\lambda_{n}}\left(\lambda-\lambda_{n}\right)y\left(x,\lambda\right)=\frac{k_{n}\cdot\varphi\left(x,\lambda_{n}\right)}{\dot{\omega}\left(\lambda_{n}\right)}\int_{0}^{\pi}\varphi^{T}\left(\xi,\lambda_{n}\right)f\left(\xi\right)d\xi.

According to (
4.2.11
) the last expression is
-\int_{0}^{\pi}h_{n}^{T}\left(\xi\right)f\left(\xi\right)d\xi\cdot h_{n}\left(x\right)=-\left(f,h_{n}\right)\cdot h_{n}\left(x\right)
, where
h_{n}
is defined as in Section
4.1
.
Lemma
4.3
is proved, and thus Theorem
4.1
is proved.

4.4
Proof of Theorems
4.2
,
4.3
and
4.4

To prove Theorem
4.2
, note that if both series converge uniformly to
f
(for example, if
f
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
4.1
), then Theorem
4.2
is obvious.
If there is no uniform convergence, then it should be noted that the terms that interfere with uniform convergence (for example
f_{1}\left(0\right)\cos\alpha+f_{2}\left(0\right)\sin\alpha
and
f_{1}\left(\pi\right)\cos\beta+f_{2}\left(\pi\right)\sin\beta
) are the same in both sums and their difference vanishes.

Theorem
4.3
is an obvious consequence of Theorem
4.1
, and we have separated it into a distinct theorem only because it is an important case of a selfadjoint operator generated by the canonical Dirac system.

Thus, in selfadjoint case the system of normalized eigenfunctions
\{h_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
form an orthonormal system, i.e.
(h_{k},h_{j})=\delta_{k,j}=\left\{\begin{matrix}1,&\,k=j,\\
0,&\,k\neq j.\end{matrix}\right.

Lemma 4.4
.

If
f\in L^{2}[(0,\pi),\mathbb{C}^{2}]
and
\{h_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an orthonormal system of eigenfunctions of the operator
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)=L(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)
, then the series

\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}|(f,h_{k})|^{2}

(4.4.1)

converges and

\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}|(f,h_{k})|^{2}\leq\|f\|^{2}.

(4.4.2)

(
4.4.2
) is called Bessel’s inequality.

Proof.

Since
\|f\|^{2}=(f,f)
, it is easy to calculate that for any finite
m
and
n
the inequality

0\leq\|f-\sum_{k=-m}^{n}(f,h_{k})h_{k}\|^{2}=\|f\|^{2}-\sum_{k=-m}^{n}|(f,h_{k})|^{2}

(4.4.3)

holds, which proves the convergence of the series (
4.4.1
) and Bessel’s inequality.
∎

Definition 4.1
.

A sequence
\{\varphi_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of vector-functions in Hilbert space
H=L^{2}[(0,\pi);\mathbb{C}^{2}]
is called complete in
H
if the closure of its span coincides with
H
.
Equivalently, a sequence
\{\varphi_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
is complete in
H
if and only if the following implication holds for every
f\in H
,

(f,\varphi_{m})=0,\quad m\in\mathbb{Z},\Rightarrow f=0.

Proof of Theorem
4.4
.

Since
D\left(L\right)
is dense everywhere in
L^{2}\left[\left(0,\pi\right)\,\mathbb{C}^{2}\right]
, then for any
f\in L^{2}
there exists
f_{\varepsilon}\in D\left(L\right)
, such that
\left\|f-f_{\epsilon}\right\|<\epsilon
(for arbitrary
\epsilon>0
).
Therefore

\left\|f-\sum_{-m}^{n}\left(f,\,h_{k}\right)h_{k}\right\|\leq\left\|f-f_{\epsilon}\right\|+\left\|f_{\epsilon}-\sum_{-m}^{n}\left(f_{\epsilon},\,h_{k}\right)h_{k}\right\|+\left\|\sum_{-m}^{n}\left(f-f_{\epsilon},\,h_{k}\right)h_{k}\right\|.

It is easy to calculate that the last term equals

\left(\sum_{-m}^{n}|(f-f_{\epsilon},\,h_{k})|^{2}\right)^{1/2},

which, by Bessel’s inequality, does not exceed
\left\|f-f_{\epsilon}\right\|
.
Applying Theorem
4.1
to the function
f_{\epsilon}
, we conclude that there exists a number
N
, depending on
\epsilon
, such that

\left\|\sum_{-m}^{n}\left(f-f_{\epsilon},\,h_{k}\right)h_{k}\right\|<\epsilon,\quad(m,n\geq N),

and, therefore,

\left\|f-\sum_{-m}^{n}\left(f,\,h_{k}\right)h_{k}\right\|\leq\epsilon+\epsilon+\epsilon=3\epsilon,

which proves the equality

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,m>N\\
N\to\infty\end{subarray}}\left\|f-\sum_{-m}^{n}\left(f,\,h_{k}\right)h_{k}\right\|=0.

Parseval’s equality directly follows from the last equality and (
4.4.3
).
Thus, we have proved Theorem
4.4
.
∎

Definition 4.2
.

A sequence
\{\varphi_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of vector-functions in Hilbert space
H=L^{2}[(0,\pi);\mathbb{C}^{2}]
is called a Riesz basis, if it admits a representation
\varphi_{m}=Te_{m},\,m\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
\{e_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an orthonormal basis in
H
and
T:H\rightarrow H
is a bounded operator with a bounded inverse.

Notes and references

The completeness of the system of eigenfunctions for the system of two equations of the first order was proved in 1921 by W.A. Hurwitz
[
Hur21
]
.

A point-wise convergence result in spectral decomposition was first investigated in
[
BL23
]
and then in
[
Tit61
]
.

Theorems
4.1
-
4.3
were published
[
HA06
]
.

Theorem
4.5
was published in
[
Tit44
]
.

Theorem
4.6
was published in
[
LS88
]
.

CHAPTER
5
Eigenvalue Function

5.1
Definition and properties.

Consider the boundary value problem
L(\Omega,\gamma,0)=L(p,q,\gamma,0)
:

\displaystyle\ell y\equiv\left\{B\dfrac{d}{dx}+\Omega(x)\right\}\,y=\lambda y,\quad y=\left(y_{1}\atop y_{2}\right),\quad 0<x<\pi,\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}

(5.1.1)

\displaystyle y_{1}(0)\cos\gamma+y_{2}(0)\sin\gamma=0,

(5.1.2)

\displaystyle y_{1}(\pi)=0,

(5.1.3)

If
p
and
q
are real, summable functions on
[0,\pi]
, i.e.
p,q\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,\pi)
, then differential operators
L(\Omega,\gamma)
, generated by the differential expression
\ell
in the Hilbert space of two-component vector-functions
L^{2}(0,\pi;\mathbb{C}^{2})
, on the domain

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{L(\Omega,\gamma)}=\left\{y=\left(y_{1}\atop y_{2}\right),\;y_{k}\in AC[0,\pi],\;(\ell y)_{k}\in L^{2}[0,\pi],\;k=1,2;\right.

\displaystyle\Big.y_{1}(0)\cos\gamma+y_{2}(0)\sin\gamma=0,\;y_{1}(\pi)=0\Big\}

are self-adjoint for every real
\gamma
(see Theorem
3.4
).
It’s known (see Chapter
3
), that
L(\Omega,\gamma)
has a discrete spectrum consisting of simple eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\gamma)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, which forms an unbounded (both from above and below) sequence.
The enumeration of the eigenvalues for
\gamma\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
is given in Chapter
3
(see (
3.6.18
)–(
3.6.19
)).

To set the enumeration of the eigenvalues for all real values of the parameter
\gamma
from (
5.1.2
), proceed as follows.
Represent an arbitrary real
\gamma
as
\gamma=\alpha-\pi m
for some
\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Define eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}(\gamma)
as follows:

\lambda_{n}(\gamma)=\lambda_{n}(\alpha-\pi m)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}\lambda_{n+m}(\alpha).

(5.1.4)

The formula (
5.1.4
) defines a unique enumeration for eigenvalues for all real values of the parameter
\gamma
from the boundary condition (
5.1.2
).

Definition 5.1
.

A function, which is defined for each real number
\gamma=\alpha-\pi m
,
\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, by formula

\lambda(\gamma)=\lambda(\alpha-\pi m)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}\lambda_{m}(\alpha),

where
\lambda_{m}(\alpha)
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, are eigenvalues of the operator
L(\Omega,\alpha)
, enumerated according to (
3.6.18
)–(
3.6.19
), is called eigenvalues function (EVF) of the family of Dirac operators
\left\{L(\Omega,\alpha):\,\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right]\right\}
.

Remark 5.1
.

In fact, saying EVF we meant the eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}(\gamma)
, continued from
\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
to the whole real axis by formula (
5.1.4
) (for
n=0
).

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
p,q\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,\pi)
.
Then the EVF
\lambda(\cdot)
of the family of Dirac operators
\left\{L(\Omega,\alpha),\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right]\right\}
has the following properties:

1.

As a function of a real variable, it is a strictly decreasing real-valued function defined along the whole axis.
There is a point
\alpha_{0}\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
, such that
\lambda(\alpha_{0})=0
.

2.

For each real point
\gamma
, there is some complex neighborhood
V_{\gamma}
, in which a single-valued analytic function
\tilde{\lambda}(\cdot)
is defined, which coincides with
\lambda(\cdot)
for real values of argument, i.e. EVF
\lambda(\cdot)
is a real analytic function.

3.

Function
c(\gamma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}\lambda(\gamma)+\gamma/\pi
has the property:

\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}c^{2}(\alpha-\pi n)<\infty\quad\text{for arbitrary}\quad\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right].

4.

For all
\alpha
and
\beta
, such that
-\frac{\pi}{2}<\alpha<\beta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
and all
n\in\mathbb{Z}

\left.\dfrac{\partial\lambda(\gamma)}{\partial\gamma}\right|_{\gamma=\alpha-\pi n}=\dfrac{\lambda(\alpha-\pi n)-\lambda(\beta-\pi n)}{\sin(\beta-\alpha)}\prod_{k=-\infty\atop{k\neq n}}^{\infty}\dfrac{\lambda(\beta-\pi k)-\lambda(\alpha-\pi n)}{\lambda(\alpha-\pi k)-\lambda(\alpha-\pi n)}.

(5.1.5)

Remark 5.2
.

In Chapter
9
we will see that conditions
1.-4.
are not only necessary, but also sufficient for a certain function defined on the entire real axis to be the EVF of a certain family of Dirac operators
\left\{L(\Omega,\alpha),\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right]\right\}
with
p,q\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

In Chapter
3
we proved, that every eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}(\alpha)=\lambda_{k}(\Omega,\alpha,0)
is a decreasing function of an argument
\alpha
on interval
\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
.

From definition
5.1
it follows that EVF is a strictly decreasing function on the intervals
\left(\pi m-\frac{\pi}{2},\pi m+\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, covering the real axis.
Let us now prove that the EVF is an analytic function on the real axis.
To do this, we will use the implicit function theorem in the following formulation (see, e.g.,
[
Bib81
]
, pg. 166):

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
,
\gamma\in\mathbb{C}
and
F(\lambda,\gamma):\,U\to\mathbb{C}
be an analytic function in some neighborhood
U
of point
(\lambda_{0},\gamma_{0})
, moreover

\frac{\partial F(\lambda_{0},\gamma_{0})}{\partial\lambda}\neq 0.

Then the equality
F(\lambda,\gamma)=F(\lambda_{0},\gamma_{0})
uniquely determines the function
\lambda=\lambda(\gamma):\,V\to\mathbb{C}
, analytic in some neighborhood
V
of the point
\gamma_{0}
and such that for
\gamma\in V

(\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)\in U
,
\lambda(\gamma_{0})=\lambda_{0}
and
F(\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)=F(\lambda_{0},\gamma_{0})
for all
\gamma\in V
.

As
F(\lambda,\gamma)
we have
F(\lambda,\gamma)=\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda,\gamma)
, which is an entire function of two complex variables
\lambda
and
\gamma
.
The eigenvalues of operator
L(\Omega,\gamma)
are zeros (by
\lambda
) of the function
\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda,\gamma)
.
Let
\gamma_{0}
is an arbitrary real number, which we represent as
\gamma_{0}=\alpha_{0}-\pi m
, where
\alpha_{0}\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, and let
\lambda_{0}=\lambda(\gamma_{0})=\lambda(\alpha_{0}-\pi m)=\lambda_{m}(\alpha_{0})
is the value of EVF at
\gamma_{0}
.
Then
\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{0},\gamma_{0})=\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{m}(\alpha_{0}),\alpha_{0}-\pi m)=0
, since eigenfunction
\varphi(x,\lambda_{m}(\alpha_{0}),\alpha_{0}-\pi m)
satisfies the boundary condition (
5.1.3
).
Let us prove that the value of the derivative
\frac{\partial\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{0},\gamma_{0})}{\partial\lambda}
at point
(\lambda_{0},\gamma_{0})
is nonzero.
To this end, note that for the solution
\varphi(x,\lambda,\gamma)
of the Cauchy problem (
2.1.13
), the identity

\frac{\partial\varphi_{2}(\pi,\lambda,\gamma)}{\partial\lambda}\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda,\gamma)-\frac{\partial\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda,\gamma)}{\partial\lambda}\varphi_{2}(\pi,\lambda,\gamma)=\int_{0}^{\pi}\left[\varphi_{1}^{2}(x,\lambda,\gamma)+\varphi_{2}^{2}(x,\lambda,\gamma)\right]\,dx

hold (see (
4.2.9
)).
Considering that
\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)=0
we get

\frac{\partial\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)}{\partial\lambda}=-\frac{1}{\varphi_{2}(\pi,\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)}\int_{0}^{\pi}\left[\varphi_{1}^{2}(x,\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)+\varphi_{2}^{2}(x,\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)\right]\,dx.

(5.1.6)

Since for real
\gamma
the operators
L(\Omega,\gamma)
are self-adjoint, the components
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
of vector-eigenfunctions
\varphi(x,\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)
can be considered real, so
|\varphi|^{2}\equiv|\varphi_{1}|^{2}+|\varphi_{2}|^{2}=\varphi_{1}^{2}+\varphi_{2}^{2}
.
It follows that the square of the
L^{2}
-norm

a(\gamma)=\int_{0}^{\pi}|\varphi(x,\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)|^{2}\,dx

(5.1.7)

of eigenfunction
\varphi(x,\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)
, which is usually called the normalization constant, coincides with the integral on the right-hand side of the formula (
5.1.6
) and is always nonzero for
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
.
It is also obvious that
\varphi_{2}(\pi,\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)\neq 0
, because otherwise, from the uniqueness of the solution to the Cauchy problem it would follow (since
\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)=0
), that
\varphi(x,\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)\equiv 0
, and this contradicts the fact that
\varphi(x,\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)
is an eigenfunction.

Thus, according to (
5.1.6
), the value of the derivative
\frac{\partial\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)}{\partial\lambda}
is nonzero for any real point
\gamma
.
Therefore, according to Theorem
5.2
, there exists a complex neighborhood
V\subset\mathbb{C}
of a real point
\gamma_{0}
, in which defined a single-valued analytic function
\lambda(\gamma)
,
\gamma\in V
, such that
\lambda(\gamma_{0})=\lambda_{0}
and
\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)=0
for all
\gamma\in V
.
Since
\gamma_{0}
is an arbitrary real number, we have proved that
\lambda(\cdot)
is analytic on the entire real axis.
More precisely, there is an open set containing a real axis, where a single-valued analytic function
\tilde{\lambda}(\cdot)
is defined, which coincides with the EVF we have defined for the real values of the argument.

From the continuity of the EVF
\lambda(\gamma)
it follows that when
\gamma
changes to
\left[-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
this function takes all values from (see definition
5.1
)

\lambda\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\lambda\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\pi\right)=\lambda_{1}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)>0\quad\mbox{to}\quad\lambda\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\lambda_{0}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\leq 0.

Therefore, there is a point
\alpha_{0}\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
, such that
\lambda(\alpha_{0})=0
.
Thus, assertions
1)
and
2)
of Theorem
5.1
are proved.

It is known (see Chapter
3
), that if
p,q\in L_{\mathbb{R}}^{2}[0,\pi]
, then for the eigenvalues of the operators
L(\Omega,\alpha)=L(\Omega,\alpha,0)
the following asymptotics holds:

\lambda_{n}(\alpha)=n-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}+h_{n}(\alpha),\qquad\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}h_{n}^{2}(\alpha)<\infty

(5.1.8)

for all
\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
.
In terms of the EVF, this asymptotics can be written as
\lambda(\gamma)=-\frac{\gamma}{\pi}+h(\gamma)
, where
h(\gamma)
is an analytic function at each real point (
h(\alpha-\pi n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}h_{n}(\alpha)
), having the property

\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}h_{n}^{2}(\alpha)<\infty\qquad\mbox{for any}\quad\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\frac{\pi}{2}\right].

To prove the representation (
5.1.5
), first, note that for any real
\gamma
and
\beta
the identity (its derivation is very similar to the derivation of the equalities (
3.4.11
) and (
3.4.12
))

[\lambda(\gamma)-\lambda(\beta)]\cdot\left(\varphi(\cdot,\lambda(\gamma),\gamma),\varphi(\cdot,\lambda(\beta),\beta)\right)=\sin(\beta-\gamma),

(5.1.9)

holds, where
(\varphi,\psi)
means scalar product.

Dividing both sides (
5.1.9
) by
\gamma-\beta
and tending
\beta\to\gamma
, we obtain

\frac{\partial\lambda(\gamma)}{\partial\gamma}\cdot\left\|\varphi(\cdot,\lambda(\gamma),\gamma)\right\|^{2}=-1,

i.e. (see (
5.1.7
))

\frac{\partial\lambda(\gamma)}{\partial\gamma}=-\frac{1}{a(\gamma)}.

(5.1.10)

On the other hand, in Theorem
3.7
we obtained the representation of the normalization constants
a_{n}(\alpha)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}a(\alpha-\pi n)
:

\frac{1}{a_{n}(\alpha)}=\frac{\lambda_{n}(\beta)-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}{\sin(\alpha-\beta)}\,\prod_{k=-\infty\atop{k\neq n}}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{k}(\beta)-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}

(5.1.11)

for arbitrary
\beta
, such that
-\frac{\pi}{2}<\alpha<\beta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
.
From the definition of EVF (
\lambda(\alpha-\pi n)=\lambda_{n}(\alpha)
) and equalities (
5.1.10
) and (
5.1.11
) the presentation (
5.1.5
) follows.
Theorem
5.1
is proved.
∎

Notes and references

The concept of an eigenvalues function (EVF) of a family of operators was introduced by T.N. Harutyunyan in
[
Har90
]
in 1990 for Dirac operators.

The concept of EVF for a family of Sturm-Liouville operators was introduced by T.N. Harutyunyan in 2000 in a paper
[
HN00
]
.
The properties of this function were studied in detail in paper
[
Har19a
]
and were applied for solving the inverse problem.

CHAPTER
6
Inverse problems. Uniqueness theorems.

6.1
Statements of Theorems.

Inverse spectral problems consist of recovering operators from their spectral characteristics.

For the first time, the inverse problems for Dirac systems were considered in papers
[
Mos57
,
PT59
,
Ver59
]
and were devoted to the reconstruction of singular Dirac operator (on the half axis) by so-called ”spectral function”.
More completely, this problem was solved in
[
GL66
]
.

In the regular symmetric case we consider, the spectral function,
\rho(\cdot)
(see the general definition in
[
GL66
]
and
[
Mar52
]
) of the problem (operator)
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
is defined on the whole (real) axis
\lambda
, a step-wise, increasing, left-continuous function having jumps at points
\lambda=\lambda_{n}
, equal to
\dfrac{1}{a_{n}}
(and normalized by condition
\rho(\lambda_{0})=0
).
In this case, having the spectral function is equivalent to have two sequences: a sequence of eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=\{\lambda_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and a sequence of normalization constants
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=\{a_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

In the case of the Sturm-Liouville problem by spectral function, one can uniquely and constructively recover the potential
q(\cdot)
and numbers
\alpha
and
\beta
, defining the boundary conditions.
In the case of the Dirac operator this is not true anymore (this was first noted in
[
GL66
]
).

Indeed, if
\omega(x)
is an absolutely continuous function, then the change
\psi(x,\lambda)=A(x)\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
, where the unitary matrix

A(x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\cos\omega(x)&\sin\omega(x)\\
-\sin\omega(x)&\cos\omega(x)\end{array}\right)=B\sin\omega(x)+E\cos\omega(x),

reduces the canonical system
\left\{B\frac{d}{dx}+\Omega(x)\right\}\,\varphi=\lambda\varphi
to a system
\left\{B\frac{d}{dx}+\tilde{\Omega}(x)\right\}\,\psi=\lambda\psi
, where
\tilde{\Omega}(x)=A^{-1}(x)B\cdot A^{\prime}(x)+A^{-1}(x)\Omega(x)A(x)
, but leaves the spectral function unchanged, i.e. eigenvalues and normalized constants.
The condition of being canonical, i.e. to be
\tilde{\Omega}(x)=\sigma_{2}\cdot\tilde{p}(x)+\sigma_{3}\cdot\tilde{q}(x)
, requires
A^{\prime}(x)\equiv 0
, i.e.
\omega(x)=const=\omega_{0}
.
But already a constant matrix

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\cos\omega_{0}&\sin\omega_{0}\\
-\sin\omega_{0}&\cos\omega_{0}\end{array}\right)

(*)

transforms the problem
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
into a problem
L(A^{-1}\Omega A,\alpha-\omega_{0},\beta-\omega_{0})
, and these two different problems have the same spectral function.
Fixing one of the boundary conditions assures that
\omega_{0}=0
, i.e., it reduces the set of unitary transformations of the form (
*
‣
6.1
) to the identity transformation.
Therefore, in what follows, we will consider one of the boundary conditions to be fixed (we often take
\beta=0
, i.e. the boundary condition (
3.1.3
) takes the form
y_{1}(\pi,\lambda)=0
).

Thus, it is clear that this condition is necessary for the unique solvability of the inverse problem by spectral function.
But is it sufficient?
For example, is ”the uniqueness theorem by the spectral function” valid?:

Theorem
.

If
\lambda_{n}(\Omega_{1},\alpha_{1},\beta)=\lambda_{n}(\Omega_{2},\alpha_{2},\beta)
and
a_{n}(\Omega_{1},\alpha_{1},\beta)=a_{n}(\Omega_{2},\alpha_{2},\beta)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
\Omega_{1}(x)=\Omega_{2}(x)
a.e. and
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
?

The positive answer to this question will be given in Section
6.2
.

In this section, we formulate and prove 4 uniqueness theorems in inverse problems for operator
L(p,q,\alpha,0)
,
\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
.
For the coefficients
p
and
q
, we assume that
p,q\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
.
As noted above (see Section
4.2
), in this case, the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}(p,q,\alpha,0)=\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, of operator
L(p,q,\alpha,0)
are all simple and therefore there exist constants
c_{n}=c_{n}(p,q,\alpha,0)=c_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)
, such that the eigenfunctions
\varphi_{n}=\varphi(x,\lambda_{n},\alpha)
and
u_{n}=u(x,\lambda_{n},0)
are connected by relation

u_{n}(x)=c_{n}\,\varphi_{n}(x),\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z}.

(6.1.1)

Let us note, that from the notation
a_{n}=\|\varphi_{n}\|^{2}
,
b_{n}=\|u_{n}\|^{2}
and (
6.1.1
) it follows, that

\dfrac{c_{n}^{2}}{b_{n}}=\dfrac{1}{a_{n}},\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z}.

(6.1.2)

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
p,q,\tilde{p},\tilde{q}\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
and for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)=

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\alpha}),

(6.1.3)

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{n}=c_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\alpha})=

\displaystyle\nu c_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)=\nu c_{n},\quad(\nu=const),

(6.1.4)

then
\alpha=\tilde{\alpha}
,
\nu=1
and
\Omega(x)=\tilde{\Omega}(x)
almost everywhere (a.e.) on
[0,\pi]
.

Theorem
6.1
is an analogue of a similar uniqueness theorem in inverse Sturm–Liouville problem, proved in
[
Har09
]
, but here we can write (
6.1.4
) in the form
\tilde{c}_{n}=\nu c_{n}
instead
\tilde{c}_{n}=c_{n}
in
[
Har09
]
, and after we prove, that
\nu=1
.
This theorem show that the set
\left\{c_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
we can consider as supplementary spectral data which together with
\left\{\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
uniquely defined operator
L(\Omega,\alpha)
.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
p,q,\tilde{p},\tilde{q}\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
and for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)=

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\alpha}),

(6.1.5)

\displaystyle a_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)=

\displaystyle\nu a_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\alpha}),\quad(\nu=const).

(6.1.6)

Then
\alpha=\tilde{\alpha}
,
\nu=1
and
\Omega(x)=\tilde{\Omega}(x)
a.e. on
[0,\pi]
.

Theorem
6.2
is an analogue of the famous Marchenko’s uniqueness theorem
[
Mar50
,
Mar52
,
Lev62
]
.
This theorem was announced in
[
Har94
]
, but without proof.
A similar result follows from the Theorem 1.2 of paper
[
Wat99
]
, which in our terms can be formulated in the following form.

Theorem

(
[
Wat99
]
)
.

Let
p,q\in AC[0,\pi]
and for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}

\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha,\beta)
,
a_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)=a_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha,\beta)
.
Then
\tilde{\Omega}(x)\equiv\Omega(x)
on
[0,\pi]
.

It is easy to see the difference between these theorems. See also the papers
[
Hor05
]
and
[
WW15
]
.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
p,q\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
and for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}

\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)=\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\alpha}),\qquad\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha_{1})=\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\alpha}_{1}),

(6.1.7)

where
\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha
.
Then
\alpha=\tilde{\alpha}
,

\alpha_{1}=\tilde{\alpha}_{1}
and
\Omega(x)=\tilde{\Omega}(x)
a.e. on
[0,\pi]
.

It is natural to call this theorem ”Borg uniqueness theorem”, since it is similar to the case of Sturm–Liouville problem (see
[
Zhi67
,
Lev73
,
Bor46
,
LS88
]
).

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
p,q\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
and for some fixed
n_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
and for a distinct convergent sequence
\left\{\alpha_{k}\right\}_{k=1}^{\infty}

\left(-{\pi}/{2}<\alpha_{k}\leqslant{\pi}/{2}\right)

\lambda_{n_{0}}(\Omega,\alpha_{k})=\lambda_{n_{0}}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha_{k}),\quad k=1,2,3,\ldots\,.

(6.1.8)

Then
\Omega(x)=\tilde{\Omega}(x)
a.e. on
[0,\pi]
.

Theorem
6.4
is an analogue of McLaughlin–Rundell Theorem (see
[
Lev49
]
) for Sturm–Liouville inverse problem.

6.2
The proof of Theorem
6.1

According to condition (
6.1.3
) and asymptotics (
3.1.4
) we have

\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)=n-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}+o(1)=n-\frac{\tilde{\alpha}}{\pi}+o(1)=\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\alpha})

when
n\to\pm\infty
. It follows, that
\alpha=\tilde{\alpha}
.

It is known (see
[
AHM05
]
,
[
GL66
]
and Chapter
2
) that there exist the transformation operator
U=E+\mathbb{K}
, which transform the solution
\varphi(x,\lambda)
of Caushy problem
\ell y=\lambda y
,
y(0,\lambda)=(\sin\alpha,-\cos\alpha)^{T}
to the solution
\tilde{\varphi}(x,\lambda)
of Caushy problem
\tilde{\ell}y\equiv By^{\prime}+\tilde{\Omega}(x)y=\lambda y
,
y(0,\lambda)=(\sin\alpha,-\cos\alpha)^{T}
, i.e.

\tilde{\varphi}(x,\lambda)=(E+\mathbb{K})\varphi=\varphi(x,\lambda)+\int\limits^{x}_{0}K(x,t)\varphi(t,\lambda)\,dt,

(6.2.1)

where the kernel
K(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
have the properties,
described in
[
AHM05
]
(see also
[
GL66
]
and Chapter
2
).
Also (see
[
Har08
]
and Section
2.5
) there exist transformation operator

V=E+\mathbb{H}
, which transfer the solution

u(x,\lambda)=u(x,\lambda,\Omega)
to solution

\tilde{u}(x,\lambda)=u(x,\lambda,\tilde{\Omega})
:

\tilde{u}(x,\lambda)=(E+\mathbb{H})u=u(x,\lambda)+\int\limits^{\pi}_{x}H(x,t)u(t,\lambda)\,dt=Vu.

(6.2.2)

Besides, it is known (see Theorem
4.4
, Definition
4.1
and Section
2.5
),
that each system of eigenfunctions

\left\{\varphi_{n}\right\}_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}
,

\left\{\tilde{\varphi}_{n}\right\}_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}
,

\left\{u_{n}\right\}_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}
and

\left\{\tilde{u}_{n}\right\}_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}

form a complete system in
L^{2}([0,\pi];\mathbb{C}^{2})
(see Chapter
4
), i.e., the expansions

\displaystyle f(x)=\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=-\infty}\frac{1}{a_{n}}(f,\varphi_{n})\varphi_{n}(x),\quad f(x)=\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=-\infty}\frac{1}{\tilde{a}_{n}}(f,\tilde{\varphi}_{n})\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(x),

(6.2.3)

\displaystyle f(x)=\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=-\infty}\frac{1}{b_{n}}(f,u_{n})u_{n}(x),\quad f(x)=\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=-\infty}\frac{1}{\tilde{b}_{n}}(f,\tilde{u}_{n})\tilde{u}_{n}(x)

(6.2.4)

converges to
f
in
L^{2}
-sense for arbitrary
f\in L^{2}([0,\pi];\mathbb{C}^{2})
.

Applying now operator
U=E+\mathbb{K}
to the both sides of (
6.2.3
), take into account (
6.2.1
) and also that we can apply
U
term-wise, (since under condition
p,q\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
, it is easy to prove, that operator
U
is ”weakly bounded” (see Section
2.7
), we obtain

Uf=\sum\limits^{\infty}_{k=-\infty}\frac{1}{a_{n}}(f,\varphi_{n})U\varphi_{n}=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{a_{n}}(f,\varphi_{n})\tilde{\varphi}_{n}.

(6.2.5)

Applying now operator
V=\mathbb{E}+\mathbb{H}
to the both sides of (
6.2.4
), taking into account (
6.1.1
), (
6.1.2
), (
6.1.4
), (
6.2.2
), (
6.2.5
) and applying
V
term-wise yields

\displaystyle Vf=

\displaystyle f+\mathbb{H}f=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{b_{n}}(f,u_{n})Vu_{n}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{b_{n}}(f,c_{n}\varphi_{n})\tilde{u}_{n}=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\bar{c}_{n}}{b_{n}}(f,\varphi_{n})\tilde{c}_{n}\tilde{\varphi}_{n}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\bar{c}_{n}c_{n}}{b_{n}}(f,\varphi_{n})\tilde{\varphi}_{n}=\nu\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{a_{n}}(f,\varphi_{n})\tilde{\varphi}_{n}=\nu Uf.

So, we have

f+\mathbb{H}f=\nu Uf=\nu f+\nu\mathbb{K}f,
or, the same

(1-\nu)f(x)+\int\limits^{\pi}_{x}H(x,t)f(t)\,dt=\nu\int\limits^{x}_{0}K(x,t)f(t)dt.

(6.2.6)

Since (
6.2.6
) hold for arbitrary
f\in L^{2}([0,\pi],\mathbb{C}^{2})
, it follows that
K(x,t)=0
a.e. for
t\in(0,x]
,

H(x,t)=0
a.e. for
x<t\leqslant\pi
and
\nu=1
. From
(
6.2.1
) we have obtain that
\tilde{\varphi}(x,\lambda)=\varphi(x,\lambda)
for all
x\in[0,\pi]
, and therefore

[\Omega(x)-\tilde{\Omega}(x)]\varphi(x,\lambda)=0
a.e. on

(0,\pi)
. Since
\varphi(x,\lambda)
is continuous and

\varphi(x,\lambda)\not\equiv 0
, we obtain, that

\tilde{\Omega}(x)=\Omega(x)
a.e. on
[0,\pi]
. Theorem

6.1
is proved.

6.3
The proof of Theorem
6.2

As we note above, it follows from condition (
6.1.3
), that
\alpha=\tilde{\alpha}
.
Also it follows from
u(\pi,\lambda)=(0,-1)^{T}
and (
6.1.2
) that
\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha),\alpha)=\frac{1}{c_{n}}u_{n1}(\pi)=0
. So, from equality

a_{n}=\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{n})\dot{\varphi}_{2}(\pi,\lambda_{n})-\dot{\varphi}_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{n})\varphi_{2}(\pi,\lambda_{n})

(see (
4.2.3
)) will follow, that

\displaystyle a_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)=-\dot{\varphi}_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha),\alpha)\varphi_{2}(\pi,\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle=-\dot{\varphi}_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha),\alpha)\frac{u_{2}(\pi,\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha))}{c_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)}=\frac{\dot{\varphi}_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha),\alpha,\Omega)}{c_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)}.

(6.3.1)

If we prove that from condition (
6.1.3
)
follow

\dot{\varphi}_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha),\alpha,\Omega)=\dot{\varphi}_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha),\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\Omega}),

(6.3.2)

n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then from (
6.3.1
) and similar equality

a_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha,0)=\frac{\dot{\varphi}_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha),\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\Omega})}{\tilde{c}_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha)}

and condition (
6.1.4
):
a_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha)=\nu a_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)
will follow, that

c_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)=\nu\tilde{c}_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z},

which, according to Theorem
6.1
, give us that
\nu=1
,
and
\Omega(x)=\tilde{\Omega}(x)
a.e. So, we will prove
Theorem
6.2
, if we will prove (
6.3.2
).

According to representation

\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\left(\sin(\lambda x+\alpha)\atop-\cos(\lambda x+\alpha)\right)+\int\limits^{x}_{-x}K(x,t)e^{-B\lambda t}\left(\sin\alpha\atop-\cos\alpha\right)dt

(6.3.3)

(see (
2.3.1
)), for
\lambda=i\mu
,
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\varphi_{1}(\pi,i\mu,\alpha,\Omega)=\sin(\pi i\mu+\alpha)+\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\left[K_{11}(\pi,t)\sin(i\mu t+\alpha)-K_{12}(\pi,t)\cos(i\mu t+\alpha)\right]\,dt,

\varphi_{1}\left(\pi,i\mu,\alpha,\tilde{\Omega}\right)=\sin(\pi i\mu+\alpha)+\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\left[\tilde{K}_{11}(\pi,t)\sin(i\mu t+\alpha)-\tilde{K}_{12}(\pi,t)\cos(i\mu t+\alpha)\right]\,dt,

where
K_{ij}(\pi,\,\cdot\,)\in L^{1}(-\pi,\pi)
(
j=1,2
). It follows from a lemma of Marchenko (see

[
Mar77
]
, p.36, lemma 3.1), that

\lim\limits_{\mu\to\infty}\frac{\varphi_{1}(\pi,i\mu,\alpha,\Omega)}{\sin(\pi i\mu+\alpha)}=\lim\limits_{\mu\to\infty}\frac{\varphi_{1}\left(\pi,i\mu,\alpha,\tilde{\Omega}\right)}{\sin(\pi i\mu+\alpha)}=1

(6.3.4)

and as a corollary from the last equality, that

\lim\limits_{\mu\to\infty}\frac{\varphi_{1}(\pi,i\mu,\alpha,\Omega)}{\varphi_{1}\left(\pi,i\mu,\alpha,\tilde{\Omega}\right)}=1.

(6.3.5)

On the other hand, also from representation (
6.3.3
) follows that
\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda,\alpha,\Omega)
and

\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda,\alpha,\tilde{\Omega})

are the entire functions on
\lambda
of exponential type (i.e. of order
1
). As the genus of an entire function does not exceed its order these functions have a genus
0
or
1
(see
[
Lev71
]
or
[
Tit80
]
).
If the genus equal zero, and
\lambda_{0}(\alpha)\neq 0
, then, according to Hadamard’s theorem (see
[
Lev71
]
, p21, or
[
Tit80
]
, p. 259), we have the representation (where
c>0
)

\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda,\alpha,\Omega)=c\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=-\infty}\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\right)=c\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}\right)\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\right)\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}\right).

(6.3.6)

If
\lambda_{0}(\alpha)=0
, then instead of factor

\left(1-\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}\right)
in (
6.3.6
)
we must write
\lambda
. If the genus equals
1
and

\lambda_{0}(\alpha)\neq 0
, then we have representation

\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda,\alpha,\Omega)=e^{a\lambda+b}\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=-\infty}\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\right)e^{\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}}

=e^{a\lambda+b}\,e^{\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}}\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}\right)\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\right)\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{-k}(\lambda)}\right)\,e^{\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}+\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}}.

(6.3.7)

If
\lambda_{0}(\alpha)=0
, then instead of factor

e^{\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}}\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}\right)
in (
6.3.7
),
we must write
\lambda
(see
[
MA77
]
).

Lemma 6.1
.

The characteristic function

\chi(\lambda)=\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda,\alpha,\Omega)
of problem

L(\Omega,\alpha,0)
uniquely defined by spectra

\left\{\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,0)\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=\left\{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that in the case (
6.3.6
) the number
c
and in the case (
6.3.7
) – the numbers
a
and
b
uniquely defined by spectra

\left\{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

Let us first consider the case (
6.3.6
). Using the well-known formula

\sin\pi z=\pi z\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\left(1-\frac{z^{2}}{k^{2}}\right)

and
(
6.3.6
), we can write

\frac{\varphi_{1}(\pi,i\mu,\alpha,\Omega)}{\sin(\pi i\mu+\alpha)}=\frac{c\left(1-\frac{i\mu}{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}\right)\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\left(1-\frac{i\mu}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\right)\left(1-\frac{i\mu}{\lambda_{-k}(\lambda)}\right)}{\pi\left(i\mu+\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\right)\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\left(1-\frac{i\mu+\frac{\alpha}{\pi}}{k}\right)\left(1-\frac{i\mu+\frac{\alpha}{\pi}}{-k}\right)}\,.

(6.3.8)

∎

Our aim is to compute the value of
c
from (
6.3.8
) by using the relation (
6.3.4
). Before continuing, we formulate two lemmas (we omit the proof of these assertions since they are long,
but elementally follow from asymptotics of

\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)
).

Lemma A.1.

For
p,q\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]

the infinite product
\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{k^{2}}{-\lambda_{k}(\alpha)\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}
converge.

Lemma A.2.

For
p,q\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]

the infinite product

\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\left|\frac{\left(\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu\right)\left(\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)-i\mu\right)}{\left(k-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}-i\mu\right)\left(-k-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}-i\mu\right)}\right|

converge uniformly by
\mu\geqslant 1
(
\mu\in[1,\infty)
) and, as corollary

\lim\limits_{\mu\to\infty}\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{\left(\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu\right)\left(\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)-i\mu\right)}{\left(k-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}-i\mu\right)\left(-k-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}-i\mu\right)}=1.

To compute
c
we rewrite (
6.3.8
) in the
form (we use Lemma A.1 and A.2)

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi_{1}(\pi,i\mu,\alpha,\Omega)}{\sin(\pi i\mu+\alpha)}=\frac{c}{\pi}\frac{\left(\frac{1}{i\mu}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}\right)}{\left(1+\frac{\alpha}{i\pi\mu}\right)}\frac{\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}}{\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{\left(k-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}-i\mu\right)\left(-k-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}-i\mu\right)}{k^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{\pi}\frac{\left(\frac{1}{i\mu}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}\right)}{\left(1+\frac{\alpha}{i\pi\mu}\right)}\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{k^{2}}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)(-\lambda_{-k}(\alpha))}\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{(\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu)(\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)-i\mu)}{\left(k-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}-i\mu\right)\left(-k-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}-i\mu\right)}

and take the logarithm of both sides (the principle value of logarithm):

\displaystyle\log\frac{\varphi_{1}(\pi,i\mu,\alpha,\Omega)}{\sin(\pi i\mu+\alpha)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}\log\left|\frac{\varphi_{1}(\pi,i\mu,\alpha,\Omega)}{\sin(\pi i\mu+\alpha)}\right|+i\arg\frac{\varphi_{1}(\pi,i\mu,\alpha,\Omega)}{\sin(\pi i\mu+\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\log\frac{c}{\pi}+\log\left|\frac{\lambda_{0}-i\mu}{\lambda_{0}\left(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}+i\mu\right)}\right|+i\arg\frac{\lambda_{0}-i\mu}{\lambda_{0}\left(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}+i\mu\right)}+\log\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{k^{2}}{\lambda_{k}(-\lambda_{-k}(\alpha))}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\left\{\log\frac{\left(\lambda^{2}_{k}+\mu^{2}\right)\left(\lambda^{2}_{-k}+\mu^{2}\right)}{\left(\left(k-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\right)^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)\left(\left(k+\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\right)^{2}+\mu^{2}\right)}+i\arg\frac{(\lambda_{k}-i\mu)(\lambda_{-k}-i\mu)}{\left(k-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}-i\mu\right)\left(-k-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}-i\mu\right)}\right\}.

According to (
6.3.4
) the left side of last equality tend to zero
when
\mu\to\infty
. On the right-hand side, separating the real and
imaginary parts and passing to the limit when
\mu\to\infty
, we
obtain

\log\frac{c}{\pi|\lambda_{0}|}\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{k^{2}}{\left[-\lambda_{k}(\alpha)\,\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)\right]}=0,\quad{\mbox{ i.e.}}\quad c=\pi|\lambda_{0}|\,\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{-\lambda_{k}(\alpha)\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}{k^{2}}.

Insert this
value of
c
in (
6.3.6
), we obtain

\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda,\alpha,\Omega)=\pi\,\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}\right)\,\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{\left(\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda\right)\left(\lambda-\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)\right)}{k^{2}},

i.e. characteristic
function
\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda,\alpha,\Omega)
uniquely defined
by spectra
\left\{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}

(
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
). In particular, if
\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)=\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
then
\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda,\alpha,\Omega)\equiv\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda,\alpha,\tilde{\Omega})
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.
It follows from the last identity that

\dot{\varphi}_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,0),\alpha,\Omega)=\dot{\varphi}_{1}\left(\pi,\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,0),\alpha,\tilde{\Omega}\right)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, i.e. we obtain (
6.3.2
). Thus, Theorem
6.2
proved in the case (
6.3.6
).

In the case (
6.3.7
) instead of (
6.3.8
) we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi_{1}(\pi,i\mu,\alpha,\Omega)}{\sin(\pi i\mu+\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{ai\mu+\frac{i\mu}{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}+b}\left(1-\frac{i\mu}{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}\right)}{\pi\left(i\mu+\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\right)}\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{k^{2}}{\lambda_{k}(-\lambda_{-k})}\times

\displaystyle\times\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{\left(\lambda_{k}-i\mu\right)\left(\lambda_{-k}-i\mu\right)e^{i\mu\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{-k}}\right)}}{\left(k-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}-i\mu\right)\left(-k-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}-i\mu\right)}\,.

(6.3.9)

Since all eigenvalues are real, the modules of factors
e^{i\mu\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{-k}}\right)}

and
e^{i\mu\left(a+\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}}\right)}
equals
1
.

This implies, in particular, that the last infinite product in
(
6.3.9
) converge uniformly by
\mu\in[1,\infty)
(it is
the reiteration of Lemma A.2). Therefore, if we take the logarithm of
both sides of (
6.3.9
), separating the real and
imaginary parts, and passing to limit when
\mu\to\infty
, we obtain

\frac{e^{b}}{\pi|\lambda_{0}(\alpha)|}\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{k^{2}}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)\left(-\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)\right)}=1.

From this formula, we uniquely
defined
b
. The quantity
a
is defined from the equality of real
parts. Thus, Theorem
6.2
completely proved.

6.4
The proof of Theorem
6.3

We note that from condition (
6.1.3
) follow
\alpha=\tilde{\alpha}
. It is well known the representation of norming constants
a_{n}
by two spectra (see
[
GD75
,
Har85b
]
):

a_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,0)=\frac{\sin(\varepsilon-\alpha)}{\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)-\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\varepsilon)}\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=-\infty\atop k\neq n}\frac{\lambda_{k}(\Omega,\alpha)-\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)}{\lambda_{k}(\Omega,\varepsilon)-\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)}

and

a_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha,0)=\frac{\sin(\varepsilon-\alpha)}{\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha)-\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\varepsilon)}\prod\limits^{\infty}_{k=-\infty\atop k\neq n}\frac{\lambda_{k}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha)-\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha)}{\lambda_{k}(\tilde{\Omega},\varepsilon)-\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha)}

for arbitrary
\varepsilon\in\left(\alpha,{\pi}/{2}\right)
.
If we take as
\varepsilon

\,\alpha_{1}
from condition (
6.1.7
), we obtain the
equality of the right sides and, therefore, equality

a_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)=a_{n}\left(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha\right)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, which together with (
6.1.3
) (according to
Theorem
6.2
) give us the equality

\Omega(x)=\tilde{\Omega}(x)
a.e. on
[0,\pi]
.
Theorem
6.3
is proved.

6.5
The proof of Theorem
6.4

In Chapter
5
we introduced the concept of eigenvalues function of family of Dirac operators by formula (we give it for
\beta=0
)
\lambda(\gamma)=\lambda(\alpha-\pi n)=\lambda_{n}(\alpha),
where
\gamma\in(-\infty,\infty)
,
\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
,

n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
It was proved in Theorem
5.1
that this function is a real analytic function on
(-\infty,\infty)
.
From condition (
6.1.8
) follow that

\displaystyle\lambda_{n_{0}}(\Omega,\alpha_{k})=

\displaystyle\lambda(\alpha_{k}-\pi n_{0},\Omega)=\lambda(\gamma_{k},\Omega)=\lambda(\gamma_{k},\tilde{\Omega})=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda(\alpha_{k}-\pi n_{0},\tilde{\Omega})=\lambda_{n_{0}}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha_{k}),

and therefore,
\lambda(\gamma,\Omega)=\lambda(\gamma,\tilde{\Omega})
, for all
\gamma\in(-\infty,\infty)
as two analytic functions, which coincide on a distinct convergent sequence
\gamma_{k}=\alpha_{k}-\pi n_{0},\,k=1,2,\ldots\,.
.
In particular,

\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)=\lambda(\alpha-\pi n,\Omega)=\lambda(\alpha-\pi n,\tilde{\Omega})=\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and arbitrary
\alpha\in\left(-\dfrac{\pi}{2},\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right]
.
Similarly, for some
\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha
.
Theorem
6.4
now follows from Theorem
6.3
.

Notes and references

In 2008 in paper
[
Har09
]
T.N. Harutyunyan proved the uniqueness theorem in inverse Sturm-Liouville problem by spectra
\{\lambda_{n}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
and the ”similarity coefficients”
\{c_{n}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
.
As corollaries from this theorem, we obtain the uniqueness theorem of Marchenko and the theorem of Borg.

The analogs of these results for the Dirac system (Theorems
6.1
–
6.4
) were
published in 2019 in paper
[
Har19b
]
.

CHAPTER
7
Isospectral Dirac operators

In this section we consider Dirac operator
L(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)
with one fixed boundary condition,
\beta=0
, hence here we consider the problem
L(\Omega,\alpha,0)
.

Definition 7.1
.

Two Dirac operators
L(\Omega,\alpha,0)
and
L(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\alpha},0)
are said to be isospectral,
if
\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,0)=\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\alpha},0)
, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Let
\Omega,\tilde{\Omega}\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
and the operators
L(\Omega,\alpha,0)
and
L(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\alpha},0)
are isospectral, then the asymptotics

\lambda_{n}=n-\dfrac{\alpha}{\pi}+o(1)

(7.0.1)

brings to
\tilde{\alpha}=\alpha
.
So, instead of isospectral operators
L(\Omega,\alpha,0)
and
L(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\alpha},0)
, we can talk about ”isospectral potentials”
\Omega
and
\tilde{\Omega}
(and we will left the term
\alpha
in
\lambda_{n}
and
a_{n}
).
Let us fix some
\Omega\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
and consider the set of all canonical potentials

\tilde{\Omega}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\tilde{p}&\tilde{q}\\
\tilde{q}&-\tilde{p}\\
\end{array}\right)
,
with the same spectrum as
\Omega
:

M^{2}(\Omega)=\{\tilde{\Omega}\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]:\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\alpha},0)=\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,0),n\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

Our main goal is to give the description of the set
M^{2}(\Omega)
.
Note that the problem of description of isospectral Sturm-Liouville operators was solved in
[
IT83
,
IMT84
,
DT84
,
PT87
,
KC09
,
JL97
]
.

From the uniqueness Theorem
6.2
it easily follows:

Corollary 7.1
.

The map

\tilde{\Omega}\in M^{2}(\Omega)\leftrightarrow\{a_{n}(\tilde{\Omega}),n\in\mathbb{Z}\}

is one-to-one.

It is known that in the case of
\Omega\in W_{k,{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}[0,\pi]
, the norming constants have the following asymptotic representation

a_{n}(\Omega)=\pi+\frac{c_{1}}{n}+\frac{c_{2}}{n^{2}}+\cdots+\frac{c_{k}}{n^{k}}+\frac{c_{k,n}}{n^{k}},

(7.0.2)

where
c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}
are some constants, and
\displaystyle\sum_{n=-\infty}^{+\infty}c_{k,n}^{2}<\infty
.
Since
\tilde{\Omega}\in M^{2}(\Omega)
, then
a_{n}(\tilde{\Omega})
have similar asymptotic representation.
Insofar as
a_{n}(\Omega)
and
a_{n}(\tilde{\Omega})
are positive numbers, there exist real numbers
t_{n}=t_{n}(\tilde{\Omega})
, such that

\dfrac{a_{n}(\Omega)}{a_{n}(\tilde{\Omega})}=e^{t_{n}}
.
Hence, we have

e^{t_{n}}=1+\frac{d_{1}}{n}+\frac{d_{2}}{n^{2}}+\cdots+\frac{d_{k}}{n^{k}}+\frac{d_{k,n}}{n^{k}},

(7.0.3)

where
d_{1},d_{2},\ldots,d_{k}
– are some constants, and
\displaystyle\sum_{n=-\infty}^{+\infty}d_{k,n}^{2}<\infty
.
By
P_{k}
we denote the set of all sequences
\{t_{n};\,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
,
t_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, which have the same asymptotics as (
7.0.3
).
As all
a_{n}(\Omega)
are fixed, then from the corollary
7.1
and the equality

a_{n}(\tilde{\Omega})=a_{n}(\Omega)e^{-t_{n}}
we will get:

Corollary 7.2
.

The map

\tilde{\Omega}\in M^{2}(\Omega)\leftrightarrow\{t_{n}(\tilde{\Omega}),n\in\mathbb{Z}\}\in P_{k}

is one-to-one.

Thus, each isospectral potential is uniquely determined by a sequence
\{t_{n};n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

7.1
Changing one norming constant

At the first we give the description of a family of isospectral potentials
\Omega(x,t),t\in\mathbb{R}
, for which only one norming constant
a_{m}(\Omega(\cdot,t))
differs from
a_{m}(\Omega)
(namely,
a_{m}(\Omega(\cdot,t))=a_{m}(\Omega)e^{-t}
), while the others are equal, i.e.
a_{m}(\Omega(\cdot,t))=a_{m}(\Omega)
, when
n\neq m
.
By
h_{n}(x,\Omega)
we denote normalized eigenfunctions (i.e.
\|h_{n}(x)\|=1
) of operator
L(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)
:

h_{n}(x)=h_{n}(x,\Omega)=\dfrac{\varphi_{n}(x,\Omega)}{\sqrt{a_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)}},

(7.1.1)

It is easy to see, that
|h_{n}(0)|^{2}=\dfrac{1}{a_{n}}
.
Let
\ell(\Omega)=\{\ell_{n}(\Omega):\,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
, where

\ell_{n}(\Omega)=\ln\frac{\left|h_{n}(\pi,\Omega)\right|}{\left|h_{n}(0,\Omega)\right|}=\ln|\varphi_{n,2}(\pi,\Omega)|,

(7.1.2)

and

\theta_{n}(x,t,\Omega)=1+(e^{*}-1)\,\int_{0}^{x}\left|h_{n}(s,\Omega)\right|^{2}\,ds.

(7.1.3)

Note, in case of
\Omega\in W_{k,{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}[0,\pi]
yields
\ell(\Omega)\in P_{k}
.
Insofar as the differential expression
\ell
is self-adjoint, thus
h_{n,1}
and
h_{n,2}
can be taken real.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha\in\left(-\dfrac{\pi}{2},\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right]
and
1
1

1

Here
∗
is a sign of transponation.

\Omega(x,t)=\Omega(x)+\dfrac{e^{t}-1}{\theta_{m}(x,t,\Omega)}\{Bh_{m}(x,\Omega)h_{m}^{*}(x,\Omega)-h_{m}(x,\Omega)h_{m}^{*}(x,\Omega)B\}.

(7.1.4)

Then,

1.

for arbitrary
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\lambda_{n}(\Omega(\cdot,t))=\lambda_{n}(\Omega)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
a_{n}(\Omega(\cdot,t))=a_{n}(\Omega)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{m\}
and
a_{m}(\Omega(\cdot,t))=a_{m}(\Omega)e^{-t}
.
The normalized eigenfunctions of the problem
L(\Omega(\cdot,t),\alpha)
are given by the formulae:

h_{n}(x,\Omega(\cdot,t))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\quad\quad\ \quad\ \quad\ \dfrac{e^{t/2}}{\theta_{m}(x,t,\Omega)}h_{m}(x,\Omega),\quad\ \quad\ \quad\ \quad if\ n=m\\
h_{n}(x,\Omega)-\dfrac{(e^{*}-1)\int_{0}^{x}h^{*}_{m}(s,\Omega)h_{n}(s,\Omega)ds}{\theta_{m}(x,t,\Omega)}h_{m}(x,\Omega),if\ n\neq m;\end{array}\right.

(7.1.5)

2.

\ell_{n}(\Omega(\cdot,t))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\ell_{n}(\Omega)-t,&\mbox{for}\;n=m\\
\displaystyle\ell_{n}(\Omega),&\mbox{for}\;n\neq m;\end{array}\right.

(7.1.6)

3.

\int^{\pi}_{0}|\Omega(x,t)-\Omega(x)|\,dx=|t|.

(7.1.7)

Proof.

At first, we will prove the relation (
7.1.7
).
From (
7.1.4
) we calculate the difference
\displaystyle\Omega(x,t)-\Omega(x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Delta p&\Delta q\\
\Delta q&-\Delta p\end{array}\right)
, which is

\Delta p=\frac{e^{*}-1}{\theta_{m}(x,t,\Omega)}\cdot 2h_{m1}(x)\,h_{m2}(x)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Delta q=\frac{e^{*}-1}{\theta_{m}(x,t,\Omega)}\cdot\left(h_{m2}^{2}(x)-h_{m1}^{2}(x)\right).

Hence, we have

|\Omega(x,t)-\Omega(x)|=\sqrt{\Delta p^{2}+\Delta q^{2}}=\frac{|e^{*}-1|}{\theta_{m}(x,t,\Omega)}|h_{m}(x)|^{2}=\left({\rm sign}\,t\right)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\ln\theta_{m}(x,t,\Omega),

and

\int^{\pi}_{0}|\Omega(x,t)-\Omega(x)|\,dx=\left({\rm sign}\,t\right)\left(\ln\theta_{m}(\pi,t,\Omega)-\ln\theta_{m}(0,t,\Omega)\right)=\left({\rm sign}\,t\right)\cdot t=|t|.

Now we’ll show that for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
there are identities (here we denote
h_{n}(x,\Omega(\cdot,t))=h_{n}(x,t)
):

\displaystyle\tilde{\ell}h_{n}(x,t)\equiv\lambda_{n}(\Omega)\,h_{n}(x,t),

(7.1.8)

\displaystyle h_{n1}(0,t)\cos\alpha+h_{n2}(0,t)\sin\alpha\equiv 0,\quad h_{n1}(\pi,t)\equiv 0

(7.1.9)

where
\displaystyle\tilde{\ell}=B\frac{d}{dx}+\Omega(x,t)
.
In particular, this yields that all
\lambda_{n}(\Omega)
,
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, are also eigenvalues for the problem
\left(\Omega(\cdot,t),\alpha\right)
.
Since from (
7.1.7
) it follows
\Omega(\cdot,t)\in L^{1}_{{\mathbb{R}}}[0,\pi]
, thus the problem
\left(\Omega(\cdot,t),\alpha\right)
does not have any other eigenvalues, because it will contradict the asymptotics (
3.1.4
).
In this way it will be proved that
\Omega(\cdot,t)
and
\Omega
are isospectral, for every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let’s show (
7.1.8
), for
n=m
(here we denote
\theta_{m}(x,t,\Omega)=\theta
,
h_{n}(x,\Omega)=h_{n}(x)=h_{n}
):

\displaystyle\tilde{\ell}h_{m}(x,t)=

\displaystyle\left\{B\frac{d}{dx}+\Omega(x)+\frac{e^{*}-1}{\theta}\left(Bh_{m}\cdot h_{m}^{*}-h_{m}\cdot h_{m}^{*}B\right)\right\}\cdot\frac{e^{\frac{t}{2}}}{\theta}h_{m}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{\frac{t}{2}}}{\theta}\left[Bh^{\prime}_{m}+\Omega(x)h_{m}\right]+

\displaystyle+\frac{e^{\frac{t}{2}}}{\theta}\left[-\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{\theta}Bh_{m}+\frac{(e^{*}-1)|h_{m}|^{2}}{\theta}Bh_{m}-\frac{(e^{*}-1)}{\theta}h_{m}\cdot h_{m}^{*}Bh_{m}\right].

From definition (
7.1.3
) it follows that
\theta^{\prime}_{m}=(e^{*}-1)|h_{m}(x)|^{2}
.
It is also obvious that
h^{*}_{m}Bh_{m}=0
.
Taking into account latter relations and that
Bh^{\prime}_{m}+\Omega(x)h_{m}=\lambda_{m}(\Omega)h_{m}
, we get (
7.1.8
), for
n=m
.

Now, the case
n\neq m
.
Then

\displaystyle\qquad\ \qquad\tilde{\ell}h_{n}(x,t)=Bh_{n}^{\prime}+\Omega(x)h_{n}-\frac{(e^{*}-1)}{\theta}\times

\displaystyle\times\left\{(h_{m}^{*}h_{n})\cdot Bh_{m}-Bh_{m}(h^{*}_{m}\cdot h_{m})+h_{m}h^{*}_{m}Bh_{n}+\int_{0}^{x}h^{*}_{m}\cdot h_{n}\,ds(Bh^{\prime}_{m}+\Omega(x)h_{m})\right\}=

\displaystyle\qquad\ \qquad=\lambda_{n}(\Omega)h_{n}-\frac{(e^{*}-1)}{\theta}\left\{h_{m}^{*}Bh_{n}+\lambda_{m}\int_{0}^{x}h^{*}_{m}h_{n}\,ds\right\}h_{m}.

In order from this to come to the identity (
7.1.8
), it is enough to show

h_{m}^{*}Bh_{n}+\lambda_{m}\int_{0}^{x}h^{*}_{m}(s)h_{n}(s)\,ds=\lambda_{n}\int_{0}^{x}h^{*}_{m}(s)h_{n}(s)\,ds.

(7.1.10)

For this, we write

\displaystyle Bh^{\prime}_{n}(s)+\Omega(s)h_{n}(s)=\lambda_{n}h_{n}(s),

(7.1.11)

\displaystyle\left(Bh^{\prime}_{m}(s)\right)^{*}+\left(\Omega(s)h_{m}(s)\right)^{*}=\lambda_{m}h^{*}_{m}(s).

(7.1.12)

Multiplying (
7.1.11
) from left by
h^{*}_{m}(s)
,and (
7.1.12
) – from right by
h_{n}(s)
and subtract the second from the first equality.
Given that
h^{*}_{m}\Omega h_{n}=\left(\Omega h_{m}\right)^{*}\cdot h_{n}
, we get that

h^{*}_{m}(s)Bh^{\prime}_{n}(s)-\left(Bh^{\prime}_{m}(s)\right)^{*}\cdot h_{n}(s)=\left(\lambda_{n}-\lambda_{m}\right)h^{*}_{m}(s)h_{n}(s).

Integrating this identity from
0
to
x
, we get (
7.1.10
).
It follows from (
7.1.5
) that
h_{n}(x,\Omega(\cdot,t))
satisfies the boundary conditions (
7.1.9
).
It remains to check whether the eigenfunctions (
7.1.5
) normalized or not.
Let
n=m
, then

\displaystyle\int^{\pi}_{0}|h_{m}(x,\Omega(\cdot,t))|^{2}\,dx=

\displaystyle e^{*}\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{|h_{m}(x)|^{2}}{\left(1+(e^{*}-1)\int_{0}^{x}|h_{m}(s)|^{2}\,ds\right)^{2}}\,dx=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{e^{*}}{e^{*}-1}\int^{\pi}_{0}\left(\frac{d}{dx}\theta^{-1}\right)\,dx=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{e^{*}}{e^{*}-1}\left(\frac{1}{\theta(\pi,t)}-\frac{1}{\theta(0,t)}\right)=-\frac{e^{*}}{e^{*}-1}\left(\frac{1}{e^{*}}-1\right)=1.

Let now
n\neq m
. Denoting the
H_{mn}(x)=\int_{0}^{x}h^{*}_{m}(s)h_{n}(s)\,ds
and noting that
H^{\prime}_{mn}(x)=h^{*}_{m}(x)h_{n}(x)
, we get:

\displaystyle|h_{n}(x,\Omega(\cdot,t))|^{2}=

\displaystyle h^{*}_{n}(x,\Omega(\cdot,t))\cdot h_{n}(x,\Omega(\cdot,t))=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|h_{n}(x)|^{2}-\frac{2(e^{*}-1)}{\theta}H_{mn}\cdot h^{*}_{m}\cdot h_{n}+\frac{(e^{*}-1)^{2}}{\theta^{2}}H_{mn}^{2}\cdot|h_{m}|^{2}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|h_{n}(x)|^{2}-(e^{*}-1)\left(\frac{H^{2}_{mn}}{\theta}\right)^{\prime}.

Since
H_{mn}(\pi)=H_{mn}(0)=0
, then

\int^{\pi}_{0}\left|h_{n}(x,\Omega(\cdot,t))\right|^{2}\,dx=\int^{\pi}_{0}|h_{n}(x)|^{2}\,dx-(e^{*}-1)\left(\frac{H^{2}_{mn}(\pi)}{\theta(\pi,t)}-\frac{H^{2}_{mn}(0)}{\theta(0,t)}\right)=1.

To prove (
7.1.6
), note that, as follows from (
7.1.5
), at
n\neq m
we have
h_{n}(\pi,\Omega(\cdot,t))=h_{n}(\pi,\Omega)
and
h_{n}(0,\Omega(\cdot,t))=h_{n}(0,\Omega)
.
It follows that
\ell_{n}(\Omega(\cdot,t))=\ell_{n}(\Omega)
, and at
n=m\leftrightarrow h_{m}(\pi,\Omega(\cdot,t))=e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\,h_{m}(\pi,\Omega)
and
h_{m}(0,\Omega(\cdot,t)=e^{\frac{t}{2}}\,h_{m}(0,\Omega)
.
Thus, we get

\ell_{m}(\Omega(\cdot,t))=\ln\left(e^{-t}\frac{|h_{m}(\pi,\Omega)|}{|h_{m}(0,\Omega)|}\right)=l_{m}(\Omega)-t.

∎

Theorem
7.1
shows that it is possible to change exactly one norming constant (or one element of the sequence
\ell(\Omega)
), keeping the others unchanged.
As an example of isospectral potentials
\Omega
and
\tilde{\Omega}
we can present

\Omega(x)\equiv 0=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
0&0\\
\end{array}\right)

and

\tilde{\Omega}(x)=\Omega_{m,t}(x)=\dfrac{\pi(e^{*}-1)}{\pi+(e^{*}-1)x}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\sin 2mx&\cos 2mx\\
\cos 2mx&\sin 2mx\\
\end{array}\right),

(7.1.13)

where
t\in\mathbb{R}
is an arbitrary real number and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
is an arbitrary integer.

7.2
Changing all norming constants

7.2.1
Recurrent description

Changing successively each
a_{m}(\Omega)
by
a_{m}(\Omega)e^{-t_{m}}
, we can obtain any isospectral potential, corresponding to the sequence
\{t_{m};m\in\mathbb{Z}\}\in P_{k}
.
It follows from the uniqueness Theorem
6.2
that the sequence in which we change the norming constants is unimportant.
That’s why we can change it in a convenient sequence.
We will use the following notations:

T_{-1}=\{\ldots,0,\ldots\}
,

T_{0}=\{\ldots,0,\ldots,0,t_{0},0,\ldots,0,\ldots\}
,

T_{1}=\{\ldots,0,\ldots,0,0,t_{0},t_{1},0,\ldots,0,\ldots\}
,

T_{2}=\{\ldots,0,\ldots,0,t_{-1},t_{0},t_{1},0,\ldots,0,\ldots\}
,

…,

T_{2n}=\{\ldots,0,0,t_{-n},\ldots,t_{-1},t_{0},t_{1},\ldots,t_{n-1},t_{n},0,\ldots\}
,

T_{2n+1}=\{\ldots,0,t_{-n},t_{-n+1},\ldots,t_{-1},t_{0},t_{1},\ldots,t_{n},t_{n+1},0,\ldots\}
,

….

Let
\Omega(x,T_{-1})\equiv\Omega(x)
and

\Omega(x,T_{m})=\Omega(x,T_{m-1})+\bigtriangleup\Omega(x,T_{m}),\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots,

(7.2.1)

where

\bigtriangleup\Omega(x,T_{m})=\dfrac{e^{t_{\tilde{m}}}-1}{\theta_{m}(x,t_{\tilde{m}},\Omega(\cdot,T_{m-1}))}[Bh_{\tilde{m}}(x,\Omega(\cdot,T_{m-1}))h_{\tilde{m}}^{*}(\cdot)-h_{\tilde{m}}(\cdot)h_{\tilde{m}}^{*}(\cdot)B],

(7.2.2)

where
\tilde{m}=\dfrac{m+1}{2}
, if
m
is odd and
\tilde{m}=-\dfrac{m}{2}
, if
m
is even.
The arguments in others
h_{\tilde{m}}(\cdot)
and
h_{\tilde{m}}^{*}(\cdot)
are the same as in the first.

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
T=\{t_{n},n\in\mathbb{Z}\}\in l^{2}
and
\Omega\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
.
Then

1.

\Omega(x,T)\equiv\Omega(x)+\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\bigtriangleup\Omega(x,T_{m})\in M^{2}(\Omega).

(7.2.3)

\ell(\Omega(\cdot,T))=l(\Omega)-T\,.

(7.2.4)

2.

The mapping (
7.2.3
)
T\longmapsto\Omega(\cdot,T)
is one-to-one, and if
\tilde{\Omega}\in M^{2}_{k}(\Omega)
, then

\tilde{\Omega}=\Omega(\cdot,\ell(\Omega)-\ell(\tilde{\Omega})),

(7.2.5)

i.e. the map
M^{2}_{k}(\Omega)\ni\tilde{\Omega}\longmapsto\ell(\Omega)-\ell(\tilde{\Omega})\in P_{k}
is the inverse to mapping (
7.2.3
).
