Proof.

Let in Theorem
7.1

t=t_{0}
.
Then, based on the initial potential
\Omega(x)=\Omega(x,T_{-1})
, by the formula (
7.1.4
) we get the isospectral potential
\Omega(x,T_{0})
.
Based on
\Omega(x,T_{0})
, quite similarly, by the formula (
7.2.1
) we get the potential
\Omega(x,T_{1})
, which has two normalizations, namely
a_{0}(\Omega(T_{1}))=a_{0}(\Omega(T_{0}))=a_{0}(\Omega)e^{-t_{0}}
and
a_{1}(\Omega(T_{1}))=a_{1}(\Omega(T_{0}))\,e^{-t_{1}}=a_{1}(\Omega)e^{-t_{1}}
differ from the norming constants
\Omega
.
By induction, based on the potential of
\Omega(T_{m-1})
, according to the formulas (
7.2.1
) and (
7.2.2
) we get the isospectral potential
\Omega(T_{m})
, which already has
m+1
of norming constants differ from the norming constants
\Omega
.
Continuing this process at infinitum (
T_{m}\to T
, when
m\to\infty
), we will come to the potential
\Omega(x,T)
(see (
7.2.3
)), having spectral data
\{\lambda_{n}(\Omega);\,a_{n}(\Omega(T))=a_{n}(\Omega)e^{-t_{n}};\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.
To prove that
\Omega(\cdot,T)\in M^{2}_{k}(\Omega)
, it remains to show that
\Omega(\cdot,T)\in W_{k,\mathbb{R}}^{2}[0,\pi]
.
For this, first note that from
\Omega\in W_{k,\mathbb{R}}^{2}[0,\pi]
it follows that
a_{n}(\Omega)
have asymptotic (
7.0.2
), and since
T\in P_{k}
, then
a_{n}(\Omega(T))=a_{n}(\Omega)e^{-t_{n}}
have the same asymptotics. Secondly, let’s use the following result of M.G. Gasimov and T.T. Jabiyev.

Theorem
.

(
[
GD75
]
).
In order for the sequences
\{\lambda_{n}\}_{-\infty}^{\infty}
and
\{a_{n}\}_{-\infty}^{\infty}
to be, respectively, eigenvalues and norming constants of the boundary values problem (
3.1.1
)-(
3.1.3
) with the potential
\Omega
of the form
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}p&q\\
q&-p\end{array}\right)
, where
p,q\in W_{k,\mathbb{R}}^{2}[0,\pi]
, it is necessary and sufficient that the asymptotic formulas (
7.0.1
) and (
7.0.2
) hold, respectively, and
\lambda_{n}\neq\lambda_{m}
at
n\neq m
, all
a_{n}>0
, and that all derivatives of orders
k
of of the matrix-function

F(x,t)=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{+\infty}\left\{\frac{1}{a_{n}}\,\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda_{n})\varphi_{0}^{*}(t,\lambda_{n})-\frac{1}{\pi}\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda^{0}_{n})\varphi_{0}^{*}(t,\lambda_{n}^{0})\right\},

where
\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin(\lambda x+\alpha)\\
-\cos(\lambda x+\alpha)\end{array}\right)
,
\lambda^{0}_{n}=n-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}
, belong to
L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}\left([0,\pi]\times[0,\pi]\right)
.

Since the asymptotic formulas (
7.0.1
) and (
7.0.2
) are valid, it follows from this theorem that
\Omega(\cdot,T)\in W_{k,\mathbb{R}}^{2}[0,\pi]
(the statement about
F(x,t)
can be checked in the same way as in
[
GD75
]
).
Equality
\ell(\cdot,T))=\ell(\Omega)-T
is proved by induction, by repeating the reasoning given in the proof (
7.1.7
) of the theorem
7.1
.
Thus, the first statement of Theorem
7.2
proved.

It is known (see Theorem
2.2
), there is a transformation operator
I+K
, representing the solution
\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)
of the Cauchy problem (
2.1.13
) in the form of

\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda,\alpha)+\int_{0}^{x}K_{0}(x,t)\varphi_{0}(t,\lambda,\alpha)\,dt

through
\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda,\alpha)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin(\lambda x+\alpha)\\
-\cos(\lambda x+\alpha)\end{array}\right)
and the matrix
K_{0}(x,t)
.
In this case, if
\Omega\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
, then
K_{0}(x,\cdot)\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,x)
, and if
\Omega\in W_{k,\mathbb{R}}^{2}[0,\pi]
, then
K_{0}(x,\cdot)\in W^{2}_{k,\mathbb{R}}(0,x)
for any
0<x\leqslant\pi
.

We can also show that, if
\Omega\in W_{k,\mathbb{R}}^{2}[0,\pi]
, then
\{\ell_{n}(\Omega)\}\in P_{k}
.
The proof of this follows from the presentation

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}(\pi,\lambda_{n},\alpha)=

\displaystyle-\cos(\lambda_{n}\pi+\alpha)+

\displaystyle+\int^{\pi}_{0}\left[K_{21}(\pi,t)\sin(\lambda_{n}t+\alpha)-K_{22}(\pi,t)\cos(\lambda_{n}t+\alpha)\right]\,dt,

if you use the asymptotics of eigenvalues (
7.0.1
) and integrate the last integral
k
times in parts.

Now we are ready to prove the second part of Theorems
7.2
.
According to the Corollary
7.2
, each
\tilde{\Omega}\in M^{2}_{k}(\Omega)
corresponds to some sequence
\tilde{T}\in P_{k}
such that
a_{n}(\tilde{\Omega})=a_{n}(\Omega)e^{-\tilde{t}_{n}}
.
Let’s construct the potential
\Omega(\cdot,\tilde{T})
from this
\tilde{T}
, according to the formula (
7.2.3
).
From (
7.2.4
) and the fact that
\{\ell_{n}(\Omega)\}\in P_{k}
, we get
\ell\left(\Omega(\cdot,\tilde{T})\right)=\ell(\Omega)-\tilde{T}\in P_{k}
.
It is also obvious that
a_{n}(\Omega(\cdot,\tilde{T}))=a_{n}(\Omega)e^{-\tilde{t}_{n}}=a_{n}(\tilde{\Omega})
.
Hence, according to the uniqueness theorem,
\tilde{\Omega}(x)=\Omega\left(x,\tilde{T}\right)
almost everywhere.
Hence,
\ell(\tilde{\Omega})=\ell\left(\Omega(\cdot,\tilde{T})\right)=\ell(\Omega)-\tilde{T}
, and
\tilde{T}=\ell(\Omega)-\ell(\tilde{\Omega})
.
Theorem
7.2
is proved.
∎

We see, that each potential matrix
\Delta\Omega(x,T_{m})
defined by normalized eigenfunctions
h_{\tilde{m}}(x,\Omega(x,T_{m-1}))
of the previous operator
L(\Omega(\cdot,T_{m-1}),\alpha)
.
This approach we call ”recurrent” description.

Theorem
7.2
describes all isospectral potentials in terms of normalized eigenfunctions
h_{n}
of potential
\Omega
and sequences
T\in P_{k}
.
From Theorem
7.2
we can conclude that in Marchenko’s uniqueness theorem, it is possible to replace
a_{n}(\Omega)
with
\ell_{n}(\Omega)
, that is, we expect that the following uniqueness theorem takes place.

Theorem 7.3
.

The mapping

\Omega\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]\longmapsto\{\lambda_{n}(\Omega),\ell_{n}(\Omega);\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\}

is one-to-one.

Proof.

Let
\lambda_{n}(\Omega_{1})=\lambda_{n}(\Omega_{2})
and
\ell_{n}(\Omega_{1})=\ell_{n}(\Omega_{2})
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
It follows that
\Omega_{2}\in M_{0}^{2}(\Omega_{1})
.
Denote
e^{t_{n}}=\frac{a_{n}(\Omega_{1})}{a_{n}(\Omega_{2})}
.
According to (
7.2.4
)
\ell(\Omega_{2})=\ell(\Omega_{1})-T
and, therefore,
T=\{0\}
, i.e.
t_{n}=0
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Thus,
a_{n}(\Omega_{1})=a_{n}(\Omega_{2})
, and according to Marchenko’s theorem
\Omega_{1}(x)=\Omega_{2}(x)
almost everywhere.
∎

7.2.2
Explicit description

We want to give a description of the set
M^{2}(\Omega)
only in terms of eigenfunctions
h_{n}(x,\Omega)
of the initial operator
L(\Omega,\alpha,0)
and sequence
T\in l^{2}
.
With this aim, let us denote by
N(T_{m})
the set of the positions of the numbers in
T_{m}
, which are not necessary zero, i.e.

N(T_{0})=\{0\}
,

N(T_{1})=\{0,1\}
,

N(T_{2})=\{-1,0,1\}
,

…,

N(T_{2n})=\{-n,-(n-1),\ldots,0,\ldots,n-1,n\}
,

N(T_{2n+1})=\{-n,-(n-1),\ldots,0,\ldots,n,n+1\}
,

…,

in particular
N(T)\equiv\mathbb{Z}
.
By
S(x,T_{m})
we denote
(m+1)\times(m+1)
square matrix

S(x,T_{m})=\Big(\delta_{ij}+(e^{t_{j}}-1)\int_{0}^{x}h_{i}^{*}(s)h_{j}(s)ds\Big)_{i,j\in N(T_{m})}

where
\delta_{ij}
is a Kronecker symbol. By
S_{p}^{(k)}(x,T_{m})
we denote a matrix, which is obtained from the matrix
S(x,T_{m})
when we replace the
k-th
column of
S(x,T_{m})
by
H_{p}(x,T_{m})=\{-(e^{t_{k}}-1)h_{k_{p}}(x)\}_{k\in N(T_{m})}
column,
p=1,2
.
Now we can formulate our result as follow:

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
T=\{t_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\in l^{2}
and
\Omega\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
.
Then the isospectral potential from
M^{2}(\Omega)
, corresponding to
T
, is given by the formula

\Omega(x,T)=\Omega(x)+G(x,x,T)B-BG(x,x,T)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}p(x,T)&q(x,T)\\
q(x,T)&-p(x,T)\\
\end{array}\right),

where

G(x,x,T)=\dfrac{1}{\det S(x,T)}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\det S_{1}^{(k)}(x,T)\\
\det S_{2}^{(k)}(x,T)\\
\end{array}\right)h_{k}^{*}(x),

and
\det S(x,T)=\displaystyle\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\det S(x,T_{m})
(the same for
\det S_{p}^{k}(x,T),p=1,2
).

In addition, for
p(x,T)
and
q(x,T)
we get an explicit representations:

\begin{array}[]{c}p(x,T)=p(x)-\dfrac{1}{\det S(x,T)}\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\displaystyle\sum_{p=1}^{2}\det S_{p}^{(k)}(x,T)h_{k_{(3-p)}}(x),\\
\\
q(x,T)=q(x)+\dfrac{1}{\det S(x,T)}\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\displaystyle\sum_{p=1}^{2}(-1)^{p-1}\det S_{p}^{(k)}(x,T)h_{k_{p}}(x).\end{array}

Proof.

The spectral function of an operator
L(\Omega,\alpha,0)
is defined as

\rho(\lambda)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle\sum_{0<\lambda_{n}\leq\lambda}\dfrac{1}{a_{n}(\Omega)},\qquad\lambda>0,\\
-\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda<\lambda_{n}\leq 0}\dfrac{1}{a_{n}(\Omega)},\qquad\lambda<0,\end{array}\right.

i.e.
\rho(\lambda)
is left-continuous, step function with jumps in points
\lambda=\lambda_{n}
equals
\dfrac{1}{a_{n}}
and
\rho(0)=0
.

Let
\Omega,\tilde{\Omega}\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
and they are isospectral.
It is known (see
[
GL66
,
LS88
,
AHM05
,
Har08
]
), that there exists a function
G(x,y)
such that:

\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha,\tilde{\Omega})=\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha,\Omega)+\int_{0}^{x}G(x,s)\varphi(s,\lambda,\alpha,\Omega)dt.

It is also known (see, e.g.,
[
GL66
,
LS88
,
AHM05
]
), that the function
G(x,y)
satisfies to the Gel’fand-Levitan integral equation:

G(x,y)+F(x,y)+\int^{x}_{0}G(x,s)F(s,y)ds=0,\quad 0\leq y\leq x,

(7.2.6)

where

F(x,y)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\varphi(x,\lambda,\alpha,\Omega)\varphi^{*}(y,\lambda,\alpha,\Omega)d[\tilde{\rho}(\lambda)-\rho(\lambda)],

If the potential
\tilde{\Omega}
from
M^{2}(\Omega)
is such that only finite norming constants of the operator
L(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha,0)
are different from the norming constants of the operator
L(\Omega,\alpha,0)
, i.e.
a_{n}(\tilde{\Omega})=a_{n}(\Omega)e^{-t_{n}},n\in N(T_{m})
and the others are equal, then we have

d\tilde{\rho}(\lambda)-d\rho(\lambda)=\displaystyle\sum_{k\in N(T_{m})}\Big(\dfrac{1}{\tilde{a_{k}}}-\dfrac{1}{a_{k}}\Big)\delta(\lambda-\lambda_{k})d\lambda=\displaystyle\sum_{k\in N(T_{m})}\Big(\dfrac{e^{t_{k}}-1}{a_{k}}\Big)\delta(\lambda-\lambda_{k})d\lambda,

where
\delta
is Dirac
\delta
-function. In this case, the kernel
F(x,y)
can be written in the form of a finite sum (using notation (
7.1.1
)):

F(x,y)=F(x,y,T_{m})=\displaystyle\sum_{k\in N(T_{m})}(e^{t_{k}}-1)h_{k}(x,\Omega)h_{k}^{*}(y,\Omega),

(7.2.7)

and consequently, the integral equation (
7.2.6
) becomes an integral equation with the degenerated kernel, i.e., it becomes a system of linear equations, and we look for the solution in the following form:

G(x,y,T_{m})=\displaystyle\sum_{k\in N(T_{m})}g_{k}(x)h_{k}^{*}(y),

(7.2.8)

where
g_{k}(x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g_{k_{1}}(x)\\
g_{k_{2}}(x)\\
\end{array}\right)

is unknown vector-function.
Putting the expressions (
7.2.7
) and (
7.2.8
) into the integral equation (
7.2.6
) we will obtain
a system of algebraic equations for determining the functions
g_{k}(x)
:

g_{k}(x)+\displaystyle\sum_{i\in N(T_{m})}s_{ik}(x)g_{i}(x)=-(e^{t_{k}}-1)h_{k}(x),\quad k\in N(T_{m}),

(7.2.9)

where

s_{ik}(x)=(e^{t_{k}}-1)\int_{0}^{x}h_{i}^{*}(s)h_{k}(s)ds.

It would be better if we consider the equations (
7.2.9
) for the vectors

g_{k}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g_{k_{1}}\\
g_{k_{2}}\\
\end{array}\right)

by coordinates
g_{k_{1}}
and
g_{k_{2}}
to be a system of scalar linear equations:

g_{k_{p}}(x)+\displaystyle\sum_{i\in N(T_{m})}s_{ik}(x)g_{i_{p}}(x)=-(e^{t_{k}}-1)h_{k_{p}}(x),\quad k\in N(T_{m}),\quad p=1,2.

(7.2.10)

The systems (
7.2.10
) might be written in matrix form

S(x,T_{m})g_{p}(x,T_{m})=H_{p}(x,T_{m}),\qquad p=1,2,

where the column vectors
g_{p}(x,T_{m})=\{g_{k_{p}}(x,T_{m})\}_{k\in N(T_{m})},\ p=1,2
, and the solution can be found in the form (Cramer’s rule):

g_{k_{p}}(x,T_{m})=\dfrac{\det S_{p}^{(k)}(x,T_{m})}{\det S(x,T_{m})},\quad k\in N(T_{m}),\quad p=1,2.

Thus we have obtained for
g_{k}(x)
the following representation:

g_{k}(x,T_{m})=\dfrac{1}{\det S(x,T_{m})}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\det S_{1}^{(k)}(x,T_{m})\\
\det S_{2}^{(k)}(x,T_{m})\\
\end{array}\right)

(7.2.11)

and then by putting (
7.2.11
) into (
7.2.8
) we find the function
G(x,y,T_{m})
.
If the potential
\Omega
is from
L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}
, then such is also the kernel
G(x,x,T_{m})
(see
[
Har94
]
), and the relation between them gives as follow:

\Omega(x,T_{m})=\Omega(x)+G(x,x,T_{m})B-BG(x,x,T_{m}).

(7.2.12)

On the other hand, we have

\Omega(x,T_{m})=\Omega(x)+\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}\bigtriangleup\Omega(x,T_{k}).

(7.2.13)

So, using the Theorem
7.2
and the equality (
7.2.13
) we can pass to the limit in (
7.2.12
), when
m\rightarrow\infty
:

\Omega(x,T)=\Omega(x)+G(x,x,T)B-BG(x,x,T).

(7.2.14)

The potentials
\Omega
in (
7.2.3
) and (
7.2.14
) have the same spectral data
\{\lambda_{n}(T),a_{n}(T)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, and therefore they are the same and
\Omega(\cdot,T)
defined by (
7.2.14
) is also from
M^{2}(\Omega)
.

Using (
7.2.8
) and (
7.2.11
) we calculate the expression
G(x,x,T_{m})B-BG(x,x,T_{m})
and pass to the limit, obtaining for the
p(x,T)
and
q(x,T)
the representations:

\begin{array}[]{c}p(x,T)=p(x)-\dfrac{1}{\det S(x,T)}\displaystyle\sum_{k\in N(T)}\displaystyle\sum_{p=1}^{2}\det S_{p}^{(k)}(x,T)h_{k_{(3-p)}}(x),\\
\\
q(x,T)=q(x)+\dfrac{1}{\det S(x,T)}\displaystyle\sum_{k\in N(T)}\displaystyle\sum_{p=1}^{2}(-1)^{p-1}\det S_{p}^{(k)}(x,T)h_{k_{p}}(x).\end{array}

The theorem is proved.
∎

For example, when we change just one norming constant (e.g., for
T_{0}
), we get two independent linear equations:

\begin{array}[]{c}(1+s_{00}(x))g_{0_{1}}(x)=-(e^{t_{0}}-1)h_{0_{1}}(x),\\
\\
(1+s_{00}(x))g_{0_{2}}(x)=-(e^{t_{0}}-1)h_{0_{2}}(x).\end{array}

For function
g
, we find

\begin{array}[]{c}g_{0_{1}}(x)=-\dfrac{(e^{t_{0}}-1)h_{0_{1}}(x)}{1+s_{00}(x)},\\
\\
g_{0_{2}}(x)=-\dfrac{(e^{t_{0}}-1)h_{0_{2}}(x)}{1+s_{00}(x)},\end{array}

and for the potentials
p(x,T_{0})
and
q(x,T_{0})
we get the following representation

\begin{array}[]{c}p(x,T_{0})=p(x)+\dfrac{e^{t_{0}}-1}{1+s_{00}(x)}(2h_{0_{1}}(x)h_{0_{2}}(x)),\\
\\
q(x,T_{0})=q(x)+\dfrac{e^{t_{0}}-1}{1+s_{00}(x)}(h_{0_{2}}^{2}(x)-h_{0_{1}}^{2}(x)).\end{array}

Notes and references

The description of isospectral Sturm-Liouville operators was given in 1983 by E.L. Isaacson and E. Trubowitz in paper
[
IT83
]
.

For the Dirac system, such a description was given by T.N. Harutyunyan in 1994 in paper
[
Har94
]
, and then in 2017 in paper
[
AH17b
]
.

CHAPTER
8
Symmetric cases

This section describes cases when the inverse problem for the canonical Dirac system is solved by a smaller set of spectral data than that required to solve the inverse problem in the general case. Some analogs of the well-known Ambartsumyan theorem (in the inverse Sturm-Liouville problem) for the Dirac system case are also obtained.

If we denote by
\lambda_{n}(q,\alpha,\beta)
,
n=0,1,2,\ldots
, eigenvalues of the Sturm-Liouville boundary value problem
L(q,\alpha,\beta)
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-y^{\prime\prime}+q(x)y=\lambda y,\quad x\in(0,\pi),\;q\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi],\;\lambda\in\mathbb{C},\\
y(0)\cos\alpha+y^{\prime}(0)\sin\alpha=0,\quad\alpha\in(0,\pi],\\
y(\pi)\cos\beta+y^{\prime}(\pi)\sin\beta=0,\quad\beta\in[0,\pi),\end{array}\right.

then the well-known theorem of V.A. Ambarzumyan (
[
Amb29
]
see also
[
GL64
,
IMT84
]
) says:

if
{\lambda_{n}\left(q,\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=n^{2}}
,
n=0,1,2,\ldots
, then
q(x)=
0 almost everywhere (a.e.).

One of the questions we want to answer in this section is: ”Is there an analog of Ambarzumyan’s theorem in the case of a boundary value problem for the canonical Dirac system?”, i.e., for the problem
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
(
p,q\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
):

\displaystyle\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right)\frac{d}{dx}+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}p(x)&q(x)\\
q(x)&-p(x)\end{array}\right)\right\}y

\displaystyle=\lambda y,\quad x\in(0,\pi),\;y=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}{y_{1}}\\
{y_{2}}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle y_{1}(0)\cos\alpha+y_{2}(0)\sin\alpha

\displaystyle=0,\quad\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right],

(8.0.7)

\displaystyle y_{1}(\pi)\cos\beta+y_{2}(\pi)\sin\beta

\displaystyle=0,\quad\beta\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right].

(8.0.8)

The eigenvalues of this problem, which we denoted by
\lambda_{n}=\lambda_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, are all simple and form a real sequence (unbounded neither from below nor from above), having asymptotics:

\lambda_{n}(p,q,\ a,\beta)=n+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi}+o(1),\quad\text{with}\;\;n\to\pm\infty\,.

(8.0.9)

Note also that for
p(x)=q(x)\equiv 0
we have the explicit form of eigenvalues:

\lambda_{n}(0,0,\alpha,\beta)=n+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{\pi},\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}.

(8.0.10)

Just say that ”in the general case” the answer to the question about the analog of Ambartsumyan’s theorem is negative, i.e., there are no such
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
, that from the equalities

\lambda_{n}(p,q,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})=n+\frac{\beta_{0}-\alpha_{0}}{\pi},\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

(8.0.11)

it would follow that
p(x)\equiv q(x)\equiv 0
a.e..
More precisely, there are infinite number of ”canonical potentials”, i.e. matrices of the form
\Omega=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}p(x)&q(x)\\
q(x)&-p(x)\end{array}\right)
, for which the set of eigenvalues of the
\lambda_{n}(p,q,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})
coincides with the set (
8.0.11
) (for any
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
).
The set of these ”isospectral” potentials is described in Chapter
7
.
In particular, for example, (
7.1.13
) shows that this ”infinite number” of isospectral (with zero potential) potential-matrices may be not only countable, but also may have continuum cardinality.

At the same time, there is an infinite number of ”particular cases” for which there are analogs of Ambartsumyan’s theorem.
For example, at the end of this chapter, it is proved that:

1) if

\lambda_{n}(0,q,\alpha,0)=n-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\quad\text{for all}\;\;n\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

(8.0.12)

for some
{\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},0\right)\cup\left(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)}
, then
q(x)=
0 almost everywhere.

2) if

\lambda_{n}\left(p,0,\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right)=n+\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\quad\text{for all}\;\;n\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

for some
{\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}
,
{\alpha\neq\frac{\pi}{4}}
, then
p(x)=0
a.e..

Here we speak about an infinite number of ”special cases”, that is, if in the Sturm-Liouville problem the statement takes place only at
{\alpha=\beta=\frac{\pi}{2}}
, then here the statement is true if the condition is fulfilled for
\alpha
from some interval.

On the other hand, these are ”essentially” particular cases, because if in the case of the Sturm-Liouville problem it is stated that the ”potential”
q(x)=0
, then here only part of the ”potential matrix”
\Omega(x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right)p(x)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)q(x)
turns to zero, provided that the other part is assumed to be ”zero” in advance.

Another, more general question that we want to answer in this section can be formulated as follows: ”Are there cases where the inverse problem for the canonical Dirac system can be solved by a smaller set of spectral data, than the set required in the general case?”

A stricter formulation of the question and its answer will be given below.

The set of eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}(p,q,\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)
and the norming constants of a given problem is called spectral data.
As noted above, for the problem
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta)
the following theorem is true, which follows from the theorem
6.2
and
[
Mal98
]
.

Theorem 8.1
.

1.

If
\lambda_{n}(\Omega_{1},\alpha_{1},\beta)=\lambda_{n}(\Omega_{2},\alpha_{2},\beta)
and
a_{n}(\Omega_{1},\alpha_{1},\beta)=a_{n}(\Omega_{2},\alpha_{2},\beta)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
\Omega_{1}(x)=\Omega_{2}(x)
a.e. and
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
.

2.

The potential matrix
\Omega(x)=\sigma_{2}p(x)+\sigma_{3}q(x)
is uniquely and constructively reconstructed by the spectral function of the problem
L(p,q,\alpha,\beta_{0})=L(\Omega,\alpha,\beta_{0})
(i.e. with a fixed
\beta=\beta_{0}
).

Unlike the case of the Sturm-Liouville problem, it follows from the asymptotic formula (
8.0.9
) for the eigenvalues of the problem
L(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)
that if

\lambda_{n}(\Omega_{1},\alpha_{1},\beta)=\lambda_{n}(\Omega_{2},\alpha_{2},\beta)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
even if
\Omega_{1}\neq\Omega_{2}
.

In inverse problems for the Dirac system, the uniqueness theorem by two spectra is known (see Theorem
6.3
, as well as the work
[
Mal98
]
), which is an analog of the Borg theorem for Sturm-Liouville problem.
The formula (see Section
3.6
) is also known, according to which the norming constants are expressed through two spectra.
Let’s formulate these results in the form of the following theorem.

Theorem 8.2
.

1.

(Uniqueness). If
\lambda_{n}(\Omega_{1},\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{n}(\Omega_{2},\alpha,\beta)
and
\lambda_{n}(\Omega_{1},\gamma,\beta)=\lambda_{n}(\Omega_{2},\gamma,\beta)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
0<|\alpha-\gamma|<\pi
, then
\Omega_{1}(x)=\Omega_{2}(x)
almost everywhere.

2.

(Representation of norming constants by two spectra).
For arbitrary
\gamma\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
, such that
0<|\alpha-\gamma|<\pi
, the equality

a_{n}(\alpha,\beta)=\frac{\sin(\gamma-\alpha)}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta)-\lambda_{n}(\gamma,\beta)}\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq n\end{subarray}}\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha,\beta)-\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta)}{\lambda_{k}(\gamma-\beta)-\lambda_{n}(\alpha,\beta)}

(8.0.13)

hold.

Denote
\Omega^{*}(x)=-\Omega(\pi-x)
.
If
\Omega(x)=\Omega^{*}(x)
, then such a potential matrix we will call odd.

Theorem 8.3
.

1.

For all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
the following equalities hold

\displaystyle\lambda_{-n}(\Omega^{*},\alpha,\beta)=-\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\beta,\alpha)\,,

(8.0.14)

\displaystyle a_{-n}(\Omega^{*},\alpha,\beta)=a_{n}(\Omega,\beta,\alpha)\,.

(8.0.15)

2.

(Uniqueness) If for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\lambda_{-n}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha,\beta)=-\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\beta,\alpha)\,,

(8.0.16)

\displaystyle a_{-n}(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha,\beta)=a_{n}(\Omega,\beta,\alpha)\,,

(8.0.17)

then
\tilde{\Omega}(x)=\Omega^{*}(x)=-\Omega(\pi-x)
almost everywhere.

3.

The odd potential
\Omega
is uniquely and constructively reconstructed by the sets
\{\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\alpha),n>0\}
and
\{a(\Omega,\alpha,\alpha),n\geq 0\}
(or
\{\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\alpha),n<0\}
and
\{a(\Omega,\alpha,\alpha),n\leq 0\}
), i.e. by the ”half” of the spectral data.

Proof.

It is easy to check that if
\varphi_{n}(x)
is an eigenfunction of the problem
L(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)
, corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}
, then
\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(x)\equiv\varphi_{n}(\pi-x)
is the eigenfunction of the problem
L(\Omega^{*},\beta,\alpha)
, corresponding to the eigenvalue
-\lambda_{n}
, which we enumerate by
-n
, i.e.
-\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)=\lambda_{-n}(\Omega^{*},\beta,\ a)
.
The problem
L(\Omega^{*},\beta,\alpha)
has no other eigenvalues, which follows from the fact that if
\mu
is an eigenvalue of the problem
L(\Omega^{*},\beta,\alpha)
, then
-\mu
is an eigenvalue of the problem
L(\Omega,\ a,\beta)
, which is proved the same way as above.
Equality
a_{-n}(\Omega^{*},\beta,\alpha)=a_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)
follows from the fact that
L^{2}
-norms of functions
\varphi_{n}(x)
and
\varphi_{n}(\pi-x)
coincide.

To prove the second part of the theorem, note that under the conditions (
8.0.16
), (
8.0.17
) from (
8.0.14
) and (
8.0.15
) it follows that the spectral data of the problems
L(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha,\beta)
and
L(\Omega^{*},\alpha,\beta)
coincide.
According to the theorem
8.1
it follows that
\tilde{\Omega}(x)=\Omega^{*}(x)
a.e.

If the potential is odd, i.e.
\Omega(x)=\Omega^{*}(x)
, then from the first part it follows that
\lambda_{-n}(\Omega,\alpha,\alpha)=-\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\ a)
(in particular,
\lambda_{0}(\Omega,\alpha,\alpha)=0
) and
a_{-n}(\Omega,\alpha,\alpha)=a_{n}(\Omega,\alpha,\alpha)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Thus, if we know the spectral data of the problem
L(\Omega,\alpha,\alpha)
only with positive indices (and also
a_{0}(\Omega,\alpha,\alpha)
), or only with negative ones, we can construct a spectral function by which.
Then, according to the second part of Theorem
8.1
, constructively recover potential of
\Omega(x)
.
Theorem
8.0.8
is proved.
∎

Theorem 8.4
.

1.

For all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
the following equalities hold

\displaystyle\lambda_{-n}(0,q,-\alpha,-\beta)=-\lambda_{n}(0,q,\alpha,\beta)\,,

(8.0.18)

\displaystyle a_{-n}(0,q,-\alpha,-\beta)=a_{n}(0,q,\alpha,\beta)\,.

(8.0.19)

2.

(Uniqueness by one spectrum.)
If

\lambda_{n}(0,q_{1},\alpha,0)=\lambda_{n}(0,q_{2},\alpha,0)\quad\text{ for all}\;\;n\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

(8.0.20)

{0<|\alpha|<\frac{\pi}{2}}
, then
q_{1}(x)=q_{2}(x)
almost everywhere.

3.

(Reconstruction by one spectrum.)
The potential of the problem
L(0,q,\alpha,0)
at
{0<|\alpha|<\frac{\pi}{2}}
is uniquely and constructively recovered by one spectrum
\left\{\lambda_{n}(0,q,\alpha,0),\;n\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}
.
In this case

a_{n}(0,q,\alpha,0)=\\
=-\frac{\sin 2\alpha}{\lambda_{n}(0,q,\alpha,0)-\lambda_{-n}(0,q,\alpha,0)}\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq n\end{subarray}}\frac{\lambda_{n}(0,q,\alpha,0)-\lambda_{k}(0,q,\alpha,0)}{\lambda_{n}(0,q,\alpha,0)+\lambda_{-k}(0,q,\alpha,0)}\;.

(8.0.21)

4.

The potential of the problem
L(0,q,0,0)
is uniquely and constructively recovered by sets:
\left\{\lambda_{n}(0,q,0,0),\;n>0\right\}
and
\left\{a_{n}(0,q,0,0),\;n\geq 0\right\}
or

\left\{\lambda_{n}(0,q,0,0),\;n<0\right\}
and
\left\{a_{n}(0,q,0,0),\;n\leq 0\right\}
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi_{n}
is the eigenfunction of the problem
L(0,q,\alpha,0)
, corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}=\lambda_{n}(0,q,\alpha,0)
.
Then the vector-function
\tilde{\varphi}_{n}=\sigma_{2}\varphi_{n}
satisfies the equation

\displaystyle\ell\tilde{\varphi}_{n}=

\displaystyle\left\{\sigma_{1}\frac{1}{i}\frac{d}{dx}+\sigma_{3}\cdot q(x)\right\}\sigma_{2}\varphi_{n}(x)=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sigma_{2}\left\{\sigma_{1}\frac{1}{i}\frac{d}{dx}+\sigma_{3}\cdot q(x)\right\}\varphi_{n}(x)=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sigma_{2}\ell\varphi_{n}=-\sigma_{2}\lambda_{n}\varphi_{n}=-\lambda_{n}\tilde{\varphi}_{n}

and the boundary conditions
(-\alpha,-\beta)
.
It follows (as in Theorem
8.0.8
) that

\lambda_{-n}(0,q,-\alpha,-\beta)=-\lambda_{n}(0,q,\alpha,\beta),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

The equality
a_{-n}(0,q,-\alpha,-\beta)=a_{n}(0,q,\alpha,\beta)
follows from the fact that
L^{2}
-norms of
\varphi_{n}
and
\sigma_{2}\varphi_{n}
coincide.

To prove the second part, note that according to (
8.0.18
) and (
8.0.20
)

\lambda_{n}(0,q_{1},-\alpha,0)=-\lambda_{-n}(0,q_{1},\alpha,0)=\\
=-\lambda_{-n}(0,q_{2},\alpha,0)=\lambda_{n}(0,q_{2},-\alpha,0),

(8.0.22)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Thus, from the coincidence of the spectra of the problems
L(0,q_{1},\alpha,0)
and
L(0,q_{2},\alpha,0)
follows the coincidence of the spectra of the problems
L(0,q_{1},-\alpha,0)
and
L(0,q_{2},-\alpha,0)
and, (note that from the condition
{0<|\alpha|<\frac{\pi}{2}}
it follows that
\alpha\neq-\alpha
) according to Theorem
8.1
,
q_{1}(x)=q_{2}(x)
a.e..

To prove the third part, it is enough in the formula (
8.0.13
) to take
\gamma=-\alpha
and, taking into account the equality (
8.0.18
), from (
8.0.13
) to obtain the formula (
8.0.21
).
Thus, knowing (one) spectrum of the problem
L(0,q,\alpha,0)
at
{0<|\alpha|<\frac{\pi}{2}}
, we know also the norming constants
a_{n}(0,q,\alpha,0)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, of this problem and, according to the Theorem
8.1
, we can uniquely and constructively recover the potential
\Omega(x)=\sigma_{3}\cdot q(x)
of this problem.

To prove the fourth part, note that the spectrum of the problem
L(0,q,0,0)
is symmetrical with respect to the origin of the coordinates, i.e.

\lambda_{0}(0,q,0,0)=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda_{-n}(0,q,0,0)=-\lambda_{n}(0,q,0,0)\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

In addition, from (
8.0.19
) it follows that
a_{-n}(0,q,0,0)=a_{n}(0,q,0,0)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and, thus, knowing the ”half” spectral data of the problem
L(0,q,0,0)
, we know also the other ”half”, i.e. we know the spectral function, which is enough to constructively recover of the potential function.
∎

Theorem 8.5
.

1.

For all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
the following equalities hold

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}(p,0,\alpha,\beta)=-\lambda_{-n}\left(p,0,\frac{\pi}{2}(sign\,\alpha)-\alpha,\frac{\pi}{2}(sign\,\beta)-\beta\right)\,,

\displaystyle a_{n}(p,0,\alpha,\beta)=a_{-n}\left(p,0,\frac{\pi}{2}(sign\,\alpha)-\alpha,\frac{\pi}{2}(sign\,\beta)-\beta\right)\,.

2.

(Uniqueness by one spectrum.)
If for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}

\lambda_{n}\left(p_{1},0,\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right)=\lambda_{n}\left(p_{2},0,\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right),

(8.0.23)

where
\alpha\neq\frac{\pi}{4}
, then
p_{1}(x)=p_{2}(x)
almost everywhere.

3.

(Reconstruction by one spectrum.)
For
\alpha\neq\frac{\pi}{4}
the potential of the problem
{L\left(p,0,\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right)}
is uniquely and constructively recovered by one spectrum
{\left\{\lambda_{n}\left((p,0,\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right),\;n\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}}
.
At the same time (assume
\sign 0=1
)

a_{n}\left(p,0,\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right)=\\
=({\rm sign}\,\alpha)\frac{\cos 2\alpha}{\lambda_{n}\left(\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right)+\lambda_{-n}\left(\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right)}\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq n\end{subarray}}\frac{\lambda_{n}\left(\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right)-\lambda_{k}\left(\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right)}{\lambda_{n}\left(\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right)+\lambda_{-k}\left(\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right)}\;.

(8.0.24)

4.

The potential of the problem
{L\left(p,0,\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{\pi}{4}\right)}
(or
{L\left(p,0,-\frac{\pi}{4},-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)}
) is uniquely and constructively recovered by sets:

{\left\{\lambda_{n}\left(p,0,\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{\pi}{4}\right),n>0\right\}}\quad
and
\quad{\left\{a_{n}\left(p,0,\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{\pi}{4}\right),n\geq 0\right\}}
or

{\left\{\lambda_{n}\left(p,0,\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{\pi}{4}\right),n<0\right\}}\quad
, and

\quad{\left\{a_{n}\left(p,0,\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{\pi}{4}\right),n\leq 0\right\}}
.

Similarly for
{\left(-\frac{\pi}{4},-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)}
.

Proof.

At first, note that the vector-function
\tilde{\varphi}_{n}=\sigma_{3}\varphi_{n}
satisfies the identity

\displaystyle\ell\tilde{\varphi}_{n}=\left\{\sigma_{1}\frac{1}{i}\frac{d}{dx}+\sigma_{2}\cdot p(x)\right\}\sigma_{3}\varphi_{n}\equiv

\displaystyle-\sigma_{3}\left\{\sigma_{1}\frac{1}{i}\frac{d}{dx}+\sigma_{2}\cdot p(x)\right\}\varphi_{n}\equiv

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-\sigma_{3}\ell\varphi_{n}\equiv-\sigma_{3}\lambda_{n}\varphi_{n}=-\lambda_{n}\tilde{\varphi}_{n},\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}

and the boundary conditions
\left(\frac{\pi}{2}({\rm sign}\,\alpha)-\alpha,\frac{\pi}{2}({\rm sign}\,\beta)-\beta\right)
, i.e.

\lambda_{-n}(p,0,\alpha,\beta)=-\lambda_{-n}\left(p,0,\frac{\pi}{2}(sign\,\alpha)-\alpha,\frac{\pi}{2}(sign\,\beta)-\beta\right),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}.

L^{2}
-norms
\varphi_{n}
and
\sigma_{3}\varphi_{n}
are equal (
{\tilde{\varphi}_{n}}=\varphi_{-n}\left(p,0,\frac{\pi}{2}(sign\,\alpha)-\alpha,\frac{\pi}{2}(sign\,\beta)-\beta\right)
).

To prove the second part, note that (let for simplicity
{\alpha\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}
,
{\alpha\neq\frac{\pi}{4}}
)

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}\left(p_{1},0,\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right)=-\lambda_{-n}\left(p_{1},0,\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right)=

(8.0.25)

\displaystyle=-\lambda_{-n}\left(p_{2},0,\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right)=\lambda_{n}\left(p_{2},0,\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}\;,

and, according to the condition
\alpha\neq\frac{\pi}{4}
,
\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha\neq\alpha
, i.e. we have a coincidence of two spectra, from where, according to Theorem
6.3
, it follows that
p_{1}(x)=p_{2}(x)
almost everywhere.
Thus, in the studied case of the problem
L\left(p,0,\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right)
knowing one spectrum
\left\{\lambda_{n}\left(p,0,\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right),\;n\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}
(at
\alpha\neq\frac{\pi}{4}
) gives information about the second spectrum
\left\{\lambda_{n}\left(p,0,\frac{\pi}{2}(sign\,\alpha)-\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right),\;n\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}
, from where, substituting in the formula (
8.0.13
)
\gamma=\frac{\pi}{2}(sign\,\alpha)-\alpha
, we get the expression (
8.0.24
) for norming constants, i.e. we know the spectral function by which we constructively recover the potential function.

Regarding the fourth part of the theorem
8.4
, it suffices to say that the spectrum
{\left\{\lambda_{n}\left(p,0,\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{\pi}{4}\right),\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}}
is symmetric with respect to the origin.
The rest is the same as in the case of Theorem
8.4
.
∎

Remark 8.1
.

There are several obvious analogs of the theorem
8.0.10
, namely, if the condition (
8.0.23
) is changed to

\lambda_{n}\left(p_{1},0,\frac{\pi}{4},\beta\right)=\lambda_{n}\left(p_{2},0,\frac{\pi}{4},\beta\right),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

\lambda_{n}\left(p_{1},0,-\frac{\pi}{4},\beta\right)=\lambda_{n}\left(p_{2},0,-\frac{\pi}{4},\beta\right),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

\lambda_{n}\left(p_{1},0,\alpha,-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)=\lambda_{n}\left(p_{2},0,\alpha,-\frac{\pi}{4}\right),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

and change part 3 accordingly, then the theorem is correct again.

Similarly, in the theorem
8.4
we can change the condition (
8.0.20
) to

\lambda_{n}(0,q_{1},0,\beta)=\lambda_{n}(0,q_{2},0,\beta),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

{0<|\beta|<\frac{\pi}{2}}
, and change part 3 accordingly.

Finally, let us turn to the analogs of Ambarzumyan’s theorem, which were mentioned in the introduction and are corollaries of Theorems
8.4
and
8.5
.

Corollary 8.1
.

If
{\lambda_{n}(0,q,a,0)=n-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
{0<|\alpha|<\frac{\pi}{2}}
, (or
{\lambda_{n}(0,q,0,\beta)=n+\frac{\beta}{\pi}}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
{0<|\beta|<\frac{\pi}{2}}
), then
q(x)=0
almost everywhere.

Given the equality (
8.0.10
), it is enough to take
q_{2}(x)\equiv 0
in part 2 of Theorem
8.4
.

Corollary 8.2
.

If
{\lambda_{n}\left(p,0,\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}\right)=n-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}+\frac{1}{4}}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
{\alpha\neq\frac{\pi}{4}}
, then
p(x)=0
almost everywhere.

Taking into account the equality (
8.0.10
), it is enough to take
p_{2}(x)\equiv 0
in part 2 of Theorem
8.5
.

From the Remark
8.1
, it follows that there are three other analogs of Ambartsumyan’s theorem for the Dirac system in appropriate special cases.

Notes and references

The results of this chapter are published in 2006 in paper
[
Har06
]
and in 2016 in
[
Har16
]
.

CHAPTER
9
Constructive solution of inverse problem

In Chapter
6
we proved that spectral data
\{\lambda_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
uniquely determine the operator
L(\Omega,\alpha,0)=L(\Omega,\alpha)
.

In this chapter we represent a method for reconstruction of operator
L(\Omega,\alpha)
by two given sequences
\{\lambda_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, which have the properties (
3.1.4
) and (
3.5.4
).
For simplicity, we will consider the case
\alpha=0
.

9.1
The derivation of Gelfand-Levitan equation

As it was shown in Chapter
2
the solution
\varphi(x,\lambda)
of the Cauchy problem (
2.1.13
) can be represented in the form (
2.1.14
), where

\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\lambda x\\
-\cos\lambda x\end{array}\right)

and
K_{0}(x,t)=K(x,t)-K(x,-t)\sigma_{2}
.
For brevity in this section we will write
K(x,t)
instead of
K_{0}(x,t)
.
And so we have

\varphi(x,\lambda)=\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda)+\int^{x}_{0}K(x,t)\varphi_{0}(t,\lambda)\ dt=(E+\mathbb{K})\varphi_{0},

(9.1.1)

where the kernel
K(x,t)
is defined in triangle
0\leq t\leq x\leq\pi
(
K
is upper-diagonal kernel).
Since
\mathbb{K}
is a Volterra integral operator with an upper-diagonal kernel, the operator
E+\mathbb{K}
is invertible, and its inverse can be written in the form
E+\mathbb{H}
, where
\mathbb{H}
is also a Volterra integral operator with the upper-diagonal kernel, i.e.

\mathbb{H}f=\int^{x}_{0}H(x,t)f(t)\ dt,

where
H(x,t)
is defined in triangle
0\leq t\leq x\leq\pi
.
By (
9.1.1
) we get

\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda)=(E+\mathbb{H})\varphi=\varphi(x,\lambda)+\int^{x}_{0}H(x,t)\varphi(t,\lambda)\ dt.

(9.1.2)

For
\varphi_{0}^{T}
we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}^{T}(x,\lambda)=

\displaystyle\varphi^{T}(x,\lambda)+\int^{x}_{0}(H(x,t)\varphi(t,\lambda))^{T}\ dt=

(9.1.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi^{T}(x,\lambda)+\int^{x}_{0}\varphi^{T}(t,\lambda)H(x,t)^{T}\ dt.

Thus from (
9.1.1
) we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi(x,\lambda_{n})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda_{n})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})+

(9.1.4)

\displaystyle+\int^{x}_{0}K(x,s)\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi_{0}(s,\lambda_{n})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})\ ds.

On the other hand, from (
9.1.3
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi(x,\lambda_{n})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi(x,\lambda_{n})\varphi^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})+

(9.1.5)

\displaystyle+\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi(x,\lambda_{n})\int^{t}_{0}\varphi^{T}(s,\lambda_{n})H^{T}(t,s)\ ds.

Using the last two equalities, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\left[\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi(x,\lambda_{n})\varphi^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})-\frac{1}{a^{0}_{n}}\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda_{n}^{0})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n}^{0})\right]=

(9.1.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\left[\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda_{n})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})-\frac{1}{a^{0}_{n}}\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda_{n}^{0})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n}^{0})\right]+

\displaystyle+\int^{x}_{0}K(x,s)\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{n}^{0}}\varphi_{0}(s,\lambda_{n}^{0})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n}^{0})\ ds+

\displaystyle+\int^{x}_{0}K(x,s)\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\left[\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi_{0}(s,\lambda_{n})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})-\frac{1}{a_{n}^{0}}\varphi_{0}(s,\lambda_{n}^{0})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n}^{0})\right]\ ds+

\displaystyle+\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi(x,\lambda_{n})\int^{t}_{0}\varphi^{T}(s,\lambda_{n})H^{T}(t,s)\ ds.

The latter equality we write in the following form

\Phi_{N}(x,t)=I_{N1}(x,t)+I_{N2}(x,t)+I_{N3}(x,t)+I_{N4}(x,t),

where

\displaystyle\Phi_{N}(x,t)=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\left[\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi(x,\lambda_{n})\varphi^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})-\frac{1}{a^{0}_{n}}\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda_{n}^{0})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n}^{0})\right],

(9.1.7)

\displaystyle I_{N1}(x,t)=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\left[\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda_{n})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})-\frac{1}{a^{0}_{n}}\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda_{n}^{0})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n}^{0})\right],

(9.1.8)

\displaystyle I_{N2}(x,t)=

\displaystyle\int^{x}_{0}K(x,s)\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{n}^{0}}\varphi_{0}(s,\lambda_{n}^{0})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n}^{0})\ ds,

(9.1.9)

\displaystyle I_{N3}(x,t)=

\displaystyle\int^{x}_{0}K(x,s)\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\left[\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi_{0}(s,\lambda_{n})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})-\frac{1}{a_{n}^{0}}\varphi_{0}(s,\lambda_{n}^{0})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n}^{0})\right]\ ds,

(9.1.10)

\displaystyle I_{N4}(x,t)=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi(x,\lambda_{n})\int^{t}_{0}\varphi^{T}(s,\lambda_{n})H^{T}(t,s)\ ds.

(9.1.11)

Let
f_{k}\in AC[0,\pi],\ k=1,2
,
f=(f_{1},f_{2})^{T}
, and
f
satisfies the boundary conditions (
3.1.2
) and (
3.1.3
).
Then according to expansion theorem
4.1
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\pi}\Phi_{N}(x,t)f(t)\ dt=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}c_{n}\varphi(x,\lambda_{n})-\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}c_{n}^{0}\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda_{n}^{0})=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}(f,\varphi(x,\lambda_{n}))\varphi(x,\lambda_{n})-\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}(f,\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda_{n}^{0}))\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda_{n}^{0})=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(x)-f(x)=0.

Let us denote by
F(x,t)
the matrix

F(x,t):=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\left[\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda_{n})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})-\frac{1}{\pi}\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda_{n}^{0})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n}^{0})\right],

(9.1.12)

then

\displaystyle F(x,t)=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\left[\frac{1}{a_{n}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\lambda_{n}x\\
-\cos\lambda_{n}x\end{array}\right)(\sin\lambda_{n}t,-\cos\lambda_{n}t)-\frac{1}{\pi}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin\lambda_{n}^{0}x\\
-\cos\lambda_{n}^{0}x\end{array}\right)(\sin\lambda_{n}^{0}t,-\cos\lambda_{n}^{0}t)\right]=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\left[\frac{1}{a_{n}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\sin\lambda_{n}x\sin\lambda_{n}t&-\sin\lambda_{n}x\cos\lambda_{n}t\\
-\cos\lambda_{n}x\sin\lambda_{n}t&\cos\lambda_{n}x\cos\lambda_{n}t\end{array}\right)\right.-

\displaystyle-\left.\frac{1}{\pi}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\sin\lambda_{n}^{0}x\sin\lambda_{n}^{0}t&-\sin\lambda_{n}^{0}x\cos\lambda_{n}^{0}t\\
-\cos\lambda_{n}^{0}x\sin\lambda_{n}^{0}t&\cos\lambda_{n}^{0}x\cos\lambda_{n}^{0}t\end{array}\right)\right].

It follows from (
9.1.8
) and (
9.1.12
) that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi}I_{N1}(x,t)f(t)\ dt=

(9.1.13)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\pi}\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\left[\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda_{n})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})-\frac{1}{a^{0}_{n}}\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda_{n}^{0})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n}^{0})\right]f(t)\ dt=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi}F(x,t)f(t)\ dt.

From (
9.1.9
), it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi}I_{N2}(x,t)f(t)\ dt=

(9.1.14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\pi}\int^{x}_{0}K(x,s)\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{n}^{0}}\varphi_{0}(s,\lambda_{n}^{0})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n}^{0})\ dsf(t)\ dt=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi}\int^{x}_{0}K(x,s)\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{n}^{0}}\varphi_{0}(s,\lambda_{n}^{0})\ ds\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n}^{0})f(t)\ dt=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{x}_{0}K(x,s)\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{a_{n}^{0}}\int_{0}^{\pi}\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n}^{0})f(t)\ dt\varphi_{0}(s,\lambda_{n}^{0})\ ds=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{x}_{0}K(x,t)f(t)\ dt.

From (
9.1.10
) and (
9.1.12
) we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi}I_{N3}(x,t)f(t)\ dt=

(9.1.15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\pi}\int^{x}_{0}K(x,s)\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\left[\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi_{0}(s,\lambda_{n})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})-\frac{1}{a_{n}^{0}}\varphi_{0}(s,\lambda_{n}^{0})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n}^{0})\right]\ dsf(t)\ dt=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi}\int^{x}_{0}K(x,s)\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\left[\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi_{0}(s,\lambda_{n})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})-\frac{1}{a_{n}^{0}}\varphi_{0}(s,\lambda_{n}^{0})\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n}^{0})\right]\ dsf(t)\ dt=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi}\int^{x}_{0}K(x,s)F(s,t)\ dsf(t)\ dt.

From (
9.1.11
), we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi}I_{N4}(x,t)f(t)\ dt=

(9.1.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\pi}\sum_{n=-N}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi(x,\lambda_{n})\int^{t}_{0}\varphi^{T}(s,\lambda_{n})H^{T}(t,s)\ dsf(t)\ dt=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{a_{n}}\varphi(x,\lambda_{n})\int_{0}^{\pi}\varphi^{T}(s,\lambda_{n})\int_{s}^{\pi}H^{T}(t,s)f(t)\ dt\ ds=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{x}^{\pi}H^{T}(t,x)f(t)\ dt

Now let us define
K(x,t)=H(x,t)=0
, for
x<t
.
Thus, combining (
9.1.13
)-(
9.1.16
) together, we obtain

\int_{0}^{\pi}\left[F(x,t)+K(x,t)+\int_{0}^{x}K(x,s)F(s,t)\ ds\right]f(t)\ dt=0,

for arbitrary
f\in AC[0,\pi]
, which satisfy the boundary conditions (
3.1.2
) and (
3.1.3
).
Since the set of such functions is dense in
L^{2}([0,\pi],\mathbb{C}^{2})
, it follows that

F(x,t)+K(x,t)+\int_{0}^{x}K(x,s)F(s,t)\ ds=0,\qquad 0\leq t\leq x\leq\pi.

(9.1.17)

Thus, the following assertion is true:

Theorem 9.1
.

For each fixed
x\in(0,\pi]
, the kernel
K(x,t)
of the transformation operator (
2.1.14
) satisfies the integral equation (
9.1.17
), where
F(x,t)
is defined by (
9.1.12
).

We will call the equation (
9.1.17
) the Gelfand-Levitan integral equation, by an analog of the similar equation in Sturm-Liouville theory.

9.2
Uniqueness of the solution of the Gelfand-Levitan equation

To prove that the Gelfand-Levitan equation (
9.1.17
) has a unique solution, it is sufficient to prove that for arbitrary
x\in(0,\pi]
the corresponding homogeneous equation

K(x,t)+\int_{0}^{x}K(x,s)F(s,t)\ ds=0,

(9.2.1)

has only trivial solution in
L^{2}(0,\pi)
.
Since here
x
is a parameter, it is the same to prove that the equation

g(t)+\int_{0}^{x}g(s)F(s,t)\ ds=0,\qquad 0\leq x\leq\pi,

(9.2.2)

has only trivial solution
g(t)\equiv 0
.
We prove it by contradiction: assume there exists a solution
g(t)\not\equiv 0
from
L^{2}(0,\pi)
.
Multiply both sides of (
9.2.2
) by
g(t)
and integrate w.r.t.
t
from
0
to
x

\int_{0}^{x}|g(t)|^{2}\ dt+\int_{0}^{x}\int_{0}^{x}g(t)g(s)F(s,t)\ dt\ ds=0,\qquad 0\leq x\leq\pi.

(9.2.3)

From the definition of
F(x,t)
(see (
9.1.12
)) is follows that

\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{a_{n}}\left(\int_{0}^{x}\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})g(t)\ dt\right)^{2}=0.

Since all
a_{n}>0
, then

(g,\varphi_{0}(\cdot,\lambda_{n}))=\int_{0}^{x}\varphi_{0}^{T}(t,\lambda_{n})g(t)\ dt=0,\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z}.

(9.2.4)

If we prove the completeness of the system of vector-functions
\{\varphi_{0}(t,\lambda_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, then from (
9.2.4
) will follow that
g(t)=0
, almost everywhere, which is a contradiction.
Therefore, the equation (
9.1.17
) has a unique solution
K(x,t)
, such that
K(x,\cdot)\in L^{2}((0,x);M^{2})
.

The question of completeness of system
\{\varphi_{0}(t,\lambda_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
were considered in
[
GD75
]
and
[
AHM05
]
and some other papers.
In
[
AHM05
]
there was proved that the system
\{\varphi_{0}(t,\lambda_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a Riesz’ basis (unfortunately with many references to other works), which is more than completeness.

Here we mention only the algorithm of reconstruction of the operator
L(p,q,0,0)
(with
p,q\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
) by sequences
\{\lambda_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, which have properties (
3.1.4
) and (
3.5.4
), respectively.

Step 1.

We construct the matrix-function
F(x,t)
by formula (
9.1.12
).

Step 2.

We consider the integral equation (
9.1.17
) and prove that it has a unique solution
K(x,t)
, such that
K(x,\cdot)\in L^{2}((0,x);M^{2})
.

Step 3.

We construct the matrix
\Omega(x)
by formula (
2.1.11
)

\Omega(x)=K_{A}(x,x)B-BK_{A}(x,x),

and consider the equation (
2.1.1
)

By^{\prime}+\Omega(x)y=\lambda y.

We prove that the function (
2.1.14
)

\varphi(x,\lambda)=\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda)+\int^{x}_{0}K(x,t)\varphi_{0}(t,\lambda)\,dt\;,

satisfies the to equation (
2.1.1
) with initial condition

\varphi(0,\lambda)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
-1\end{array}\right)

and boundary condition
\varphi_{1}(\pi,\lambda_{n})=0
(
\beta=0
), for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Step 4.

We prove that

\left(\varphi(\cdot,\lambda_{n}),\varphi(\cdot,\lambda_{m})\right)=\begin{cases}a_{n},&\text{ if }m=n,\\
0,&\text{ if }m\neq n.\end{cases}

Notes and references

In the case of smooth coefficients,
p
and
q
, the constructive solution of the inverse problem was considered in some articles and two books
[
LS88
]
, and
[
Sar05
]
.

The case
p,q\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}[0,\pi]
were considered in articles
[
GD75
,
AHM05
,
LM21
]
and some others.
The Riesz basis property of the root vector-system for some Dirac type operators was considered in
[
LM21
]
(there were also a rich set of references).

Deep results on the completeness and Riesz basis property of the system of root functions of Dirac operators (and more general operators) were obtained in the works of A.A. Shkalikov, M.A. Savchuk, B.S. Mityagin and P.B. Djakov (see, e.g.,
[
SS14
,
DM20
,
Shk21
]
).

CHAPTER
10
Singular Dirac operators

10.1
Asymptotics of the Weyl-Titchmarsh function

Consider the canonical system of Dirac differential equations on the semi-axis

\ell y=\left\{\sigma_{1}\frac{1}{i}\frac{d}{dr}+\sigma_{2}p(r)+\sigma_{3}q(r)\right\}\,y=\lambda y,\quad y=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{array}\right)\,,

(10.1.1)

where
p
and
q
are real, locally absolutely integrable functions on the semi-axis.
Boundary-value problem

\ell y=\lambda y,\quad y_{1}(0)\cos\alpha+y_{2}(0)\sin\alpha=0\,,\quad\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]\,.

(10.1.2)

let’s denote
L(p,q,\alpha)
or
L(\Omega,\alpha)
.
As in previously, here
\Omega(r)=\sigma_{2}p(r)+\sigma_{3}q(r)
.
Through
L(\Omega,\alpha)
we will also denote the self-adjoint extension (see
[
LS88
]
) of the operator generated by the differential expression
\ell
on the set of smooth, finite (from the right) vector-functions satisfying the boundary condition of (
10.1.2
).
It is also known
[
LS88
]
that there is a unique solution up to multiplication by the constant
c(\lambda)
, at
u(r,\lambda)
of the system (
10.1.1
), belonging to
L^{2}(0;\infty;\mathbb{C}^{2})
at
\im\lambda\neq 0
.

Denote by
m(\lambda)
the function

m(\lambda)=\frac{u_{1}(0,\lambda)\cos\alpha+u_{2}(0,\lambda)\sin\alpha}{u_{1}(0,\lambda)\cos\beta+u_{2}(0,\lambda)\sin\beta}=\frac{m_{0}(\lambda)\cos\alpha+\sin\alpha}{m_{0}(\lambda)\cos\beta+\sin\beta}\,,

(10.1.3)

where

m_{0}(\lambda)=\frac{u_{1}(0,\lambda)}{u_{2}(0,\lambda)}\,.

Since
u(r,\lambda)
is unique up to multiplication by a constant, the formula (
10.1.3
)
m(\lambda)
is defined uniquely.

The statement about the existence of a solution from
L^{2}
at
\im\lambda\neq 0
is usually called the H. Weyl theorem, since it first appeared in the classical work of H. Weyl
[
Wey10
]
(devoted to the Sturm-Liouville equation), where the function
m(\lambda)
was introduced for the first time.
For the Dirac system, the function
m(\lambda)
was defined for the first time in the work of Titchmarsh
[
Tit61
]
.
The study of the analytical and asymptotic properties of the function
m(\lambda)
, which is usually called the Weyl-Titchmarsch function, plays an important role in the spectral analysis of the corresponding operators (see Weyl-Titchmarsh function)
[
Har82
,
EHS83
,
Har89
,
Har85b
,
Har85a
,
EB80
,
HS81a
,
HS81b
,
HS82
,
Lev82
]
).

Here we prove the following statement.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let the real coefficients
p
and
q\in L^{1}_{loc}[0,\infty)
.
Then there is an asymptotic formula

\lim_{\mu\to\pm\infty}m_{0}(\nu+i\mu)=\pm i

(10.1.4)

uniformly with respect to all real
\nu
, and as a consequence of the definition (
10.1.3
),

\lim_{\mu\to\pm\infty}m(\nu+i\mu)=e^{\pm i(\beta-\alpha)}.

(10.1.5)

Proof of theorem
10.1
.

.
Let
\lambda=\nu+i\mu
and let
u(r,\lambda)
is a solution to the system (
10.1.1
), belonging to
L^{2}(0,\infty;\mathbb{C}^{2})
at
\im\lambda\neq 0
.
Write the system (
10.1.1
) in coordinate-wise:

\displaystyle u^{\prime}_{2}+p(r)u_{1}+q(r)u_{2}=\nu u_{1}+i\mu u_{1}\,,

(10.1.6)

\displaystyle-u^{\prime}_{1}+q(r)u_{1}-p(r)u_{2}=\nu u_{2}+i\mu u_{2}\,,

(10.1.7)

and write down also the conjugate identities:

\displaystyle\bar{u}^{\prime}_{2}+p(r)\bar{u}_{1}+q(r)\bar{u}_{2}=\nu\bar{u}_{1}-i\mu\bar{u}_{1}\,,

(10.1.8)

\displaystyle-\bar{u}^{\prime}_{1}+q(r)\bar{u}_{1}-p(r)\bar{u}_{2}=\nu\bar{u}_{2}-i\mu\bar{u}_{2}\,.

(10.1.9)

Multiplying (
10.1.6
) by
\bar{u}_{2}
, (
10.1.7
) by
-\bar{u}_{1}
, (
10.1.8
) by
{u}_{2}
, (
10.1.9
) by
-{u}_{1}
and adding all four equations, we get:

-\frac{d}{dr}|u|^{2}=4p(r)\re\left(u_{1}\cdot\bar{u}_{2}\right)+2q(r)\left(|u_{2}|^{2}-|u_{1}|^{2}\right)+4\mu{\im\left(u_{1}\cdot\bar{u}_{2}\right)}.

(10.1.10)

From here, taking into consideration the inequalities

\left|2\re\left(u_{1}\cdot\bar{u}_{2}\right)\right|\leq|u|^{2},\quad\left|2\im\left(u_{1}\cdot\bar{u}_{2}\right)\right|\leq|u|^{2},\quad\left||u_{2}|^{2}-|u_{1}|^{2}\right|\leq|u|^{2},

we get

-\frac{\frac{d}{dr}\,|u|^{2}}{|u|^{2}}=-\frac{d}{dr}\,\ln|u|^{2}\leq 2\left(|p(r)|+|q(r)|+|\mu|\right)\,.

By integrating the last inequality from zero to
r
and exponating, we get

\frac{\left|u(r,\lambda)\right|^{2}}{\left|u(0,\lambda)\right|^{2}}\geq e^{-2\int^{r}_{0}\left(|p(s)|+|q(s)|+|\mu|\right)\,ds}.

(10.1.11)

Define the function
N(\lambda)=\frac{\int^{\infty}_{0}\left|u(r,\lambda)\right|^{2}dr}{\left|u(0,\lambda)\right|^{2}}
.
By integrating the inequality (
10.1.11
) from
0
to
\infty
, we have

\displaystyle N(\lambda)\geq\int^{\infty}_{0}\exp\left\{-2\int^{r}_{0}\left(|p(s)|+|q(s)|+|\mu|\right)\,ds\right\}\,dr>

\displaystyle\quad>\int^{\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}}_{0}\exp\left\{-2\int^{r}_{0}\left(|p(s)|+|q(s)|\right)\,ds\cdot\exp\left(-2|\mu|\,r\right)\right\}\,dr\geq

\displaystyle\quad\geq\int^{\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}}_{0}\exp\left\{-2\int^{\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}}_{0}\left(|p(s)|+|q(s)|\right)\,ds\right\}\,\exp\left(-2|\mu|\,r\right)\,dr=

\displaystyle\quad=\exp\left\{-2\int^{\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}}_{0}\left(|p(s)|+|q(s)|\right)\,ds\right\}\cdot\,\frac{1-e^{-2|\mu|^{\frac{1}{2}}}}{2|\mu|}\,.

Due to the local absolute integrability of
p
and
q
, from the latter inequality follows the limit ratio:

\lim_{|\mu|\to\infty}|\mu|\cdot N(\nu+i\mu)\geq\frac{1}{2}\,.

(10.1.12)

On the other hand, multiplying (
10.1.6
) by
\bar{u}_{1}
, (
10.1.7
) by
\bar{u}_{2}
, (
10.1.8
) by
{u}_{1}
, (
10.1.9
) by
{u}_{2}
, we get (
\lambda=\nu+i\mu
):

\displaystyle u^{\prime}_{2}\cdot\bar{u}_{1}+p(r)|u_{1}|^{2}+q(r)u_{2}\cdot\bar{u}_{1}=\lambda|u_{1}|^{2},

(10.1.13)

\displaystyle-u^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\bar{u}_{2}+q(r)u_{1}\cdot\bar{u}_{2}-p(r)|u_{2}|^{2}=\lambda|u_{2}|^{2},

(10.1.14)

\displaystyle\bar{u}^{\prime}_{2}\cdot u_{1}+p(r)|u_{1}|^{2}+q(r)\bar{u}_{2}\cdot u_{1}=\bar{\lambda}|u_{1}|^{2},

(10.1.15)

\displaystyle-\bar{u}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot u_{2}+q(r)\bar{u}_{1}\cdot u_{2}-p(r)|u_{2}|^{2}=\bar{\lambda}|u_{2}|^{2}.

(10.1.16)

Adding the first and third equations and subtracting the second and fourth, we get:

\frac{d}{dr}\left(u_{1}\bar{u}_{2}+\bar{u}_{1}u_{2}\right)+2p(r)|u|^{2}=2\nu\left(|u_{1}|^{2}-|u_{2}|^{2}\right)\,.

(10.1.17)

Since
u_{1}
and
u_{2}\in L^{2}(0,\infty)
, there are sequences
\{r_{n}\}^{\infty}_{1}
and
\{r^{\prime}_{n}\}^{\infty}_{1}
, tending to infinity, such that
u_{1}(r_{n},\lambda)\to 0
and
u_{2}(r^{\prime}_{n},\lambda)\to 0
at
n\to\infty
(you can actually take
r_{n}=r^{\prime}_{n}
).
Therefore, integrating the equality (
10.1.17
) from
0
to
r_{n}
(or to
r^{\prime}_{n}
), and passing
n\to\infty
, we get the equality

\displaystyle-u_{1}(0,\lambda)\bar{u}_{2}(0,\lambda)-\bar{u}_{1}(0,\lambda)u_{2}(0,\lambda)+2\int^{\infty}_{0}p(r)|u(r,\lambda)|^{2}dr=

(10.1.18)

\displaystyle=2\nu\int^{\infty}_{0}\left(|u_{1}|^{2}-|u_{2}|^{2}\right)\,dr\,.

Now adding the equations (
10.1.13
) and (
10.1.14
) and subtracting (
10.1.15
) and (
10.1.16
), we get:

\frac{d}{dr}\left(u_{2}\bar{u}_{1}-u_{1}\bar{u}_{2}\right)=\left(\lambda-\bar{\lambda}\right)\cdot|u|^{2}=2i\mu\cdot|u|^{2}\,.

(10.1.19)

Again integrating (
10.1.19
) from
0
to
r_{n}
and passing
n\to\infty
, we get:

u_{1}(0,\lambda)\bar{u}_{2}(0,\lambda)-u_{2}(0,\lambda)\bar{u}_{1}(0,\lambda)=2i\mu\int^{\infty}_{0}|u(r,\lambda)|^{2}dr\,.

(10.1.20)

By taking (
10.1.20
) and the equality (
10.1.18
), we get:

\displaystyle 2u_{1}(0,\lambda)\bar{u}_{2}(0,\lambda)=2\int^{\infty}_{0}p(r)|u(r,\lambda)|^{2}dr+2\nu\int^{\infty}_{0}\left(|u_{1}|^{2}-|u_{2}|^{2}\right)\,dr+

(10.1.21)

\displaystyle+2i\mu\int^{\infty}_{0}|u(r,\lambda)|^{2}dr\,.

Dividing both sides of the last equality by
2\cdot|u(0,\lambda)|^{2}
and noting that
\frac{u_{1}(0,\lambda)\cdot\overline{u_{2}(0,\lambda)}}{|u(0,\lambda)|^{2}}=\frac{\frac{u_{1}(0,\lambda)}{u_{2}(0,\lambda)}}{1+\frac{|u_{1}(0,\lambda)|^{2}}{|u_{2}(0,\lambda)|^{2}}}=\frac{m_{0}(\lambda)}{1+|m_{0}(\lambda)|^{2}}\,
, rewrite the equality (
10.1.21
) as

m_{0}(\lambda)=\left(1+\left|m_{0}(\lambda)\right|^{2}\right)\left\{\int^{\infty}_{0}p(r)\,\frac{\left|u(r,\lambda)\right|^{2}}{\left|u(0,\lambda)\right|^{2}}\,dr+\nu\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{\left|u_{2}\right|^{2}-\left|u_{1}\right|^{2}}{\left|u(0,\lambda)\right|^{2}}\,dr+i\mu\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{\left|u(r,\lambda)\right|^{2}}{\left|u(0,\lambda)\right|^{2}}\,dr\right\}.

Rewrite this equality in the following form

m_{0}(\lambda)=\left(1+\left|m_{0}(\lambda)\right|^{2}\right)\left\{c(\lambda)+\nu b(\lambda)+i\mu N(\lambda)\right\}\,,

(10.1.22)

where by
c(\lambda)
and
b(\lambda)
we denote the corresponding integrals, which are obviously real, and taking the absolute values, we get the inequalities

\frac{1}{2}\geq\frac{\left|m_{0}(\lambda)\right|}{1+\left|m_{0}(\lambda)\right|^{2}}=\left\{\left|c(\lambda)+\nu b(\lambda)\right|^{2}+\mu^{2}N^{2}(\lambda)\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}>|\mu|\cdot N(\lambda)\,.

Passing to the limit at
|\mu|\to\infty
and taking into account (
10.1.12
), we get

\frac{1}{2}\geq\lim_{|\mu|\to\infty}\frac{\left|m_{0}(\lambda)\right|}{1+\left|m_{0}(\lambda)\right|^{2}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\,,

which is possible only when

\lim_{|\mu|\to\infty}\left|m_{0}(\nu+i\mu)\right|=1\,.

(10.1.23)

On the other hand, (
10.1.22
) follows the identity

\im m_{0}(\lambda)=\left(1+\left|m_{0}(\lambda)\right|^{2}\right)\cdot\mu N(\lambda)

(10.1.24)

from which, taking into account (
10.1.12
) and (
10.1.23
), follows

1\geq\lim_{|\mu|\to\infty}\left|\im m_{0}(\lambda)\right|=\lim_{|\mu|\to\infty}\left(1+\left|m_{0}(\lambda)\right|^{2}\right)\cdot|\mu|\cdot N(\lambda)\geq 1

i.e.

\lim_{|\mu|\to\infty}\left|\im\,m_{0}(v+i\mu)\right|=1\,.

The latter, together with (
10.1.23
), gives the equality
\lim_{|\mu|\to\infty}\re m_{0}(\nu+i\mu)=0
.
Thus,
\lim_{|\mu|\to\infty}m_{0}(\nu+i\mu)=\pm i
and the sign on the right hand side is defined from the identity (
10.1.24
), i.e. finally have

\lim_{\mu\to\pm\infty}m_{0}(\nu+i\mu)=\pm i

and the theorem
10.1
is proven.
∎

10.2
Representation of norming constants by two spectra

Suppose that the real coefficients
p
and
q
, except for the local integrability condition, satisfy the conditions that ensure the pure discreteness of the spectrum of the boundary value problem
L(p,q,\alpha)
(
10.1.2
). We will continue to call this the condition
(D)
.
For example, the condition (see
[
Mar68
]
)

\lim_{r\to\infty}\left[p^{2}(r)+q^{2}(r)-\sqrt{\left[p^{\prime}(r)\right]^{2}+\left[q^{\prime}(r)\right]^{2}}\right]=\infty,\qquad^{\prime}=\frac{d}{dr}\,,

provides a pure discrete spectrum.
Note that if at some
\alpha_{0}
the spectrum of the operator
L(\Omega,\alpha_{0})
is purely discrete, then the spectra of the operators
L(\Omega,\alpha)
are purely discrete for all real
\alpha
.
If the problem is self-adjoint and the spectrum is purely discrete, then all eigenvalues (besides that they are real) are simple (this can be proved similarly as in Chapter
3
).

The eigenvalues of the problem (
10.1.2
) we will denote by
\lambda_{n}(p,q,\alpha)
,
\,n\in\mathbb{Z}
, (often we will also use the short designations
\lambda_{n}(p,q,\alpha)=\lambda_{n}(\Omega,\alpha)=\lambda_{n}(\alpha)
) and enumerating them in ascending order:

\ldots<\lambda_{-n}(\alpha)<\lambda_{-n+1}(\alpha)<\ldots<\lambda_{0}(\alpha)\leq 0<\lambda_{1}(\alpha)<\ldots<\lambda_{n}(\alpha)<\ldots\;.

(10.2.1)

Denote by
\Lambda(\alpha)=\left\{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)\right\}^{\infty}_{n=-\infty}
the set of eigenvalues (spectrum) of the problem.
Through
\varphi(r,\lambda)
denote the solution of the Cauchy problem

\ell\varphi=\lambda\varphi,\quad\varphi_{1}(0,\lambda)=\sin\alpha,\quad\varphi_{2}(0,\lambda)=-\cos\alpha\,.

(10.2.2)

It is obvious that
\varphi(r,\lambda_{n}(\alpha))
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
are eigenfunctions of the problem (
10.1.2
).

The squares of the
L^{2}
-norms of these eigenfunctions,
a_{n}(\alpha)=\|\varphi(\cdot,\lambda_{n}(\alpha))\|^{2}
, usually are called norming constants.
The left continuous, monotone increasing, step function

\rho_{\alpha}(\lambda)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum_{0\leq\lambda_{n}(\alpha)<\lambda}a^{-1}_{n}(\alpha),&\lambda>0,\\
-\sum_{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)<\lambda\leq 0}a^{-1}_{n}(\alpha),&\lambda<0,\end{array}\right.

is called the spectral function of the problem (
10.1.2
) (or of the operator
L(\Omega,\alpha)
).

The inverse problem by two spectra is to determine the potential function
\Omega(r)
by the spectrum
\lambda(\alpha)
of the problem
L(\Omega,\alpha)
and by the spectrum
\Lambda(\beta)=\left\{\lambda_{n}(\beta)\right\}^{\infty}_{n=-\infty}
of the problem
L(\Omega,\beta)
different from (
10.1.2
) only by the boundary condition.
In the work
[
GL66
]
, it is proved that by the spectral function
\rho_{0}(\lambda)
it is possible to uniquely and constructively recover the potential function
\Omega(r)
.
From
[
GL66
]
it also follows that
\Omega(r)
can be recovered by
\rho_{\alpha}(\lambda)
(at
\alpha\neq 0
) if the parameter
\alpha
from the boundary condition is known in advance.
Therefore, the inverse problem by two spectra can be considered as a problem for determining the norming constants
a_{n}(\alpha)
by the spectra
\Lambda(\alpha)
and
\Lambda(\beta)
.

Theorem 10.2
.

Let
p,q\in L^{1}_{loc}[0,\infty)
and satisfy the condition
(D)
.
Then the norming constants
a_{n}(\alpha)
are determined by the formulas:

a_{n}(\alpha)=c\,\frac{\sin(\beta-\alpha)}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-\lambda_{n}(\beta)}\,\cdot\,\frac{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{0}(\beta)-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\beta)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,p_{k}\,,\quad n\neq 0,

(10.2.3)

where
\beta
is arbitrary number from
\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
,
\beta\neq\alpha
,

p_{k}=\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{k}(\beta)-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}\quad\text{ for }\;k\neq n,\quad\text{ and }\;p_{n}=1,

a_{0}(\alpha)=c\,\frac{\sin(\beta-\alpha)}{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)-\lambda_{0}(\beta)}\,\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\beta)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda_{0}(\alpha))}{\lambda_{k}(\beta)-\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}\,,

(10.2.4)

and the positive constant
c
is being determined from the relation

c\cdot\lim_{\mu\to\infty}\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\beta)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\,\left|\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{k}(\beta)-i\mu}\right|=1\,,

(10.2.5)

i.e.
\rho_{\alpha}(\lambda)
, and therefore the potential function
\Omega(r)
, are defined uniquely by two spectra.

In the next Section
10.3
an example (i.e., clearly written out potential) of the canonical Dirac operator will be constructed, the spectrum of which coincides with the spectrum in advance given canonical Dirac operator, but a finite number of the norming constants differ.
This example, in particular, shows that the Dirac operator is not defined uniquely (generally speaking) by a single spectrum.
However, if the coefficient
p(r)\equiv 0
, then it turns out that
\lambda_{n}(-\alpha)=-\lambda_{-n}(\alpha)
, i.e. knowing the spectrum
\Lambda(\alpha)
, we automatically know the second spectrum
\Lambda(-\alpha)
(if
\alpha\neq 0
), and taking in the formulas (
10.2.3
)-(
10.2.5
)
\beta=-\alpha
(
\alpha\neq 0
), we can determine the norming constants on one spectrum.
Similarly, it is proved that when
q(r)\equiv 0
, if
0\leq\alpha\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
,
\lambda_{n}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha\right)=-\lambda_{-n}(\alpha)
and taking
\beta=\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha
(
\alpha\neq\frac{\pi}{4}
), we solve the problem by one spectrum

\left\{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)\right\}^{\infty}_{-\infty}
, if
-\frac{\pi}{2}<\alpha<0
,
\alpha\neq-\frac{\pi}{4}
, then
\lambda_{n}\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha\right)=-\lambda_{-n}(\alpha)
and taking
\beta=-\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha
, we again find the norming constants by one spectrum
\left\{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)\right\}^{\infty}_{-\infty}
, (
\alpha\neq-\frac{\pi}{4}
).

Theorem 10.3
.

1.

Let
p(r)\equiv 0
, and
q\in L^{1}_{loc}(0,\infty)
and satisfies the condition
(D)
.
Then the norming constants are determined by the formulas
(\alpha\neq 0
,
\alpha\neq\frac{\pi}{2})

a_{n}(\alpha)=c\,\frac{\sin 2\alpha}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)+\lambda_{-n}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\,\frac{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)+\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{-\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,p_{k},\quad n\neq 0,

where

p_{k}=\frac{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)+\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}\quad\text{ for }\;k\neq n,\quad p_{n}=1,

a_{0}(\alpha)=-c\,\frac{\sin 2\alpha}{2\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}\,\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)+\lambda_{0}(\alpha)},

and the positive constant
c
is being determined from the relation

c\cdot\lim_{\mu\to\infty}\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{-\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\,\left|\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)+i\mu}\right|=1.

2.

Let
q(r)\equiv 0
, and
p\in L^{1}_{loc}[0,\infty)
and satisfies
(D)
.
Then (at
\alpha\neq\pm\frac{\pi}{4}
)

a_{n}(\alpha)=c\,\frac{\cos 2\alpha}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)+\lambda_{-n}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\,\frac{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)+\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{-\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,p_{k},\quad n\neq 0,

where

p_{k}=\frac{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)-\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)+\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}\quad\text{ for }\;k\neq n,\quad p_{n}=1,

a_{0}(\alpha)=c\,\frac{\cos 2\alpha}{2\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}\,\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}{\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)+\lambda_{0}(\alpha)},

and the
c
is the same as in the first part of the theorem.

From the definition (
10.1.3
) of the function
m(\lambda)
it can be seen that
m(\lambda)
is a meromorphic function, and its zeros are the spectrum
\Lambda(\alpha)
of the problem (
10.1.2
), and the poles are the spectrum
\Lambda(\beta)
.
From (
10.1.3
) it is also not difficult to obtain equality (see the proof of equality (
3.6.15
))

\im\,m(\lambda)=\frac{\int^{\infty}_{0}\left|u(r,\lambda)\right|^{2}dr\,\cdot\sin(\beta-\alpha)}{\left|u_{1}(0,\lambda)\cos\beta+u_{2}(0,\lambda)\sin\beta\right|^{2}}\,\cdot\im\lambda\,,

(10.2.6)

from which it follows that
m(\lambda)
is a ”real” meromorphic function (i.e.
\im\,m(\lambda)=0
at
\im\,\lambda=0
), mapping at
\sin(\beta-\alpha)>0
, i.e. at
-\frac{\pi}{2}<\alpha<\beta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
the upper half-plane to the upper half-plane and, therefore, according to the theorem
3.8
:

1.

The zeros
\left\{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)\right\}^{\infty}_{n=-\infty}
and the poles
\left\{\lambda_{n}(\beta)\right\}^{\infty}_{n=-\infty}
of the function
m(\lambda)
are all simple and are alternated with each other, and (according to the enumeration of the eigenvalues specified at the beginning), the zeros lie to the right of the poles, i.e.

\lambda_{n}(\beta)<\lambda_{n}(\alpha)<\lambda_{n+1}(\beta),\quad n=0,\pm 1,\pm 2,\ldots,\lambda_{-1}(\alpha)<0<\lambda_{1}(\beta)\,.

(10.2.7)

2.

There is a representation:

m(\lambda)=c\,\frac{\lambda-\lambda_{0}(\alpha)}{\lambda-\lambda_{0}(\beta)}\,\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\beta)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-\lambda}{\lambda_{k}(\beta)-\lambda}\,,

(10.2.8)

where
c>0
.

Calculating the derivative of
\left.\frac{dm(\lambda)}{d\lambda}\right|_{\lambda=\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}
based on the definition (
10.1.3
) we get

\left.\frac{dm(\lambda)}{d\lambda}\right|_{\lambda=\lambda_{n}(\alpha)}=\frac{a_{n}(\alpha)}{\sin(\beta-\alpha)}\,.

Calculating the same derivative from the representation (
10.2.8
) and equating their values, we get the formulas (
10.2.3
) and (
10.2.4
).
Therefore, the question of determining the norming constants by two spectra is reduced to the definition of a positive constant
c
, participating in the presentation of (
10.2.8
).

Lemma 10.1
.

The constant
c>0
, participating in the representation (
10.2.8
), is defined by two spectra
\left\{\lambda_{n}(\alpha)\right\}^{\infty}_{n=0}
and
\left\{\lambda_{n}(\beta)\right\}^{\infty}_{n=0}
(without
\lambda_{0}(\alpha)
and
\lambda_{0}(\beta)
) from equality

c\cdot\lim_{\mu\to\infty}\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\beta)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\,\left|\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{k}(\beta)-i\mu}\right|=1\,.

In addition, there is equality

\lim_{\mu\to\infty}\sum^{\infty}_{k=-\infty}\arg\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{k}(\beta)-i\mu}=\beta-\alpha\;.

(10.2.9)

Proof.

Write the representation (
10.2.6
) at
\lambda=i\mu
, i.e.

m(i\mu)=c\,\frac{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{0}(\beta)-i\mu}\,\prod^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\beta)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{k}(\beta)-i\mu}\,,

and take the logarithm (the principal value of the logarithm) from both sides:

\displaystyle\ln m(i\mu):=

\displaystyle\ln|m(i\mu)|+i\arg m(i\mu)=

(10.2.10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln\left|c\,\frac{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{0}(\beta)-i\mu}\right|+i\arg\left(c\,\frac{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{0}(\beta)-i\mu}\right)+

\displaystyle+\sum^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\left\{\ln\left|\frac{\lambda_{k}(\beta)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{k}(\beta)-i\mu}\right|+i\arg\left(\frac{\lambda_{k}(\beta)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\,\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{k}(\beta)-i\mu}\right)\right\}\,.

Since
c>0
and
\frac{\lambda_{k}(\beta)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}>0
at
k\neq 0
, these multipliers under the argument sign can be discarded.
Note also
\arg\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{k}(\beta)-i\mu}
is equal to the angle under which the segment of the real axis
\left[\lambda_{k}(\beta),\lambda_{k}(\alpha)\right]
from the point
i\mu
is visible.
Therefore, the sequence
\varphi_{n}(\mu)=\sum^{n}_{k=-n}\arg\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{k}(\beta)-i\mu}
is a positive, monotonic increasing and limited (uniformly with respect to
\mu>0
,
\varphi_{n}(\mu)<\pi
) sequence, i.e. it converges.
Taking all this into account and separating the real and imaginary parts in (
10.2.10
), we get two equations:

\ln\left|m(i\mu)\right|=\ln\left(c\cdot\left|\frac{\lambda_{0}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{0}(\beta)-i\mu}\right|\right)+\sum^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\ln\left(\frac{\lambda_{k}(\beta)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\left|\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{k}(\beta)-i\mu}\right|\right)\,,

(10.2.11)

\arg m(i\mu)=\sum^{\infty}_{-\infty}\arg\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{k}(\beta)-i\mu}\,.

(10.2.12)

Since from the equality (
10.1.5
) follows the equalities

\lim_{\mu\to\infty}\left|m(i\mu)\right|=1,\quad\lim_{\mu\to\infty}\arg m(i\mu)=\beta-\alpha\,,

then (
10.2.11
) can be rewritten as

0=\ln c+\lim_{\mu\to\infty}\sum^{\infty}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=-\infty\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}\,\ln\left(\frac{\lambda_{k}(\beta)}{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)}\,\cdot\left|\frac{\lambda_{k}(\alpha)-i\mu}{\lambda_{k}(\beta)-i\mu}\right|\right)\,,

(10.2.13)

and (
10.2.12
) as (
10.2.9
).
Obviously, (
10.2.13
) can be rewritten as (
10.2.5
).
The Lemma
10.1
is proven.
∎

Thus, the Theorem
10.2
is proved.

Let’s move on to the proof of the theorem
10.3
.
Let
p(r)\equiv 0
and let
\varphi_{n}(\alpha):=\varphi\left(r,\lambda_{n}(\alpha)\right)
is a vector-eigenfunction corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}(\alpha)
and normalized by the condition (
10.2.2
).
Let
v_{-n}:=-\sigma_{2}\varphi_{n}(\alpha)=\left(-\varphi_{n1},\varphi_{n2}\right)^{T}
, where
T
is stands for transposition.
Since the matrices
\sigma_{k}
are anti-commutating, we have

\ell v_{-n}=\left(\sigma_{1}\frac{1}{i}\frac{d}{dx}+\sigma_{3}q(x)\right)v_{-n}=-\ell\sigma_{2}\varphi_{n}=\sigma_{2}\lambda_{n}(\alpha)\varphi_{n}=\lambda_{n}(\alpha)\sigma_{2}\varphi_{n}=-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)v_{-n}\,,

and from the definition of
v_{-n}
it follows that it satisfies the initial condition
v_{-n}(0)=\left(\sin(-\alpha),-\cos(-\alpha)\right)^{T}
and, therefore, the boundary condition
y_{1}(0)\cos(-\alpha)+y_{2}(0)\sin(-\alpha)=0
, i.e.
v_{-n}
is an eigenfunction of the problem
\ell y=y
,
y_{1}(0)\cos(-\alpha)+y_{2}(0)\sin(-\alpha)=0
, corresponding to the eigenvalue
-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)
and normalized by the initial condition mentioned above.
Under the designations adopted above, we have

-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)=\lambda_{-n}(-\alpha),\;v_{-n}(r)=\varphi\left(r,\lambda_{-n}(-\alpha)\right)=\varphi_{-n}(-\alpha),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

Therefore, if you take
-\alpha
as
\beta
, then a simple substitution of
\lambda_{k}(\beta)=-\lambda_{-k}(\alpha)
in (
10.2.3
), (
10.2.4
) and (
10.2.5
) leads to the formulas of the first part of the theorem
10.3
.

Let now
q(r)\equiv 0
and
\alpha\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
.
Let’s prove that in this case
\lambda_{n}(\alpha)=-\lambda_{-n}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha\right)
, i.e.
\lambda_{n}(p,0,\alpha)=-\lambda_{-n}\left(p,0,\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha\right)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\alpha\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
.

If
\varphi(r,\lambda)
is a solution to the Cauchy problem (
10.2.2
), then
\varphi(r,\lambda_{n}(\alpha))=\varphi_{n}(r)
is an eigenfunction corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}(\alpha)
.
Take

v_{-n}(r):=\sigma_{3}\varphi_{n}(r)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\varphi_{n1}\\
\varphi_{n2}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\varphi_{n2}(r)\\
\varphi_{n1}(r)\end{array}\right).

Then

\displaystyle\ell v_{-n}(r)

\displaystyle\equiv\left\{\sigma_{1}\frac{1}{i}\frac{d}{dr}+\sigma_{2}p(r)\right\}\sigma_{3}\varphi_{n}(r)\equiv-\sigma_{3}\left\{\sigma_{1}\frac{1}{i}\frac{d}{dr}+\sigma_{2}p(r)\right\}\varphi_{n}(r)\equiv

\displaystyle\equiv-\sigma_{3}\ell\varphi_{n}(r)\equiv-\sigma_{3}\lambda_{n}(\alpha)\varphi_{n}(r)\equiv-\lambda_{n}(r)v_{-n}(r).

Also,

v_{-n}(0)=\left(\varphi_{n2}(0),\varphi_{n1}(0)\right)^{T}=\left(-\cos\alpha,\sin\alpha\right)^{T}=-\left(\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha\right),-\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha\right)\right)^{T}

and
v_{-n}(r)
satisfies the boundary condition

v_{-n1}(0)\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha\right)+v_{-n2}(0)\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha\right)=0,

and
-\frac{\pi}{2}<\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
.

If
\alpha\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\,,\,0\right)
, then
-\frac{\pi}{2}<-\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha<0
and
v_{-n}(0)=\left(-\cos\alpha,\sin\alpha\right)^{T}
satisfies the boundary condition
v_{-n1}(0)\cos\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha\right)+v_{-n2}(0)\sin\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha\right)=0
, i.e. in this case,
\lambda_{-n}\left(p,0,-\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha\right)=-\lambda_{n}(\alpha)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Theorem
10.3
is proved.

10.3
Changing finite number of spectral data

In this paragraph, we consider the canonical self-adjoint operator
L=L(\Omega,\alpha)
, defined in Section
10.1
, without any restrictions on its spectrum.
In this (general) case, it is known
[
LS88
]
that for the operator
L
there is a unique, non-decreasing function
\rho_{\alpha}(\lambda)
, defined on the real axis, such that for any
f\in L^{2}\left(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)
there is Parseval equality

\|f\|^{2}=\int^{\infty}_{0}\left\{f^{2}_{1}(x)+f^{2}_{2}(x)\right\}\,dx=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}F^{2}_{n}(\lambda)\,d\rho_{\alpha}(\lambda)\,,

where

F_{n}(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{n}\left\{f_{1}(x)\varphi_{1}(x,\lambda)+f_{2}(x)\varphi_{2}(x,\lambda)\right\}\,dx\,,

and
\varphi(x,\lambda)
is the solution to the Cauchy problem (
10.2.2
).

The function
\rho(\lambda)
is called the spectral function of the operator
L
.
If the spectrum of the operator
L
contains eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
, then it is obvious that
\varphi(x,\lambda_{0})
is an eigenfunction.
The square of its
L_{2}
-norms will be denoted the
a^{-1}_{0}
(usually we denote
a_{0}
, but here it is convenient to use inverse values):

\|\varphi(\cdot,\lambda_{0})\|^{2}=\int^{\infty}_{0}\left[\varphi^{2}_{1}(x,\lambda_{0})+\varphi^{2}_{2}(x,\lambda_{0})\right]\,dx=a^{-1}_{0}\,.

The number
a_{0}
is called norming constant.

This paragraph is devoted to obtaining explicit formulas for rebuilding the Dirac operator coefficients when changing a finite number of eigenvalues and/or norming constants.
Roughly speaking, we answer the following question:
”What happens to the potential
\Omega(x)
if a finite number of eigenvalues and/or norming constants is being changed?”.

The spectrum of the operator
L
will be denoted by
\sigma(L)
, and the set of eigenvalues by
\sigma_{d}(L)
.
It is known that
\sigma_{d}(L)
coincides with the set of all points of discontinuity of the spectral function
\rho(\lambda)
.

Consider an arbitrary finite set of
n
of real numbers
\mu_{k}
, not belonging to
\sigma_{d}(L)
, the arbitrary finite set of
n+l
of positive numbers
b_{k}
and the finite set of
m+l
of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}
of the operator
L
(
n,m,l\geq 0
).
By
a_{k}
, we will denote the norming constants corresponding to
\lambda_{k}
.
By
\delta(\lambda)
we will denote the Dirac
\delta
function.
In these notations, the main result of this section is formulated as follows.

Theorem 10.4
.

Let
\rho(\lambda)
be the spectral function of the operator
L
.
Then the function
\tilde{\rho}(\lambda)
, defined by the relation

\displaystyle d\tilde{\rho}(\lambda)=d\rho(\lambda)

\displaystyle+\sum^{n}_{k=1}b_{k}\delta(\lambda-\mu_{k})\,d\lambda-\sum^{m}_{i=1}a_{i}\delta(\lambda-\lambda_{i})\,d\lambda+

(10.3.1)

\displaystyle+\sum^{l}_{p=1}\left(b_{p+n}-a_{p+m}\right)\,\delta\left(\lambda-\lambda_{p+m}\right)\,d\lambda\,,

is also a spectral function.
More precisely, there is a unique self-adjoint canonical Dirac operator
\tilde{L}=L\left(\tilde{\Omega},\alpha\right)
for which
\tilde{\rho}(\lambda)
is a spectral function.
In this case
\tilde{\Omega}(x)
is defined by the expression

\displaystyle\tilde{\Omega}(x)=\Omega(x)+\sum^{n+m+l}_{k=1}\frac{\gamma_{k}}{1+\gamma_{k}g_{k-1}(x,\nu_{k})}

\displaystyle\left[B\varphi_{k-1}(x,\nu_{k})\varphi^{T}_{k-1}(x,\nu_{k})-\right.

(10.3.2)

\displaystyle\left.-\varphi_{k-1}(x,\nu_{k})\varphi^{T}_{k-1}(x,\nu_{k})\,B\right]\,,

where
T
is the transponation sign,

\nu_{k}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mu_{k},&1\leq k\leq n\,,\\
\lambda_{k},&n+1\leq k\leq n+m+l\,,\end{array}\right.\quad\gamma_{k}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}b_{k},&1\leq k\leq n\,,\\
-a_{k-n},&n+1\leq k\leq n+m\,,\\
b_{k-m}-a_{k-n},&n+m+1\leq k\leq n+m+l\,,\end{array}\right.

and vector-functions
\varphi_{k}(x,\lambda)
are determined from recurrent relations

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}(x,\lambda)=

\displaystyle\varphi(x,\lambda),

(10.3.3)

\displaystyle\varphi_{k}(x,\lambda)=

\displaystyle\varphi_{k-1}(x,\lambda)-\frac{\gamma_{k}\cdot\varphi_{k-1}(x,\nu_{k})}{1+\gamma_{k}g_{k-1}(x,\nu_{k})}\int^{\pi}_{0}\varphi^{T}_{k-1}(t,\nu_{k})\,\varphi_{k-1}(t,\lambda)\,dt,

for
k=1,2,\ldots,n+m+l
, where

g_{k-1}(x,\nu_{k})=\int^{x}_{0}\left|\varphi_{k-1}(t,\nu_{k})\right|^{2}dt\,.

Before proceeding to the proof of this theorem, note that similar questions for the Schrödinger operator on the semi-axis were studied in
[
JK53
]
(see also
[
Kre53
,
Kre54
]
).
For the regular Sturm-Liouville operator, these questions were studied in the work of H. Hochstadt
[
Hoc73
]
.
From another point of view, B.M. Levitan
[
Lev78
]
approached the problem of finite-dimensional perturbations of the Sturm-Liouville operator on the finite interval.
For the Sturm-Liouville operator on the finite segment, this question was studied in the works
[
Pan80a
,
Pan80b
,
KH79b
]
